Asymptotic Analysis and Uniqueness of blowup solutions of non-quantized singular mean field equations
Daniele Bartolucci, Wen Yang, Lei Zhang

Abstract
. For singular mean field equations defined on a compact Riemann surface, we prove the uniqueness of bubbling solutions as far as blowup points are either regular points or non-quantized singular sources. In particular the uniqueness result covers the most general case extending or improving all previous works of Bartolucci-Jevnikar-Lee-Yang [ 10 , 11 ] and Wu-Zhang [ 81 ] . For example, unlike previous results, we drop the assumption of singular sources being critical points of a suitably defined Kirchoff-Routh type functional. Our argument is based on refined estimates, robust and flexible enough to be applied to a wide range of problems requiring a delicate blowup analysis. In particular we come up with several new estimates of independent interest about the concentration phenomenon for Liouville-type equations.



1.
Introduction

The main goal of this article is to study the uniqueness property of the following mean field equation with singular sources:

(1.1)

\Delta_{g}\nu+\rho\bigg(\frac{he^{\nu}}{\int_{M}he^{\nu}{\rm d}\mu}-\frac{1}{vol_{g}(M)}\bigg)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}4\pi\alpha_{j}\left(\delta_{q_{j}}-\frac{1}{vol_{g}(M)}\right)\quad{\rm in}\ \;M,

where
(M,g)
is a Riemann surface with metric
g
,
\Delta_{g}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator (
-\Delta_{g}\geq 0
),
h
is a positive
C^{5}
function on
M
,
q_{1},\cdots,q_{N}
are distinct points on
M
,
\rho>0,\alpha_{j}>-1
are constants,
\delta_{q_{j}}
is the Dirac measure at
q_{j}\in M
,
vol_{g}(M)
is the volume of
(M,g)
. Throughout the article we assume
vol_{g}(M)=1
for the sake of simplicity. Equation (
1.1
) arises in many branches of Mathematics and Physics, such as conformal geometry (
[
3
,
36
,
43
,
47
,
74
,
76
]
), Electroweak and Self-Dual Chern-Simons vortices (
[
1
,
67
,
70
,
71
,
82
]
), the statistical mechanics description of turbulent Euler flows, plasmas and self-gravitating systems (
[
9
,
24
,
66
,
75
,
80
]
), Cosmic Strings (
[
21
,
64
]
), the theory of hyperelliptic
curves and modular forms (
[
25
]
) and CMC immersions in hyperbolic 3-manifolds (
[
73
]
). Among many other things which we cannot list here, these were some of the motivations for the huge effort made in the study of (
1.1
), including existence (
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
15
,
18
,
22
,
37
,
26
,
30
,
31
,
33
,
34
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
55
,
55
,
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
]
), uniqueness (
[
8
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
16
,
17
,
27
,
45
,
49
,
52
,
54
,
68
,
81
]
), concentration-compactness and bubbling behavior (
[
19
,
20
,
23
,
41
,
42
,
44
,
48
,
50
,
51
,
69
,
72
,
77
,
78
,
79
,
83
,
84
]
) just to quote a few. In particular, started in

[
32
,
56
]
, a series of groundbreaking results has been recently pushed forward in the case of the flat two torus with singular sources, see
[
35
,
53
]
and references quoted therein.

To write the main equation in an equivalent form, we invoke the standard Green’s function
G_{M}(z,p)
, defined to be the unique (
[
2
]
) solution of:

\begin{cases}-\Delta_{g}G_{M}(x,p)=\delta_{p}-1\quad{\rm in}\ \;M\\
\\
\int_{M}G_{M}(x,p){\rm d}\mu=0.\end{cases}

Then
in local isothermal coordinates
z=z(x)
centered at
p
,
0=z(p)
,

G(z,0)=G_{M}(x(z),p)
can be written as
follows

G(z,0)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|z|+R(z,0),

where
R(z,0)
denotes the regular part in local coordinates. By using
G_{M}(x,p)
we write (
1.1
) as follows,

(1.2)

\Delta_{g}{\rm w}+\rho\bigg(\frac{He^{\rm w}}{\int_{M}He^{\rm w}{\rm d}\mu}-1\bigg)=0\quad{\rm in}\ \;M,

where

(1.3)

{\rm w}(x)=\nu(x)+4\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j}G_{M}(x,q_{j}),

and

H(x)=h(x)\prod_{j=1}^{N}e^{-4\pi\alpha_{j}G_{M}(x,q_{j})}.

Note that in a local coordinates system near
q_{j}
,
0=z(q_{j})
,

H(z)=h_{j}(z)|z|^{2\alpha_{j}},\quad|z|\ll 1,\quad 1\leq j\leq N,

for some
h_{j}(z)>0
.

We say that
\{\nu_{k}\}
is a sequence of bubbling solutions of (
1.1
) if the
L^{\infty}
norm of the corresponding
{\rm w}_{k}
defined by (
1.3
) tends to infinity as
k
goes to infinity. In this situation it is well known (
[
19
,
20
,
50
]
) that
{\rm w}_{k}
blows up at a finite number of points. To say that the set of blowup points is
\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}\}
means that there exist
m
sequences of points
p_{k,1},\cdots,p_{k,m}
such that, possibly along a subsequence,
{\rm w}_{k}(p_{k,j})\to+\infty
and
p_{k,j}\to p_{j}(j=1,\cdots,m)
as
k\to+\infty
. If none of the blowup points is a singular source,
the uniqueness of the bubbling solutions has been proved in
[
10
]
. Therefore we consider here the case where at least one blow up point is a singular source. By assumption the strength of the singular source at
p
is
4\pi\alpha_{p}
, where if
p
is a regular point, the corresponding
\alpha_{p}=0
. Thus we use
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{m}
to denote the strengths at
p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}
, respectively and we let the first
\tau\geq 1
of them be singular blowup points:

(1.4)

\alpha_{i}>-1,\quad\alpha_{i}\not\in\mathbb{N},\quad 1\leq i\leq\tau,\quad\alpha_{\tau+1}=\cdots=\alpha_{m}=0,

where
\mathbb{N}
is the set of natural numbers including
n=0
. We will say that the singular source located at
p
is
non-quantized
if
\alpha_{p}
is not a positive integer. Here and in the rest of this work we assume that all the singular sources, as far as they happen to be blowup points, are non-quantized and let
\alpha_{M}
denote the maximum of
\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m}\}
.

There is a variational structure of equation (
1.2
) which is in fact the Euler-Lagrange equation of the functional:

I_{\rho}({\rm w})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|\nabla{\rm w}|^{2}+\rho\int_{M}{\rm w}-\rho\log\int_{M}He^{\rm w},\quad{\rm w}\in H^{1}(M).

Since adding a constant to any solution of (
1.2
) still yields a solution, there is no loss of generality in defining
I_{\rho}
on the subspace of
H^{1}(M)
functions with vanishing mean. A complete discussion about the variational structure of (
1.2
) can be found in
[
62
]
.

To state the main results we adopt the following notations:

G_{j}^{*}(x)=8\pi(1+\alpha_{j})R_{M}(x,p_{j})+8\pi\sum_{l,l\neq j}(1+\alpha_{l})G_{M}(x,p_{l}),

where
R_{M}(\cdot,p_{j})
is the regular part of
G_{M}(\cdot,p_{j})
,

(1.5)

L(\mathbf{p})=\sum_{j\in I_{1}}[\Delta\log h(p_{j})+\rho_{*}-N^{*}-2K(p_{j})]h(p_{j})^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{M}}}e^{\frac{G_{j}^{*}(p_{j})}{1+\alpha_{M}}},

where
K(\cdot)
denotes the Gaussian curvature of the ambient manifold,

(1.6)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{M}=\max_{i}\alpha_{i},\quad I_{1}=\{i\in\{1,\cdots,m\};\quad\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{M}\},\\
\\
\rho_{*}=8\pi\sum_{j=1}^{m}(1+\alpha_{j}),\quad N^{*}=4\pi\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}.\end{cases}

In other words,
L(\mathbf{p})
takes the sum on those indexes whose
\alpha_{j}
equals the largest among them.
As mentioned above, whenever some blowup points are non-quantized singular sources while some others are regular, we assume without loss of generality that
p_{1},\cdots,p_{\tau}
are singular sources and
p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m}
are regular points. In this case
for
(x_{\tau+1},\cdots,x_{m})\in M\times\cdots\times M
we define

\displaystyle f^{*}(x_{\tau+1},\cdots,x_{m})=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=\tau+1}^{m}\big[\log h(x_{j})+4\pi R_{M}(x_{j},x_{j})\big]

\displaystyle+4\pi\sum_{l\neq j}^{\tau+1,\cdots,m}G_{M}(x_{l},x_{j})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=\tau+1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}8\pi(1+\alpha_{i})G_{M}(x_{j},p_{i}).

It is well known (
[
30
]
) that
(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})
is a critical point of
f^{*}
. In this article we consider all possible cases of combinations of blowup points and split them in two classes:

\mbox{\bf Class One:}\quad\alpha_{M}>0,\quad\mbox{\bf Class Two:}\quad\alpha_{M}\leq 0.

Obviously in
Class One
the set of blowup points contains at least one positive singular source
while in
Class two
either the blowup set is a combination of regular blowup points (
\alpha_{M}=0
) and negative singular sources, or it only contains negative singular sources
(\alpha_{M}<0)
. Our first result concerns
Class One
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\nu_{k}^{(1)}
and
\nu_{k}^{(2)}
be two sequences of bubbling solutions of
(
1.1
)
with the same
\rho_{k}
:
\rho_{k}^{(1)}=\rho_{k}=\rho_{k}^{(2)}
and the same blowup set:
\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}\}
. Suppose
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})
satisfies
(
1.4
)
,
\alpha_{M}>0
,
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
and, as far as
\tau<m
,
\det\big(D^{2}f^{*}(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})\big)\neq 0
. Then there exists
n_{0}>1
such that

\nu_{k}^{(1)}=\nu_{k}^{(2)}
for all
k\geq n_{0}
.

Here
D^{2}f^{*}
denotes for the Hessian tensor field on
M
and we observe that, when evaluating
\det(D^{2}f^{*})
at
p_{j}
for
j=\tau+1,\cdots,m
, we use
\phi_{j}
to denote the conformal factor that satisfies

\Delta_{g}=e^{-\phi_{j}}\Delta,\,\,\phi_{j}(0)=|\nabla\phi_{j}(0)|=0,\,\,e^{-\phi_{j}}\Delta\phi_{j}=-2K,

for
j=\tau+1,\cdots,m
and
h_{j}
is understood as
h_{j}e^{\phi_{j}}
in a small neighborhood of
p_{j}
(
j=\tau+1,\cdots,m
).
Therefore, if
\tau=m
, which means all blowup points are singular sources, then as far as
\alpha_{M}>0
we see that
the unique relevant assumption is

L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
.

To state the result about
Class two
, we first observe that the set of blowup points consists only of regular points and negative sources. We shall introduce new quantities. We use
B(q,r)
to denote the geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at
q\in M
, while
\Omega(q,r)
denotes the pre-image of the Euclidean ball of radius
r
,
B_{r}(q)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, in a suitably defined isothermal coordinate system. If
m-\tau\geq 2
we fix a family of open, mutually disjoint sets
M_{j}
, the closure of their union being the whole
M
. Then we set

D(\mathbf{p})=\lim_{r\to 0}\sum_{j=1}^{m}h(q_{j})e^{G_{j}^{*}(q_{j})}\bigg(\int_{M_{j}\setminus\Omega(q_{j},r_{j})}e^{\Phi_{j}(x,\mathbf{q})}d\mu(x)-\frac{\pi}{1+\alpha_{j}}r_{j}^{-2-2\alpha_{j}}\bigg),

where
M_{1}=M
if
m=1
,

r_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r\bigg(8h(q_{j})e^{G_{j}^{*}(q_{j})}\bigg)^{1/2},\quad\tau<j\leq m,\\
r,\quad 1\leq j\leq\tau,\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{j}(x,\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{m}8\pi(1+\alpha_{l})G(x,q_{l})-G_{j}^{*}(q_{j})

\displaystyle+\log h_{j}(x)-\log h_{j}(q_{j})+4\pi\alpha_{j}(R_{M}(x,q_{j})-G_{M}(x,q_{j})),

where we remark that in local isothermal coordinates centered at
q_{j}
,
0=z(q_{j})
, we have
4\pi\alpha_{j}(R(z,0)-G(z,0))=2\alpha_{j}\log|z|
. It is important to note that in this case, regular blow-up points are critical points of

(1.7)

G_{j}^{*}(x)-G_{j}^{*}(q_{j})+\log h_{j}(x)-\log h_{j}(0).

For the integrability of
\Phi_{j}
we make this assumption on singular blow-up points. Thus for all blow-up points we require that,

(1.8)

\nabla\bigg(G_{j}^{*}(x)+\log h_{j}(x)\bigg)|_{x=q_{j}}=0,\quad 1\leq j\leq m.

Concerning (
1.8
), let us remark that in this part we are just concerned with
Class two
solutions, whence
\alpha_{M}\leq 0
and then, if a singular source exists with strength
\alpha_{j}
, then it satisfies
-1<\alpha_{j}<0
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, except that here
\alpha_{M}\leq 0
and (
1.8
) holds for all blowup points, then in any one of the following cases:

(1)

\alpha_{M}=0
,
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
,
\det\big(D^{2}f^{*}(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})\big)\neq 0

(2)

\alpha_{M}=0
,
L(\mathbf{p})=0
,
D(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
,
\det\big(D^{2}f^{*}(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})\big)\neq 0

(3)

\alpha_{M}<0
,
D(\mathbf{p})\neq 0

the same uniqueness property as in Theorem
1.1
holds true.

Remark 1.1
.

The limit in the definition of
D(\mathbf{p})
exists because
\Omega(q,r)
is the pre-image of a ball, a non-integrable term in the neighborhood of
q
cancels out in the evaluation as
r\to 0
.

Remark 1.2
.

Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
extend or improve previous results
[
10
,
11
,
81
]
significantly in at least three aspects:

(1)

They include all possible blowup points combinations: blowup points can be either positive or negative singular sources or both, together with regular points as well;

(2)

They do not anymore require positive singular blowup points to be critical points of certain Kirchoff-Routh type functionals. In previous work
[
11
,
81
]
, for technical reasons, this assumption was made providing a relevant simplification of the proof. On the one hand, the removal of this hypothesis is crucial for applications and also rather surprising from the physical point of view, as long-lived vortex structures are known to concentrate at critical points of those finite-dimensional functionals (
[
24
]
). On the other side, uniqueness becomes much harder to prove whenever we drop this assumption;

(3)

Let
\alpha_{M}
be the largest index among positive singular sources. It was assumed in
[
81
]
that
0<\alpha_{M}<1
, an hypothesis that we drop here as well. Also,
[
11
]
was concerned with just one positive singular source.

With the removal of restrictions of previous results, the generality of the main theorems is a handy tool suitable for a number of applications. However, as we will shortly discuss below, the proof requires new ideas and several new estimates of independent interest about the concentration phenomenon.

Here we also make a striking comparison about the quantities we use. First it is easy to see that
L(\mathbf{p})
is local in nature because it only depends on information at certain blowup points, while
D(\mathbf{p})
is involved with global integration. Both quantities have played important roles in various contexts. For example,
L(\mathbf{p})
has been critical in Chen-Lin’s degree counting program for Liouville equations
[
30
,
31
]
,
D(\mathbf{p})
plays a crucial role in

[
27
,
32
]
and in
[
14
,
16
]
for regular and singular Liouville equations (see in particular
[
53
]
and references therein) in
[
46
,
58
]
for Liouville systems and in
[
55
]
for Chern-Simon’s equation. Since Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
include all possible combinations of singular and regular blow up points, it is worth to explain the roles of
L(\mathbf{p})
and
D(\mathbf{p})
in an unified manner:
When
\alpha_{M}>0
,
L(\mathbf{p})
plays the leading role. When
\alpha_{M}=0
,
L(\mathbf{p})
is still more important than
D(\mathbf{p})
. But when
L(\mathbf{p})=0
,
D(\mathbf{p})
plays the leading role together with the non-degeneracy assumption on
f^{*}
. The interplay of these two quantities reveals the highly nontrivial nature of the uniqueness problem. Again the case of the flat two torus plays a special role, see
[
57
]
. Let us also remark that around negative singular sources, locally defined quantities play no role, in the sense that from Theorem
1.2
we infer that only
D(\mathbf{p})
matters as far as
\alpha_{M}<0
and
D(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
.

One can also obtain similar conclusions for the corresponding Dirichlet problem.
Let
\Omega
be an open and bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with regular boundary
\partial\Omega\in C^{2}
,
\nu
be a solution of

(1.9)

\begin{cases}\Delta\nu+\rho\frac{he^{\nu}}{\int_{\Omega}he^{\nu}{\rm d}x}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}4\pi\alpha_{j}\delta_{q_{j}}&{\rm in}\;\ \Omega,\\
\\
\nu=0&{\rm on}\;\ \partial\Omega,\end{cases}

where
h>0
is a
C^{1}
function in
\Omega
,
q_{1},\cdots,q_{N}
are distinct points in
\Omega
,
\rho>0
,
\alpha_{j}>-1
are constants. As above we need some local/global quantities suitable to describe the combinations of blowup points. Obviously we keep the analogue definitions adopted above about bubbling solutions of (
1.9
).

Let
G_{\Omega}
be the Green’s function uniquely defined by

\begin{cases}-\Delta G_{\Omega}(x,p)=\delta_{p}&{\rm in}\;\ \Omega,\\
\\
G_{\Omega}(x,p)=0&{\rm on}\;\ \partial\Omega,\end{cases}

R_{\Omega}(x,p)=G_{\Omega}(x,p)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x-p|
be the regular part of
G_{\Omega}(x,p)
and

G_{j,\Omega}^{*}(x)=8\pi(1+\alpha_{j})R_{\Omega}(x,p_{j})+8\pi\sum_{l\neq j}^{1,\cdots,m}(1+\alpha_{l})G_{\Omega}(x,p_{l}),

As above we write,

H(x)=h(x)\prod_{j=1}^{N}e^{-4\pi\alpha_{j}G_{\Omega}(x,q_{j})},

where we remark that,
for
q_{j}
fixed, we have

H(x)=h_{j}(x)|x-q_{j}|^{2\alpha_{j}},\quad x\neq q_{j}

where
h_{j}(x)>0
. Similar to notations for the first part, we still assume (
1.4
) for
(\alpha_{1},..,\alpha_{N})
and keep the same conventions about
I_{1}
and
\alpha_{M}
. Next let us define,

L_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})=\sum_{j\in I_{1}}\Delta\log h(p_{j})h(p_{j})^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{M}}}e^{\frac{G_{j,\Omega}^{*}(p_{j})}{1+\alpha_{M}}},

\displaystyle f_{\Omega}^{*}(x_{\tau+1},\cdots,x_{m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=\tau+1}^{m}\big[\log h(x_{j})+4\pi R(x_{j},x_{j})\big]+4\pi\sum_{l\neq j}^{\tau+1,\cdots,m}G(x_{l},x_{j}),

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=\tau+1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}8\pi(1+\alpha_{i})G(x_{j},p_{i}),

and let
D^{2}f_{\Omega}^{*}
be the Hessian on
\Omega
. Of course, in this case
(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})
is a critical point of
f_{\Omega}^{*}
(
[
59
]
).
Concerning
Class one
we have,

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\nu_{k}^{(1)}
and
\nu_{k}^{(2)}
be two sequences of bubbling solutions of
(
1.9
)
with
\rho_{k}^{(1)}=\rho_{k}=\rho_{k}^{(1)}
,
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{N})
satisfying
(
1.4
)
,
\alpha_{M}>0
and
L_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
. If
\tau<m
we assume

\det\big(D^{2}f^{*}(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})\big)\neq 0
.
Then there exists
n_{0}>1
such that

\nu_{k}^{(1)}=\nu_{k}^{(2)}
for all
k\geq n_{0}
.

Concerning
Class two
we set

\displaystyle\Phi_{j,\Omega}(\mathbf{q}):=~

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{m}8\pi(1+\alpha_{l})G_{\Omega}(x,q_{l})-G_{j,\Omega}^{*}(q_{j})+\log h_{j}(x)-\log h_{j}(q_{j})

\displaystyle+2\alpha_{j}\log|x-q_{j}|

and

D_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})=\lim_{r\to 0}\sum_{j=1}^{m}h(q_{j})e^{G_{j,\Omega}^{*}(q_{j})}\bigg(\int_{\Omega_{j}\setminus B_{r_{j}}(p_{j})}e^{\Phi_{j,\Omega}(\mathbf{q})}dx-\frac{\pi}{1+\alpha_{j}}r_{j}^{-2\alpha_{j}-2}\bigg),

where
\displaystyle{r_{j}=r\left(\frac{h(p_{j})e^{G_{j,\Omega}^{*}(p_{j})}}{8(1+\alpha_{j})^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\alpha+2}}}
,
\Omega_{1}=\Omega
if
1=m
, otherwise we have
\Omega_{l}\cap\Omega_{s}=\emptyset
for
l\neq s
and
\cup_{j=\tau+1}^{m}\bar{\Omega}_{j}=\bar{\Omega}
.
Similar to the previous case we require

(1.10)

\nabla\bigg(G_{j,\Omega}^{*}(x)+\log h_{j}(x)\bigg)\bigg|_{x=q_{j}}=0,\quad 1\leq j\leq m.

Then we have,

Theorem 1.4
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
1.3
, except that here
\alpha_{M}\leq 0
and (
1.10
) holds, in any one of the following cases:

(1)

\alpha_{M}=0
,
L_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
,
\det\big(D^{2}f_{\Omega}^{*}\big)(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})\neq 0
.

(2)

\alpha_{M}=0
,
L_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})=0
,
D_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
,
\det\big(D^{2}f_{\Omega}^{*}\big)(p_{\tau+1},\cdots,p_{m})\neq 0

(3)

\alpha_{M}<0
,
D_{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})\neq 0

the same uniqueness property as in Theorem
1.3
holds true.

Since the proof of Theorem
1.1
is
very long and requires several estimates of independent interest,
we summarize below the main ideas behind it. One of the crucial steps is to obtain highly refined estimates around each blowup point. The mixture of blowup points forces one to consider different contributions in the many needed expansions each one with its own order of magnitude. The difference of these orders due to the singular sources leads to tremendous difficulties in the asymptotic analysis. This is a major obstacle which we need to overcome, the first tool to achieve this goal being new refined asymptotic estimates around each blowup point, actually far better than those at hand so far. As a matter of fact, known estimates always stops at order two, whenever the second derivatives of coefficient functions shows up. Here we need to carry out at least a fourth order expansion, taking advantage as well of some elegant algebraic identities. Among other things which will be clear in the course of the proof, in this context this has a major advantage since, unlike
[
10
,
11
,
81
]
,
in the very final step of the uniqueness proof, we do not need anymore to consider refined Pohozaev identities, but just rely on the classical one.

We do not want to state here such involved expansions of independent interest and refer, respectively, to Theorem
3.1
and (
3.32
), (
3.33
) for positive singular sources, Theorem
3.2
and (
3.35
), (
3.36
) for negative singular sources, and Theorem
3.3
and Remark
3.5
for regular blow-up points.
The analysis is rather hard, which is why, concerning singular sources, we provide full details in the positive case (
3.32
), (
3.33
), while we will be more sketchy about the negative case (
3.35
), (
3.36
). Unlike positive singular sources and regular blow up points, at least to our knowledge, the estimate in Theorem
3.2
is the first of its kind. No shortcut seems to be at hand for the regular case, which we have to discuss in full detail. Actually, in this case, we have to come up with a subtle refinement of an estimate (
[
44
,
83
]
) about the vanishing of the gradient of the normalized weight (say
\nabla\bar{h}_{k}
, see (
3.41
)) as well as, interestingly enough, with the entirely new and rather surprising vanishing estimate of
\nabla\Delta\bar{h}_{k}
, see (
3.42
).

However, even with those refined estimates at hand, the proof of the uniqueness result is not trivial at all. Indeed previous uniqueness results
[
10
,
11
,
81
]
are based on the following idea: the normalized difference of two solutions (say
\xi_{k}
) should converge after suitable
rescaling to some element of the kernel of a linear operator on the plane, the proof being concluded by showing that the coefficients defining this function
(say roughly speaking
b_{0},b_{1},b_{2}
) vanish one by one. However, since we miss the assumptions mentioned above in
[
11
,
81
]
, we face at this stage two genuine new difficulties. First of all the proof of
b_{0}=0
requires a further improvement of the estimates about the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
and then a sort of boostrap argument, showing a self improving property of this decay. This part of the proof is really delicate, see Lemma
4.3
and the part
running from Remark
4.6
to Remark
4.7
. If this were not enough, in this situation to prove that
b_{1}=0=b_{2}
one needs to know the rate of vanishing of the approximating sequence of
b_{0}
, say
b_{0,k}
, see Lemma
4.7
. Actually one needs somehow to really restart the contradiction argument ab initio by taking into account the contribution of those terms proportional to
b_{0,k}
with the aim of estimating again the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
. This is very long and non trivial and requires other delicate estimates, see for example Lemma
4.6
and Proposition
4.1
. We split this last part of the proof into seven steps, in an attempt to make it easier to read, see subsection
4.3
for further details.

Our uniqueness result has several applications. First of all, it furnishes an alternative argument for computing the topological degree of the resolvent operator for equation (
1.1
), which has been previously established in
[
31
,
34
]
with and without singularities respectively. In more specific terms, the authors in
[
30
,
33
]
derived a sharp estimate for characterizing the blow-up rates of bubbling solutions and demonstrated that all bubbling solutions exist within a special set. Subsequently, they computed the degree of the associated Fredholm operator within this set and the desired topological degree of equation (
1.1
) is obtained. With the assistance of this uniqueness result, we can now accurately identify bubbling solutions as the parameter
\rho
crosses critical positions, compute the Morse index of certain operators, and calculate the contribution of degree from the bubbling solutions, which yields at once a much direct computation of the topological degree for (
1.1
). Secondly, it is well-known (
[
30
]
) that the regular blowup points are critical points of the Kirchhoff-Routh function associated with the ambient manifold. Since according to our result, for a fixed set of singular blow up points, there is only one bubbling solution attached to each such point, we could in principle exactly count the total number of bubbling solutions of this sort, once those critical points are completely classified. This is a relevant
fact since the exact counting of solutions is in general a challenging open problem about (
1.1
), (
1.9
). However, as mentioned above, a series of groundbreaking results concerning this point have been recently push forward in the case of the flat two torus with singular sources, see
[
53
]
and the references quoted therein. Last but not least, we expect the uniqueness result to be the first step to establish non-degeneracy of the bubbling sequences of (
1.1
), which would allow in turn the evaluation of the specific heat in the negative temperature regime induced by fixed vortices in the statistical mechanics mean field description of 2D flows and plasmas, in the same spirit of
[
9
]
for the regular case.

A word of warning about notations.
B_{\tau}=B_{\tau}(0)
will always denote the ball centered at the origin
of some local isothermal coordinates
y\in B_{\tau}
. Whenever
B_{\tau}
is any such ball centered at some point
0=y(p),p\in M
, then we will denote by
\Omega(p,\tau)\subset M
the pre-image of
B_{\tau}
.
On the other side,
B(p,\tau)\subset M

will always denote a geodesic ball. Also, in many different estimates we will need some positive small number
\epsilon_{0}>0
, which will change in general from estimate to estimate.

The organization of this paper is as follows. In section two we collect some preliminary estimates needed for the proof of the main theorems. In section three we derive pointwise estimates for local equations. Then in section four and section five we provide the proofs of the main theorems.

Research Grant No. SRG2023-00067-FST. D. Bartolucci’s Research is partially supported by the MIUR Excellence Department Project MatMod@TOV awarded to the Department of Mathematics, University of Rome Tor Vergata and by PRIN project 2022, ERC PE1_11,
”
Variational and Analytical aspects of Geometric PDEs
”. D. Bartolucci is member of the INDAM Research Group ”Gruppo Nazionale per l’Analisi Matematica, la Probabilità e le loro Applicazioni”.

2.
Preliminary Estimates

Since the proof of the main theorems requires delicate analysis, in this section we list some estimates established in
[
4
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
30
,
33
,
50
,
83
,
84
]
.

Let
{\rm w}_{k}
be a sequence of solutions of (
1.2
) with
\rho=\rho_{k}
and assume that
{\rm w}_{k}
blows up at
m
points
\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}\}
. To describe the bubbling profile of
{\rm w}_{k}
near
p_{j}
, we set

u_{k}={\rm w}_{k}-\log\bigg(\int_{M}He^{w_{k}}{\rm d}\mu\bigg),

so that

\int_{M}He^{u_{k}}{\rm d}\mu=1,

and the equation for
u_{k}
takes the form,

\Delta_{g}u_{k}+\rho_{k}(He^{u_{k}}-1)=0\quad{\rm in}\ \;M.

From well known results about Liouville-type equations (
[
21
,
50
]
),

u_{k}-\overline{u_{k}}\ \to\sum_{j=1}^{m}8\pi(1+\alpha_{j})G(x,p_{j})\quad{\rm in}\ \;{\rm C}_{\rm loc}^{2}(M\backslash\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}\})

where
\overline{u}_{k}
is the average of
u_{k}
on
M
:

\overline{u}_{k}=\int_{M}u_{k}{\rm d}\mu
. For later convenience we fix
r_{0}>0
small enough and
M_{j}\subset M,1\leq j\leq m
such that

M=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\overline{M}_{j};\quad M_{j}\cap M_{l}=\varnothing,\ {\rm if}\ j\neq l;\quad B(p_{j},3r_{0})\subset M_{j},\ j=1,\cdots,m.

According to this definition
M_{1}=M
, if
m=1
.

The notation about local maximum is of particular relevance in this context. If
p_{j}
is a regular blow up point (i.e.
\alpha_{j}=0
) we define
p_{k,j}
and
\lambda_{k,j}
as follows,

\lambda_{k,j}=u_{k}(p_{k,j})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\max_{B(p_{j},r_{0})}u_{k},

while if
p_{j}
is a singular blow up point (i.e.
\alpha_{j}\neq 0
), then we define,

p_{k,j}:=p_{j}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\lambda_{k,j}:=u_{k}(p_{k,j}).

Next, let us define the so called ”standard bubble”
U_{k,j}
to be the solution of

\Delta U_{k,j}+\rho_{k}h_{j}(p_{k,j})|x-p_{k,j}|^{2\alpha_{j}}e^{U_{k,j}}=0\quad{\rm in}\ \;\mathbb{R}^{2}

which takes the form (
[
28
,
29
,
65
]
),

U_{k,j}(x)=\lambda_{k,j}-2\log\Big(1+\frac{\rho_{k}h_{j}(p_{k,j})}{8(1+\alpha_{j})^{2}}e^{\lambda_{k,j}}|x-p_{k,j}|^{2(1+\alpha_{j})}\Big).

It is well-known (
[
4
,
21
,
50
]
) that
u_{k}
can be approximated by the standard bubbles
U_{k,j}
near
p_{j}
up to a uniformly bounded error term:

\big|u_{k}(x)-U_{k,j}(x)\big|\leq C,\quad x\in B(p_{j},r_{0}).

As a consequence, in particular we have,

|\lambda_{k,i}-\lambda_{k,j}|\leq C,\quad 1\leq i,j\leq m,

for some
C
independent of
k
.

In case
\tau<m
, it has been shown in
[
30
]
that,

(2.1)

\nabla(\log h+G_{j}^{*})(p_{k,j})=O(\lambda_{k,j}e^{-\lambda_{k,j}}),\quad\tau+1\leq j\leq m,

which, in view of the non-degeneracy condition

\det\big(D^{2}f^{*}(p_{\tau+1},\cdot,p_{m})\big)\neq 0,

readily implies that,

(2.2)

|p_{k,j}-p_{j}|=O(\lambda_{k,j}e^{-\lambda_{k,j}}),\quad\tau+1\leq j\leq m.

Later, sharper estimates were obtained in
[
33
,
84
]
for
1\leq j\leq\tau
and
in
[
30
,
44
,
83
]
for
\tau+1\leq j\leq m
.

By using
\lambda_{i}^{k}
to denote the maximum of
u_{i}^{k}
,
i=1,2
which share the same value of the parameter
\rho_{k}
, then it is a simple consequence of
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
(see
[
10
,
81
]
) that

(2.3)

|\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}|\leq Ce^{-\gamma\lambda_{1}^{k}},\mbox{ \rm for some }\gamma>0.

Let us also recall that it has been established in
[
19
,
20
]
that (see (
1.6
))
\rho_{*}=\lim\limits_{k\to+\infty}\rho_{k}
.
Concerning the difference between
\rho_{k}
and
\rho_{*}
,
we set

\rho_{k,j}=\rho_{k}\int\limits_{\Omega(p_{j},\tau)}He^{u_{k}}.

For
\alpha_{j}\geq 0
, the following estimates hold (see
[
30
]
,
[
33
]
):

Theorem 2.1
.

There exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
d_{j}>0
such that,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho_{k,j}-8\pi(1+\alpha_{j})=2\pi d_{j}e^{-\frac{\lambda_{k,j}}{1+\alpha_{j}}}+O(e^{-\frac{1+\epsilon_{0}}{1+\alpha_{j}}\lambda_{k,j}}),&1\leq j\leq\tau,\\
\\
\rho_{k,j}-8\pi=O\big(\lambda_{k,j}e^{-\lambda_{k,j}}\big),&\tau+1\leq j\leq m.\end{cases}

As mentioned above, we were not aware of any result of this sort about the case
\alpha_{j}\in(-1,0)
and we refer to the new estimates in Theorem
3.2
below concerning this point.

Assuming without loss of generality that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{M}
, then it has been proved in
[
33
]
that

(2.4)

\rho_{k}-\rho_{*}=L(\mathbf{p})e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{1+\alpha_{M}}}+O(e^{-\frac{1+\epsilon_{0}}{1+\alpha_{M}}\lambda_{k,j}})

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
L(\mathbf{p})
as defined in (
1.5
).
Next let us state two relevant results about suitably defined global linear problems.

2.1.
A uniqueness lemma

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\alpha>-1
,
\alpha\not\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\phi
be a
C^{2}
solution of

\begin{cases}\Delta\phi+8(1+\alpha)^{2}|x|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{\alpha}}\phi=0\;\;\hbox{ {\rm in} }\;\;\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\\
|\phi(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{\tau},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{cases}

where

U_{\alpha}(x)=\log\frac{1}{(1+|x|^{2\alpha+2})^{2}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\tau\in[0,1).

Then there exists some constant
b_{0}
such that

\phi(x)=b_{0}\frac{1-|x|^{2(1+\alpha)}}{1+|x|^{2(1+\alpha)}}.

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

The proof for
\alpha>0
can be found in
[
33
,
81
]
. We just focus on
-1<\alpha<0
. Let
b_{0}=\phi(0)
, we set

\Phi=\phi-b_{0}\frac{1-|x|^{2(1+\alpha)}}{1+|x|^{2(1+\alpha)}}.

Let
l\geq 1
be an
integer. We define

\phi_{l}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\cos(l\theta)d\theta.

Then
\phi_{l}
satisfies

(2.5)

\begin{cases}\phi_{l}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}\phi_{l}^{\prime}(r)+(8(1+\alpha)^{2}r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{\alpha}}-\frac{l^{2}}{r^{2}})\phi_{l}(r)=0,\quad 0<r<\infty,\\
\\
\lim_{r\to 0}\phi_{l}(r)=0,\quad|\phi_{l}(r)|\leq C(1+r)^{\tau},\quad r>0.\end{cases}

We claim that
\phi_{l}\equiv 0
for all
l\geq 1
.
Note that by assumption
\phi_{l}(r)=o(r)
at infinity and
\phi_{l}(0)=0
. Then by direct computation
we verify that the following functions are two linearly independent fundamental
solutions of (
2.5
):

(2.6)

\begin{split}F_{1,l}(r)&=\frac{(\frac{l}{1+\alpha}+1)r^{l}+(\frac{l}{1+\alpha}-1)r^{l+2(1+\alpha)}}{1+r^{2(1+\alpha)}},\\
F_{2,l}(r)&=\frac{(\frac{l}{1+\alpha}+1)r^{-l+2(1+\alpha)}+(\frac{l}{1+\alpha}-1)r^{-l}}{1+r^{2(1+\alpha)}}.\end{split}

Consequently
\phi_{l}=c_{1}F_{1,l}+c_{2}F_{2,l}
where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are
two constants. From (
2.5
) we see that
c_{1}=0
because
otherwise
|\phi_{l}|\sim r^{l}
at
\infty
. Similarly by
observing the behavior of
\phi_{l}
at
0
we have
c_{2}=0
because
otherwise
|\phi_{l}|\sim r^{-l}
near
0
. So
\phi_{l}\equiv 0
for all

l\geq 1
. The same argument also shows that the projection of

\phi
along
\sin(l\theta)
(
\forall l\geq 1
) is
0
. Finally,
from
\Phi(0)=0
the projection of
\Phi
along the constant direction has a zero initial condition.
Besides the known fundamental solution of this ODE
(which is of course
\frac{1-|x|^{2(1+\alpha)}}{1+|x|^{2(1+\alpha)}}
),
the other one has a logarithmic growth near the origin and infinity.
This is done by standard ODE techniques,
see for example
[
84
]
. The boundedness of
\Phi
near zero rules out the
second fundamental solution while the zero initial condition rules out the first,
whence we conclude that
\Phi(x)\equiv 0
.
∎

For
\alpha=0
, the following lemma has been proved in
[
30
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\varphi
be a
C^{2}
solution of

\begin{cases}\Delta\varphi+e^{U}\varphi=0\quad&{\rm in}\ \;\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\\
|\varphi|\leq c\big(1+|x|\big)^{\kappa}\quad&{\rm in}\ \;\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{cases}

where
U(x)=\log\frac{8}{(1+|x|^{2})^{2}}
and
\kappa\in[0,1)
. Then there exist constants
b_{0}
,
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
such that

\varphi=b_{0}\varphi_{0}+b_{1}\varphi_{1}+b_{2}\varphi_{2},

where

\varphi_{0}(x)=\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{1+|x|^{2}},\quad\varphi_{1}(x)=\frac{x_{1}}{1+|x|^{2}},\quad\varphi_{2}(x)=\frac{x_{2}}{1+|x|^{2}}.

3.
Local asymptotic analysis near a blowup point

In this section we study the asymptotic expansion of a sequence of blowup solutions near a non-quantized singular source. By a straightforward change of variables and with an obvious abuse of notations, we assume that
u_{k}
denotes in local coordinates a sequence of blowup solutions of

\Delta u_{k}+|x|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(x)e^{u_{k}}=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad B_{2\tau}

where
B_{\tau}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
for
\tau>0
is the ball centered at the origin with radius
\tau>0
,

(3.1)

\frac{1}{C}\leq h_{k}(x)\leq C,\quad\|D^{m}h_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{2\tau})}\leq C\quad\forall x\in B_{2\tau},\quad\forall m\leq 5,

and

\int_{B_{2\tau}}|x|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(x)e^{u_{k}}<C,

for some uniform
C>0
. The sequence
\{u_{k}\}
has its only blowup point at the origin:

(3.2)

\mbox{there exists}\quad z_{k}\to 0,\mbox{ such that}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}u_{k}(z_{k})\to\infty

and for any fixed
K\Subset B_{2\tau}\setminus\{0\}
, there exists
C(K)>0
such that

(3.3)

u_{k}(x)\leq C(K),\quad x\in K.

It is also standard to assume that
u_{k}
has bounded oscillation on
\partial B_{\tau}
:

(3.4)

|u_{k}(x)-u_{k}(y)|\leq C\quad\forall x,y\in\partial B_{\tau},

for some uniform
C>0
.
The following harmonic function
\psi_{k}
is needed to encode the boundary oscillation of
u_{k}
:

\begin{cases}\Delta\psi_{k}=0\quad\hbox{in }B_{\tau},\\
\\
\psi_{k}=u_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int_{\partial B_{\tau}}u_{k}dS\quad\hbox{on }\partial B_{\tau}.\end{cases}

Since
u_{k}
has a bounded oscillation on
\partial B_{\tau}
(see (
3.4
)),
u_{k}-\psi_{k}
is constant on
\partial B_{\tau}
, the bounded oscillation of
u_{k}
means
\|D^{m}\psi_{k}\|_{B_{\tau/2}}\leq C(m)
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
The mean value property of harmonic functions also gives
\psi_{k}(0)=0
.
For the sake of simplicity let us define,

V_{k}(x)=h_{k}(x)e^{\psi_{k}(x)},\quad\varepsilon_{k}=e^{-\frac{u_{k}(0)}{2(1+\alpha)}}

and

(3.5)

v_{k}(y)=u_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)+2(1+\alpha)\log\varepsilon_{k}-\psi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y),\quad y\in\Omega_{k}:=B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}(0).

Obviously we have
V_{k}(0)=h_{k}(0)
,
\Delta\log V_{k}(0)=\Delta\log h_{k}(0)
.
Let

(3.6)

U_{k}(y)=-2\log\left(1+\frac{h_{k}(0)}{8(\alpha+1)^{2}}|y|^{2\alpha+2}\right),

be the standard bubble which satisfies

\Delta U_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}(y)}=0\quad\hbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In the remaining part of this section we split the discussion into three subsections according to the sign of
\alpha
(
\alpha>0
,
\alpha<0
and
\alpha=0
).

3.1.
Asymptotic analysis for
\alpha>0

We say that a function is separable if it is the product of a polynomial of two variables
P(x_{1},x_{2})
times a radial function with certain decay at infinity, where
P
is the sum of monomials
x_{1}^{n}x_{2}^{m}
where
n,m
are non negative integers and at least one among them is odd. The main result of this subsection is

Theorem 3.1
.

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau}}h_{k}(x)|x|^{2\alpha}e^{u_{k}}=~

\displaystyle 8\pi(1+\alpha)\left(1-\frac{e^{-u_{k}(0)}}{e^{-u_{k}(0)}+\frac{h_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}\tau^{2\alpha+2}}\right)

\displaystyle+d_{1,k}\Delta\log h_{k}(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+T_{1}(V_{k})\varepsilon_{k}^{4}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}),

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
, where,

\displaystyle d_{1,k}=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{(1+\alpha)\sin\frac{\pi}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{16\pi(1+\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha}\frac{|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}}{V_{k}(0)\Delta\log V_{k}(0)}+\frac{16\pi(1+\alpha)^{4}}{\alpha V_{k}(0)}\right)\tau^{-2\alpha}\varepsilon_{k}^{2\alpha},

and
T_{1}(V_{k})
is a bounded function of
D^{\beta}V_{k}(0)
for
|\beta|=0,1,2,3,4
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{k}=e^{-\frac{u_{k}(0)}{2+2\alpha}}
, we denote by
z_{k}
the maximum point of
u_{k}
. It is proved in

[
4
,
21
]
that
\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}z_{k}\to 0
. Note that we are allowed to use

u_{k}(0)
to define
\varepsilon_{k}
since it follows from the results in
[
4
,
21
]
that

u_{k}(0)/u_{k}(z_{k})=1+o(1)
. From the definition of
\psi_{k}
,

u_{k}-\psi_{k}
has vanishing oscillation on
\partial B_{1}
and it satisfies

\Delta(u_{k}-\psi_{k})+|x|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(x)e^{(u_{k}-\psi_{k})(x)}=0\quad\hbox{ in }B_{1}.

Without loss of generality we assume

V_{k}(0)=h_{k}(0)\to 8(1+\alpha)^{2}.

A straightforward inspection of the arguments in
[
4
,
21
]
shows that, letting
x_{k}
be the maximum point of
u_{k}-\psi_{k}
,
then we still have
\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}x_{k}\to 0
. Next let us define
v_{k}
as in (
3.5
),

v_{k}(y)=u_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\psi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-u_{k}(0),\quad y\in\Omega_{k}:=B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}(0),

which satisfies,

\begin{cases}\Delta v_{k}(y)+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{v_{k}(y)}=0,\quad y\in\Omega_{k},\\
\\
v_{k}(0)=0,\quad y_{k}:=\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}x_{k}\to 0,\\
\\
v_{k}(y)\to(-2)\log(1+|y|^{2\alpha+2})\quad\mbox{in}\quad C^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
\\
v_{k}(y_{1})=v_{k}(y_{2}),\quad\forall y_{1},y_{2}\in\partial\Omega_{k}=\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}(0).\end{cases}

Note that in the second equation above we use
y_{k}
to denote the
maximum point of
v_{k}
. Let
U_{k}
be the standard bubble defined above,

U_{k}(y)=(-2)\log\left(1+\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(\alpha+1)^{2}}|y|^{2\alpha+2}\right),

(recall that
V_{k}(0)=h_{k}(0)
) then it is well known that for all
\alpha>-1
(see
[
4
,
21
,
84
]
)

|v_{k}(y)-U_{k}(y)|\leq C,\quad|y|\leq\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.

Therefore
U_{k}
is the first term in the expansion of
v_{k}
and to identify
the second term we need the radial function
g_{k}
which satisfies:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}g_{k}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}g_{k}^{\prime}(r)+(r^{2\alpha}V_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}(r)}-\frac{1}{r^{2}})g_{k}(r)=-V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha+1}e^{U_{k}(r)},~r\in(0,\infty)\\
\\
\lim\limits_{r\to 0+}g_{k}(r)=\lim\limits_{r\to\infty}g_{k}(r)=0,\quad|g_{k}(r)|\leq C\frac{r}{1+r^{2\alpha+2}}.\end{cases}

By direct computation one checks that in fact

(3.7)

g_{k}(r)=-\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\frac{r}{1+\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}r^{2\alpha+2}},

solves that equation. Let

(3.8)

c_{1,k}(y)=\varepsilon_{k}g_{k}(r)\nabla(\log V_{k})(0)\cdot\theta,\quad\theta=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),\;\theta_{j}=y_{j}/r,j=1,2.

Then by a straightforward evaluation we see that
c_{1,k}
satisfies,

(3.9)

\Delta c_{1,k}+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}(y)}c_{1,k}=-\varepsilon_{k}(\nabla(\log V_{k})(0)\cdot y)V_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}(y)},\quad

in
\Omega_{k}
. We claim that
c_{1,k}
is the second term in the expansion of
v_{k}
. Let

w_{k}(y)=v_{k}(y)-U_{k}(y)-c_{1,k},

and observe that the oscillation of
w_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha})
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For any
\delta>0
small enough, there exists
C(\delta)>0
such
that for all
k
large enough we have,

|w_{k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|y|)^{\delta}\quad\mbox{{\rm in}}\quad\Omega_{k}.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

By using the equations satisfied by
U_{k}
and
c_{1,k}
we see that

\Delta(U_{k}+c_{1,k})+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{1,k}}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})(1+r)^{-2-2\alpha}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k}.

Then we can write the equation for
w_{k}
as follows,

\begin{cases}\Delta w_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{\xi_{k}}w_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})(1+r)^{-2-2\alpha},\\
\\
w_{k}(0)=0,\quad|w_{k}(y)|\leq C,\quad y\in\Omega_{k},\quad w_{k}|_{\partial\Omega_{k}}=\tilde{a}_{k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}),\end{cases}

where
\xi_{k}
is obtained by the mean value theorem,
e^{\xi_{k}}=(e^{v_{k}}-e^{U_{k}+c_{1,k}})/w_{k}
, and consequently satisfies
e^{\xi_{k}(\eta)}\leq C(1+|\eta|)^{-4-4\alpha}
. Let

\tilde{\Lambda}_{k}=\max\frac{|w_{k}(y)|}{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|y|)^{\delta}},\quad y\in\bar{\Omega}_{k}.

Our goal is to show
\tilde{\Lambda}_{k}=O(1)
. We prove this by a contradiction and assume that
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\to\infty
. Then let
\widetilde{y}_{k}
denote a point where
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}
is attained and let us define

\widetilde{w}_{k}(y)=\frac{w_{k}(y)}{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}},

which satisfies,

(3.10)

\Delta\widetilde{w}_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{\xi_{k}}\widetilde{w}_{k}=O(1)\frac{(1+r)^{-2-2\alpha}}{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}.

Since the oscillation of
w_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha})
, the oscillation of
\widetilde{w}_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is of order
o(1)(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{-\delta}
. Moreover by the definition of
\widetilde{w}_{k}
we have

(3.11)

|\widetilde{w}_{k}(y)|=\left|\frac{w_{k}(y)}{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\tilde{\Lambda}_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta}}\frac{(1+|y|)^{\delta}}{(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}\right|\leq\frac{(1+|y|)^{\delta}}{(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}.

Therefore we conclude that, along a subsequence if necessary,
\widetilde{w}_{k}
converges in
C^{\beta}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(for some
\beta\in(0,1)
) to a
solution
w
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta w+r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{\alpha}}w=0,\quad\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\\
w(0)=0,\quad|w(y)|\leq C(1+|y|)^{\delta}.\end{array}\right.

If we had, possibly along a sub-subsequence, that
\widetilde{y}_{k}
converge to
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then we would also have

|w(y_{0})|=1
by continuity. However this is impossible because an
application of Lemma
2.1
shows that
w\equiv 0
. Therefore necessarily
|\widetilde{y}_{k}|\to+\infty
.
It follows from
|\widetilde{w}_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k})|=1
and the Green representation formula that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\pm 1=

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k})-\widetilde{w}_{k}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}(G_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k},\eta)-G_{k}(0,\eta))

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{|\eta|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}\eta)e^{\xi_{k}(\eta)}\widetilde{w}_{k}(\eta)+\frac{O(1)(1+|\eta|^{-2-2\alpha})}{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}\bigg\}d\eta+o(1)

where
G_{k}
is the Green function with Dirichlet boundary condition on
\Omega_{k}
, which takes the form,

G_{k}(y,\eta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|y-\eta|+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\left(\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\left|\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{-2}y}{|y|^{2}}-\eta\right|\right).

Note that in (
3.12
) we used the fact that
\mbox{\rm osc}_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\widetilde{w}_{k}=o(1)
. We deduce from (
3.12
) that,

(3.13)

1\leq\int_{\Omega_{k}}|G_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k},\eta)-G_{k}(0,\eta)|\bigg(\frac{(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{-\delta}}{(1+|\eta|)^{4+2\alpha-\delta}}+o(1)\frac{(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{-\delta}}{(1+|\eta|)^{2+2\alpha}}\bigg)d\eta,

where we used

|{\widetilde{w}_{k}(\eta)}|\leq\frac{(1+|\eta|)^{\delta}}{(1+|y_{k}|)^{\delta}},\quad e^{\xi_{k}(\eta)}\leq C(1+|\eta|)^{-4-4\alpha},\quad\tilde{\Lambda}_{k}\to\infty.

We will come up with a contradiction to (
3.13
) by showing that:

(3.14)

\int_{\Omega_{k}}|G_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k},\eta)-G_{k}(0,\eta)|\frac{(1+|\eta|)^{\delta-4-2\alpha}+o(1)(1+|\eta|)^{-2-2\alpha}}{(1+|\widetilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}d\eta=o(1).

There are only two possibilities, either along a subsequence
|\widetilde{y}_{k}|=o(1)\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}
, or
|\widetilde{y}_{k}|\sim\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}
.
Assume first that
|\widetilde{y}_{k}|=o(1)\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}
and observe that
G_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k},\eta)
can be written as follows

\displaystyle G_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{k},\eta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\widetilde{y}_{k}-\eta|+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\bigg|\frac{\widetilde{y}_{k}}{|\widetilde{y}_{k}|}-\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\widetilde{y}_{k}|\eta\bigg|.

Thus we are reduced to show that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\bigg(\bigg|\log\frac{|\tilde{y}_{k}-\eta|}{|\eta|}\bigg|+\bigg|\log\big|\frac{\tilde{y}_{k}}{|\tilde{y}_{k}|}-\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\tilde{y}_{k}|\eta\big|\bigg|\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\frac{(1+|\eta|)^{-4-2\alpha}}{(1+|\tilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}+o(1)\frac{(1+|\eta|)^{-2-2\alpha}}{(1+|\tilde{y}_{k}|)^{\delta}}\bigg)d\eta=o(1),

which is a consequence of elementary estimates. In fact, for fixed
\widetilde{y}_{k}
, we decompose
\Omega_{k}
as follows
\Omega_{k}=E_{1}\cup E_{2}\cup E_{3}
where

E_{1}:=\left\{\eta\in\Omega;~|\eta|<\frac{|\widetilde{y}_{k}|}{2}\right\},\quad E_{2}=\left\{\eta\in\Omega_{k};~|\eta-\widetilde{y}_{k}|<\frac{|\widetilde{y}_{k}|}{2}\right\},

and

E_{3}=\Omega_{k}\setminus(E_{1}\cup E_{2}).

The integrals on the left of (
3.15
) over the regions
E_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
can be easily verified to be
o(1)
in view of the following elementary inequalities:

\bigg|\log\frac{|\widetilde{y}_{k}-\eta|}{|\eta|}\bigg|\leq\begin{cases}C\log|\widetilde{y}_{k}|+C|\log|\eta||,\quad&\eta\in E_{1},\\
\\
C\log|\widetilde{y}_{k}|+|\log|\widetilde{y}_{k}-\eta||,\quad&\eta\in E_{2},\\
\\
C|\widetilde{y}_{k}|/|\eta|,\quad&\eta\in E_{3}.\end{cases}

The second logarithmic term can be estimated as follows,

\bigg|\log\left|\frac{\tilde{y}_{k}}{|\tilde{y}_{k}|}-\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\tilde{y}_{k}|\eta\,\right|\bigg|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\tilde{y}_{k}||\eta|,\quad|\tilde{y}_{k}|=o(1)\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.

Next we consider the case
|\tilde{y}_{k}|\sim\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}
. For the Green function it is enough to observe that

{|G_{k}(\tilde{y}_{k},\eta)-G_{k}(0,\eta)|\leq C\left(|\log|\eta||+|\log|y_{k}-\eta||+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),}

for some uniform
C>0
. Then
(
3.14
) follows once more by elementary estimates. This fact concludes the proof of Proposition
3.1
and thus the first step in the expansion of
v_{k}
. ∎

Remark 3.1
.

We will often need elliptic estimates of the same sort as those used in the proof of Proposition
3.1
above. To avoid repetitions we point out here a few key assumptions, which, whenever verified at the same time, allow the adoption of the same argument. First of all, in equation (
3.10
), the right hand side has to be
O(r^{-2-\delta})
for some positive
\delta
and
r
large enough. Moreover we need the inequality in (
3.11
) ,
w_{k}(0)=0
and
w_{k}
having small enough oscillation on the boundary.

Before making further improvements in the expansion, we recall that

\theta_{1}=y_{1}/r=\cos\theta
,
\theta_{2}=y_{2}/r=\sin\theta
.
Now we calculate the equation of
U_{k}+c_{1,k}
in more detail. We first write down the equation for
U_{k}+c_{1,k}
as follows,

\displaystyle\Delta(U_{k}+c_{1,k})+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{1,k}}

\displaystyle=\Delta U_{k}+\Delta c_{1,k}+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}+c_{1,k}+\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}}

\displaystyle=\Delta U_{k}+\Delta c_{1,k}+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}\bigg(1+c_{1,k}+\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left(c_{1,k}+\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3}r^{3})\bigg).

The Taylor expansion of the logarithmic term gives

\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}=\varepsilon_{k}\nabla\log V_{k}(0)\cdot\theta r+\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(r^{2}\Theta_{2}+\mathcal{R}_{2})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3}r^{3}),

where

\displaystyle\Theta_{2}=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{11}\log V_{k}(0)(\theta_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2})+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{22}\log V_{k}(0)(\theta_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2})+\partial_{12}\log V_{k}(0)\theta_{1}\theta_{2}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\cos 2\theta(\partial_{11}\log V_{k}(0)-\partial_{22}\log V_{k}(0))+2\sin 2\theta\partial_{12}\log V_{k}(0))\bigg)

and

\mathcal{R}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}r^{2}\Delta\log V_{k}(0).

Then we have

\displaystyle c_{1,k}+\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}=\epsilon_{k}\nabla\log V_{k}(0)\cdot\theta(r+g_{k})+\varepsilon_{k}^{2}r^{2}\Theta_{2}+\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3})r^{3},

and

\displaystyle(c_{1,k}+\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)})^{2}=\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(\nabla\log V_{k}(0)\cdot\theta)^{2}(g_{k}+r)^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3})r^{3}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}+\frac{\cos 2\theta}{2}\left((\partial_{1}\log V_{k}(0))^{2}-(\partial_{2}\log V_{k}(0))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{1}(\log V_{k})(0)\partial_{2}(\log V_{k})(0)\sin 2\theta\bigg)(g_{k}+r)^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3}r^{3}).

By using these estimates together with the equation for
U_{k}+c_{1,k}
we have,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\Delta(U_{k}+c_{1,k})+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{1,k}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}^{2}V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)r^{2}+\frac{1}{4}|\nabla(\log V_{k})(0)|^{2}(g_{k}+r)^{2}

\displaystyle+\hat{\Theta}_{2}\bigg)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3})(1+r)^{-1-2\alpha},

where

(3.17)

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}_{2}

\displaystyle=(E_{2,1}(r)\cos 2\theta+E_{2,2}(r)\sin 2\theta)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\left(\partial_{11}(\log V_{k})(0)-\partial_{22}(\log V_{k})(0)\right)r^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\left((\partial_{1}\log V_{k}(0))^{2}-(\partial_{2}\log V_{k}(0))^{2}\right)(g_{k}+r)^{2}\bigg)\cos 2\theta

\displaystyle+\left(\partial_{12}(\log V_{k})(0)\frac{r^{2}}{2}+\frac{\partial_{1}V_{k}(0)}{V_{k}(0)}\frac{\partial_{2}V_{k}(0)}{V_{k}(0)}\frac{(g_{k}+r)^{2}}{2}\right)\sin 2\theta\bigg).

Next we define
c_{0,k}
to be the unique solution of

(3.18)

\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}c_{0,k}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,k}+V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}c_{0,k}\\
=-\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}\bigg((g_{k}+r)^{2}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}+\Delta\log V_{k}(0)r^{2}\bigg).\\
\\
c_{0,k}(0)=c^{\prime}_{0,k}(0)=0,\quad 0<r<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.\end{cases}

By standard ODE theory
c_{0,k}
is the unique solution of (
3.18
) on
0<r<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}
and a rough estimate of
c_{0,k}
shows that,

|c_{0,k}(r)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\log(1+r),\quad 0<r<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.

We will need the following relevant property of
c_{0k}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}=d_{1,k}\Delta\log V_{k}(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})

for a small constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
d_{1,k}
stated as in the claim of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

To better evaluate
c_{0,k}
we use the following function:

\xi(r)=\frac{1-\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}r^{2+2\alpha}}{1+\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}r^{2+2\alpha}},

which solves

\Delta\xi+V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}\xi=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Let

E_{1}^{k}(r)=\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}\bigg((g_{k}+r)^{2}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}+\Delta\log V_{k}(0)r^{2}\bigg)

then integration by parts gives

(3.19)

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}\xi-\partial_{\nu}\xi c_{0,k}=-\int_{\Omega_{k}}E_{1}^{k}\xi.

On
\partial\Omega_{k}=\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}(0)
, we have
\xi=-1+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha})
and
\partial_{\nu}\xi=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3+2\alpha})
.
Clearly we also have,

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}\xi=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})

and

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}\xi c_{0,k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}),

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
since on
\partial\Omega_{k}
it holds,

\xi=-1+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha})\quad\mbox{and }\quad\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3})\log\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.

So (
3.19
) becomes

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}=\int_{\Omega_{k}}E_{1}^{k}\xi+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}).

At this point we use the following two identities derived in
[
33
]
to estimate the right hand side. The first one is

(3.20)

\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{2\alpha+3}e^{U(s)}\frac{(1-s^{2\alpha+2})(\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}-s^{2\alpha+2})^{2}}{(1+s^{2\alpha+2})^{3}}ds=0,

where
U(x)=-2\log(1+|x|^{2\alpha+2})
. Based on this, for
a_{k}=\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}
, since

\frac{s^{2\alpha+3}(\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}-s^{2\alpha+2})^{2}(1-s^{2\alpha+2})}{(1+s^{2\alpha+2})^{5}}=-s^{-2\alpha-1}+O(s^{-4\alpha-3}),\quad s>C\varepsilon_{k}^{-1},

then we deduce that,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}}V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}(g_{k}+r)^{2}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\pi}{2}\frac{|\nabla V_{k}(0)|^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\int_{0}^{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\frac{r^{2\alpha+3}(\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}-a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2})^{2}(1-a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2})}{(1+a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2})^{5}}dr

\displaystyle=b_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha+2}}\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\alpha+3}(\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}-s^{2\alpha+2})^{2}(1-s^{2\alpha+2})}{(1+s^{2\alpha+2})^{5}}ds

\displaystyle=-b_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\int_{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha+2}}\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}^{\infty}\frac{s^{2\alpha+3}(\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}-s^{2\alpha+2})^{2}(1-s^{2\alpha+2})}{(1+s^{2\alpha+2})^{5}}ds

\displaystyle=b_{1,k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+4\alpha}),

where

b_{k}=4\pi(1+\alpha)^{2}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}\left(\frac{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}},

and

b_{1,k}=\frac{16\pi(1+\alpha)^{4}}{\alpha V_{k}(0)}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}\tau^{-2\alpha}\varepsilon_{k}^{2\alpha}.

The second identity reads,

(3.22)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{8(1+\alpha)^{2}s^{2\alpha+3}}{(1+s^{2(1+\alpha)})^{2}}\frac{1-s^{2(1+\alpha)}}{1+s^{2(1+\alpha)}}{\rm d}s=-\frac{4\pi}{(1+\alpha)\sin\frac{\pi}{1+\alpha}}.

Since

\frac{s^{2\alpha+3}(1-s^{2\alpha+2})}{(1+s^{2\alpha+2})^{3}}=-s^{-2\alpha-1}+O(s^{-4\alpha-3}),\quad s>C\varepsilon_{k}^{-1},

we use (
3.22
) to obtain

(3.23)

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}}|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\frac{1-a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)\int_{0}^{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\frac{V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha+3}}{(1+a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2})^{2}}\frac{1-a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2}}{1+a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2}}dr

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)V_{k}(0)a_{k}^{-\frac{2\alpha+4}{2\alpha+2}}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha+2}}\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\alpha+3}(1-s^{2\alpha+2})}{(1+s^{2\alpha+2})^{3}}ds

\displaystyle=b_{2,k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+b_{3,k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+4\alpha})

where

b_{2,k}=-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{(1+\alpha)\sin\frac{\pi}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)

and

b_{3,k}=\frac{16\pi(1+\alpha)^{4}}{\alpha V_{k}(0)}\tau^{-2\alpha}\varepsilon_{k}^{2\alpha}\Delta(\log V_{k})(0).

Therefore, in view of (
3.21
) and (
3.23
) we deduce that,

(3.24)

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}{\partial_{\nu}c_{0,k}}=d_{1,k}\Delta\log V_{k}(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
, where

\displaystyle d_{1,k}=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{(1+\alpha)\sin\frac{\pi}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{16\pi(1+\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha}\frac{|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}}{V_{k}(0)\Delta\log V_{k}(0)}+\frac{16\pi(1+\alpha)^{4}}{\alpha V_{k}(0)}\right)\tau^{-2\alpha}\varepsilon_{k}^{2\alpha}.

Then Lemma
3.1
is established. ∎

Now we derive another intermediate estimate by adding one more term in the expansion of
v_{k}
. This term is
c_{2,k}
which satisfies

(3.25)

\Delta c_{2,k}+V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}c_{2,k}=-\varepsilon_{k}^{2}V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}\hat{\Theta}_{2}.

Toward this goal we first construct a function
f_{2a}(r)
that solves

f_{2a}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}f_{2a}^{\prime}(r)+(V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}-\frac{4}{r^{2}})f_{2a}(r)=-V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}E_{21}

for
0<r<\infty
. Similarly we set
f_{2b}
to solve

f_{2b}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}f_{2b}^{\prime}(r)+(V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}-\frac{4}{r^{2}})f_{2b}(r)=-V_{k}(0)r^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}E_{22}

where
E_{21}
and
E_{22}
are defined in (
3.17
).
Let
F_{12}
and
F_{22}
be the two fundamental solutions as readily derived from the explicit expressions in (
2.6
). We have

|F_{12}(r)|\sim r^{2}
at
0
and
\infty
,
|F_{22}(r)|\sim r^{-2}
at
0
and
\infty
. Then we define
f_{2a}
as follows,

f_{2a}(r)=F_{12}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s^{1+2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}E_{21}F_{22}}{4(1-\frac{4}{(1+\alpha)^{2}})}ds-F_{22}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s^{1+2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}E_{21}F_{12}}{4(1-\frac{4}{(1+\alpha)^{2}})}ds

where
E_{2a}
is the right hand side of the equation of
f_{2a}
. Obviously
f_{2b}
can be constructed in a similar manner.
So we have

(3.26)

|f_{2a}(r)|+|f_{2b}(r)|=O(1+r)^{-2\alpha},\quad 0<r<\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}

and
c_{2,k}
is defined by

(3.27)

c_{2,k}=\varepsilon_{k}^{2}V_{k}(0)f_{2a}(r)\cos(2\theta)+\varepsilon_{k}^{2}V_{k}(0)f_{2b}(r)\sin(2\theta).

With the definition of
c_{2,k}
we estimate how
U_{k}+c_{0,k}+c_{1,k}+c_{2,k}
approximates
v_{k}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

|v_{k}(y)-(U_{k}+c_{0,k}+c_{1,k}+c_{2,k})(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{2\epsilon_{0}}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k},

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

We use

w_{2,k}=v_{k}-(U_{k}+c_{0,k}+c_{1,k}+c_{2,k}).

Then based on (
3.16
),(
3.18
),(
3.25
) we have

(\Delta+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{v_{k}})w_{2,k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3})(1+|y|)^{-1-2\alpha}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k}.

Obviously the right hand side can be written as

O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{-2-\epsilon_{0}/2}
for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
. The oscillation of
w_{2,k}
is not zero. But analysing the oscillations of
c_{1,k}
and
c_{2,k}
we see that the oscillation of
w_{2,k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha})
. Therefore, encoding such an oscillation in a suitable harmonic function
\phi_{2,k}
, we deduce that,

|\phi_{2,k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{3+2\alpha}|y|.

The construction of
c_{0,k},c_{1,k},c_{2,k}
implies that
w_{2,k}(0)=0
. Based on the key points mentioned in Remark
3.1
, we have

|w_{2,k}-\phi_{2,k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{2\epsilon_{0}}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k},

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
small. Since
\phi_{2,k}
can be considered as a part of the error, Lemma
3.2
is established. ∎

Lemma
3.2
will be used in the proof of the main theorem later on. However we still need more terms in the expansion. We shall use
U_{k}+c_{k}
to approximate
v_{k}
,
where

(3.28)

c_{k}=c_{0,k}+\bar{c}_{0,k}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}c_{i,k},

and
c_{0,k}
,
c_{1,k}
,
c_{2,k}
have been already defined. To define the remaining terms we write the expansion of
\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}
as follows,

\displaystyle\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\beta|=1}^{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{|\beta|}\frac{\partial^{\beta}\log V_{k}(0)}{\beta!}y^{\beta}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{5})|y|^{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\beta|=1}^{4}(\varepsilon_{k}^{|\beta|}\Theta_{|\beta|}|y|^{|\beta|}+\varepsilon_{k}^{|\beta|}\mathcal{R}_{|\beta|})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{5})|y|^{5},

where
\mathcal{R}_{|\beta|}
stand for the radial parts (thus
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{3}=0
),
while
\Theta_{|\beta|}
are the collection of projections onto non radial modes. For later convenience let us set:

(3.29)

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{1,k}

\displaystyle=c_{1,k}+\varepsilon_{k}\nabla\log V_{k}(0)\cdot\theta r

\displaystyle=c_{1,k}+\varepsilon_{k}\nabla\log h_{k}(0)\cdot\theta r

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}\nabla\log h_{k}(0)\cdot\theta(r-\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\frac{r}{1+a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2}}),\quad a_{k}=\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}},

and similarly,

\tilde{c}_{2,k}=c_{2,k}+\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\Theta_{2}|y|^{2}.

Let us define,

L=\Delta+|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}},

then the equation for
c_{3,k}
takes the form,

Lc_{3,k}=|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}\bigg(\Theta_{3}r^{3}\varepsilon_{k}^{3}+\tilde{c}_{1,k}(\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\mathcal{R}_{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+c_{0,k})+\frac{1}{6}\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{3}\bigg),

while for
c_{4,k}
we have, using

\tilde{c}_{0,k}=c_{0,k}+\mathcal{R}_{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2},

\displaystyle Lc_{4,k}=|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}\bigg(

\displaystyle\Theta_{4}r^{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{4}+(\tilde{c}_{0,k}\tilde{c}_{2,k})_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2}(\tilde{c}_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}))_{A}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\tilde{c}_{2,k}^{2})_{A}}{2}+(\tilde{c}_{1,k}\tilde{c}_{3,k})_{A}+\frac{1}{24}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{4})_{A}\bigg)

where
(\cdot)_{A}
denotes the angular part of the expression in the brackets.
We define
\bar{c}_{0,k}
as follows,

(3.30)

\displaystyle L\bar{c}_{0,k}=h_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}\bigg(

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{4}+(\tilde{c}_{0,k}\tilde{c}_{2,k})_{r}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2}(\tilde{c}_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}))_{r}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\tilde{c}_{2,k}^{2})_{r}}{2}+(\tilde{c}_{1,k}\tilde{c}_{3,k})_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{c}_{0,k}^{2}+\frac{1}{24}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{4})_{r}\bigg)

where
(\cdot)_{r}
means the radial part of the quantity in the brackets. Similar to
\tilde{c}_{1,k}
and
\tilde{c}_{2,k}
, we define

\tilde{c}_{i,k}=c_{i,k}+\varepsilon_{k}^{i}r^{i}\Theta_{i},\quad i=3,4.

Then direct computation leads to

\Delta(U_{k}+c_{k})+|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{k}}=E_{w}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k}.

where
E_{w}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{5})(1+|y|)^{1-2\alpha}
.
A short remark is in order about the computation. The aim of
c_{i,k}
for
i=1,2,3,4
is to solve angular terms proportional to
e^{i\theta}
whose order is
\varepsilon_{k}^{i}
, for
i=1,2,3,4
, respectively. The purpose of
c_{0,k}
is to solve the radial term starting with
\varepsilon_{k}^{2}
, the aim of
\bar{c}_{0,k}
is to solve the radial term starting with
\varepsilon_{k}^{4}
. Then, by writing
|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{k}}
as

\displaystyle|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{k}}=~

\displaystyle|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}+c_{k}+\mathfrak{h}_{k}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}\left(1+\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{1}{l!}(c_{k}+\mathfrak{h}_{k})^{l}+h.o.t\right)

where
\mathfrak{h}_{k}=\log\frac{V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{V_{k}(0)}
, a lengthy evaluation allows one to identify the terms in each group.

Here we further remark that
c_{l,k}
for
l=1,2,3,4
decays as

(3.31)

|c_{l,k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{l}(1+|y|)^{l-2-2\alpha},\quad l=1,2,3,4

because each
c_{l,k}
is a finite sum of terms of the form
\varepsilon_{k}^{l}e^{il\theta}f_{l}(r)
where

f_{l}(r)
solves

f_{l}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}f_{l}^{\prime}(r)+(r^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}-\frac{l^{2}}{r^{2}})f_{l}(r)=O(1+r)^{l-4-2\alpha},\quad 0<r<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.

In particular, in view of (
2.6
), standard ODE techniques show that

f_{l}(r)=O(1+r)^{l-2-2\alpha},

see for example (
3.26
) above concerning
c_{2,k}
.
Therefore the oscillation of
c_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha})
. If we use
\phi_{1,k}
to encode the oscillation of
c_{k}
,

\Delta\phi_{1,k}=0\quad\mbox{in }\quad\Omega_{k},\quad\phi_{1,k}=-c_{k}(y)+\frac{1}{2\pi\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}c_{k},

then we have,

|\phi_{1,k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{3+2\alpha}|y|\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k}.

In particular by the mean value theorem
\phi_{1,k}(0)=0
and the first term in the Taylor expansion reads
a_{1}^{k}y_{1}+a_{2}^{k}y_{2}
with
|a_{1}^{k}|+|a_{2}^{k}|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{3+2\alpha}
. Let us set
U_{k}+c_{k}+\phi_{1,k}
to approximate
v_{k}
, then we need to consider the error caused by
\phi_{1,k}
, which at first order takes the form
|y|^{2\alpha}V_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{k}}\phi_{1,k}
. It is readily seen that

\displaystyle|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U_{k}+c_{k}}\phi_{1,k}=

\displaystyle-|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}(a_{1}^{k}y_{1}+a_{2}^{k}y_{2})

\displaystyle+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+2\alpha})(1+|y|)^{-2-2\alpha}.

The first term can be eliminated by a separable function
\phi_{2,k}
, which satisfies:

L\phi_{2,k}=|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}(a_{1}^{k}y_{1}+a_{2}^{k}y_{2}),\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k}.

Remark that
\phi_{2,k}
is separable whence
\phi_{2,k}(0)=0
.
Also, recalling that
a_{i}^{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3+2\alpha})
, then we see that,

\phi_{2,k}(y)=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3+2\alpha})(1+r)^{-1-2\alpha}

and the oscillation of
\phi_{2,k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+4\alpha})
. Higher order terms due to
\phi_{1,k}
are readily seen to yield a minor contributions of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{6+4\alpha})(1+|y|)^{-2-2\alpha}
.

Next we set
\phi_{3,k}
to eliminate the term
E_{w}
,

\begin{cases}-\Delta\phi_{3,k}=E_{w}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k},\\
\\
\phi_{3,k}(0)=0,\quad\phi_{3,k}=constant\,\,\mbox{on}\quad\partial\Omega_{k},\end{cases}

which we define as follows,

\phi_{3,k}(y)=\int_{\Omega_{k}}(G_{k}(y,\eta)-G_{k}(0,\eta))E_{w}(\eta)d\eta.

Then by standard estimates we have,

|\phi_{3,k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{5}(\log(1+|y|)+(1+|y|)^{3-2\alpha}).

Recalling the definition of
c_{k}
in (
3.28
) and setting

(3.32)

w_{k}=v_{k}-U_{k}-c_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{i,k},

we have

\displaystyle\Delta w_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{\xi_{k}}w_{k}

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{5})((1+|y|)^{-1-4\alpha}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+2\alpha})(1+|y|)^{-2-4\alpha}

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\delta})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta}

in
\Omega_{k}
for some
\delta>0
. Here we note that the second term on the r.h.s of the first equality comes from
\phi_{2,k}
. Since
w_{k}(0)=0
and
w_{k}
has an oscillation of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+4\alpha})
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
, by Remark
3.1
,
we have

(3.33)

|w_{k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\delta}(1+|y|)^{\delta}.

At this point, scaling the domain of integration to
\Omega_{k}
, we evaluate the integral over
B_{\tau}
as follows,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau}}|x|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(x)e^{u_{k}(x)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{k}}|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}(1+\tilde{c}_{0,k}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2})_{r}

\displaystyle\quad+\bar{c}_{0,k}+\mathcal{R}_{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{4}+(\tilde{c}_{0,k}\tilde{c}_{2,k})_{r}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2}(\tilde{c}_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}))_{r}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{2,k}^{2})_{r}+(\tilde{c}_{1,k}\tilde{c}_{3,k})_{r}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{c}_{0,k}^{2}+\frac{1}{24}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{4})_{r}\bigg)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{k}}|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}+\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\frac{\partial c_{0,k}}{\partial\nu}+\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\frac{\partial\bar{c}_{0,k}}{\partial\nu}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}).

Remark that the contribution from
\phi_{1,k}
vanishes since it is the sum of odd functions. The same observation applies to
\phi_{2,k}
which is a separable function. Higher order contributions are readily seen to be of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})
. By a direct computation we have,

\int_{\Omega_{k}}h_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{k}}=8\pi(1+\alpha)\left(1-\frac{e^{-u_{k}(0)}}{e^{-u_{k}(0)}+\frac{h_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}\tau^{2\alpha+2}}\right),

which gives the first term in the statement of Theorem
3.1
. From (
3.24
) and the expression of
d_{1,k}
right after it we obtain the second term in the statement while for the last term we need a remark about
\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}\bar{c}_{0,k}
. Using (
3.30
) and
\xi
as a solution of
L\xi=0
, we have, as in the case for
c_{0,k}
, that

\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}\bar{c}_{0,k}=-\int_{\Omega_{k}}\bar{E}_{0,k}\xi+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})

where
\bar{E}_{0,k}
stands for the right hand side of (
3.30
). The proof is concluded by the identification of
T_{1}(V_{k})\varepsilon_{k}^{4}
with
\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}\bar{c}_{0,k}
. Thus Theorem
3.1
is established.
\hfill\Box

3.2.
Asymptotic analysis around a negative pole

In this case we use
\beta\in(-1,0)
to denote the strength of a negative pole and define,

\displaystyle\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\frac{\lambda^{k}_{1}}{2(1+\beta)}}.

We use the same notations to approximate
v_{k}
. The main result in this case takes the form,

Theorem 3.2
.

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau}}h_{k}(x)|x|^{2\beta}e^{u_{k}}=~

\displaystyle 8\pi(1+\beta)\left(1-\frac{e^{-u_{k}(0)}}{e^{-u_{k}(0)}+\frac{h_{k}(0)}{8(1+\beta)^{2}}\tau^{2\beta+2}}\right)

\displaystyle+b_{k}(\tau,\beta)+\ell_{k}(\tau,\beta)+O(e^{(-2-\epsilon_{0})u_{k}(0)})

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
, where both
b_{k}(\tau,\beta)
and
\ell_{k}(\tau,\beta)
depend on
|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|
and
\Delta\log V_{k}(0)
,
b_{k}(\tau,\beta)\sim e^{-u_{k}(0)}
,
\ell_{k}(\tau,\beta)\sim e^{-2u_{k}(0)}
for
\tau>0
, and

(3.34)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{b_{k}(\tau,\beta)}{e^{-u_{k}(0)}}=0,\quad\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{\ell_{k}(\tau,\beta)}{e^{-2u_{k}(0)}}=0.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

The proof of Theorem
3.2
is different from that of Theorem
3.1
, as one can see from the following proposition:

Proposition 3.2
.

For fixed
-1<\beta<0
we have,

|v_{k}(y)-U_{k}(y)-c_{1,k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+2\beta-\delta}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad y\in\Omega_{k}

for some
\delta>0
small enough, where
c_{1,k}
is defined as in (
3.8
) with
\alpha
replaced by
\beta
in the definition of
g_{k}
in (
3.7
).

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Direct computation shows that
w_{k}
satisfies

\Delta w_{k}+|y|^{2\beta}e^{V_{k}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}y)}w_{k}=O(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})(1+|y|)^{-2-2\beta}

in
\Omega_{k}
. Since
\beta\in(-1,0)
, the right hand side is bounded above by

O(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+2\beta-\delta})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta}

for a small
\delta>0
. It is easy to see that the oscillation of
c_{1,k}
is
O(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+2\beta})
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
. Thus all the key ingredients mentioned in Remark
3.1
are verified and the conclusion of Proposition
3.2
holds.
∎

It is readily seen that the bound of the error in this case is different. We use the same notations for
c_{0,k}
,
c_{i,k}
(
i=1,2,3
) and in this case we set

(3.35)

w_{k}=v_{k}-U_{k}-c_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{i,k},

and follow step by step the same argument to deduce that,

(3.36)

|w_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4+4\beta-\delta}(1+|y|)^{\delta}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k},

for some small
\delta>0
. We do not provide the details just to avoid repetitions. By using the expansion of
v_{k}
and this estimate we see that the error in the computation of the integral
\int_{B_{\tau}}|x|^{2\beta}h_{k}e^{u_{k}}
is of order
O(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4+4\beta-\delta})
. Since
\beta
is negative, some care is needed about the two identities (
3.20
), (
3.22
). The estimate which replaces (
3.21
) reads,

(3.37)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}}V_{k}(0)r^{2\beta}e^{U_{k}}(g_{k}+r)^{2}|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\pi}{2}\frac{|\nabla V_{k}(0)|^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\int_{0}^{\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{r^{2\beta+3}(\frac{2+\beta}{\beta}-a_{k}r^{2\beta+2})^{2}(1-a_{k}r^{2\beta+2})}{(1+a_{k}r^{2\beta+2})^{5}}dr

\displaystyle=d_{k}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\beta+2}}\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\beta+3}(\frac{2+\beta}{\beta}-s^{2\beta+2})^{2}(1-s^{2\beta+2})}{(1+s^{2\beta+2})^{5}}ds

where
\displaystyle{a_{k}=\frac{V_{k}(0)}{8(1+\beta)^{2}}}
and

d_{k}=4(1+\beta)^{2}\pi|\nabla\log V_{k}(0)|^{2}\left(\frac{8(1+\beta)^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta+1}}.

Here we observe that,

\frac{s^{2\beta+3}(\frac{2+\beta}{\beta}-s^{2\beta+2})^{2}(1-s^{2\beta+2})}{(1+s^{2\beta+2})^{5}}=-s^{-2\beta-1}+O(s^{-4\beta-3}),\quad s\to+\infty.

The term that corresponds to
\Delta(\log V_{k}(0))
in replacement of (
3.23
) takes the form,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}}|y|^{2\beta}V_{k}(0)e^{U_{k}}\Delta(\log V_{k}(0))|y|^{2}\frac{1-a_{k}|y|^{2\beta+2}}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\beta+2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\Delta(\log V_{k}(0))\int_{0}^{\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{V_{k}(0)r^{2\beta+3}}{(1+a_{k}r^{2\beta+2})^{2}}\frac{1-a_{k}r^{2\beta+2}}{1+a_{k}r^{2\beta+2}}dr

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\Delta(\log V_{k}(0))V_{k}(0)a_{k}^{-\frac{2\beta+4}{2\beta+2}}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\beta+2}}\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\beta+3}(1-s^{2\beta+2})}{(1+s^{2\beta+2})^{3}}ds

\displaystyle=\pi\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\frac{\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)}{(V_{k}(0))^{\frac{1}{\beta+1}}}\left(8(1+\beta)^{2}\right)^{\frac{\beta+2}{\beta+1}}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\beta+2}}\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\beta+3}(1-s^{2\beta+2})}{(1+s^{2\beta+2})^{3}}ds.

At this point we set
b_{k}(\tau,\beta)
to be the sum of the last term of (
3.37
) and the last term of (
3.38
),

\displaystyle b_{k}(\tau,\beta)=~

\displaystyle d_{k}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\beta+2}}\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\beta+3}(\frac{2+\beta}{\beta}-s^{2\beta+2})^{2}(1-s^{2\beta+2})}{(1+s^{2\beta+2})^{5}}ds

\displaystyle+\bar{d}_{k}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\int_{0}^{a_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\beta+2}}\tau\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{s^{2\beta+3}(1-s^{2\beta+2})}{(1+s^{2\beta+2})^{3}}ds,

where

\bar{d}_{k}=4(1+\beta)^{2}\pi\Delta(\log V_{k})(0)\left(\frac{8(1+\beta)^{2}}{V_{k}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\beta}}.

It is easy to see that both terms are of the order
O(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+2\beta})
and in particular that
b_{k}(\tau,\beta)\sim e^{-u_{k}(0)}
. If we still use
\bar{c}_{0,k}
to denote the radial correction term that corresponds to the radial error of order
O(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4+4\beta})
, then we identify the
\ell_{k}(\tau,\beta)
term in the statement with
\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}\bar{c}_{0,k}
as in the case
\alpha>0
. Thus (
3.34
) can be verified by the definitions of
b_{k}(\tau,\beta)
and
\ell_{k}(\tau,\beta)
. Theorem
3.2
is proved.
∎

3.3.
Asymptotic analysis around a regular blowup point

In this subsection we analyze the solution near a regular blow up point. After a translation we have that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}=\max\limits_{B_{2\tau}}u_{k}=u_{k}(0)
denotes the height of the bubble, set
\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{k}/2}
and consider the equation,

\Delta u_{k}+\bar{h}_{k}(x)e^{u_{k}}=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad B_{2\tau},

where the ball is now centered at the maximum point.
We assume in particular that there is only one blow up point in
B_{2\tau}
, that the standard uniform bound

\int_{B_{2\tau}}\bar{h}_{k}e^{u_{k}}\leq C
holds and finally that (
3.4
) holds, implying
by well known results (
[
50
]
) that the blow up point is simple.

Let
\phi_{R}^{k}
be the harmonic function defined by the oscillation of
u_{k}
on
\partial B_{\tau}
:

\Delta\phi_{R}^{k}=0,\quad\mbox{in}\,B_{\tau},

\phi_{R}^{k}(x)=u_{k}(x)-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int_{\partial B_{\tau}}u_{k}(y)dS_{y},\,x\in\partial B_{\tau}.

The main result of this subsection is the following,

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
q_{k}
denote the local maximum of
u_{k}-\phi_{R}^{k}
. Then

(3.39)

q_{k}=\left(-\frac{2}{\bar{h}_{k}(0)}\partial_{1}\phi_{R}^{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2},-\frac{2}{\bar{h}_{k}(0)}\partial_{2}\phi_{R}^{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\right)+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}).

Moreover we have,

(3.40)

\int_{B_{\tau}}\bar{h}_{k}(x)e^{u_{k}}dx=8\pi-\frac{8\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}+a_{k}\tau^{2}}-\frac{\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}}{2}\Delta(\log\bar{h}_{k})(0)\bar{h}_{k}(0)b_{0,k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\epsilon_{0}}),

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
, where

b_{0,k}=\int_{0}^{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{r^{3}(1-a_{k}r^{2})}{(1+a_{k}r^{2})^{3}}dr,\quad a_{k}=\bar{h}_{k}(0)/8,

and, for any
\delta>0
small,

(3.41)

\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})+\left(1+\frac{4}{\tau\bar{h}_{k}(0)}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\right)\nabla\phi_{R}^{k}(q_{k})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3-\delta}),

(3.42)

\nabla\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1-\delta}).

Remark 3.2
.

For later purposes, remark that, even if one integrates on a disk centered at
q_{k}
rather than at the maximum of
u_{k}
, whence slightly different from
B_{\tau}
, the difference would be of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4})
. Indeed
|q_{k}|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
and then the symmetric difference of these two disks has an area of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
, while on this set we also have
e^{u_{k}}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
. Thus the integral on the difference of these two disks is of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4})
. As far as (
2.2
) holds true as well, a similar observation applies whenever the integral is centered at the the blow up point rather than at the maximum of
u_{k}
.

Proof.

Set

\tilde{u}_{k}=u_{k}-\phi_{R}^{k},\quad\mbox{in}\quad B_{\tau},

then we cite the estimates in
[
44
,
83
]
in the following form. Let

\tilde{v}_{k}(y)=\tilde{u}_{k}(q_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)+2\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{k},

then

\tilde{v}_{k}(y)=-2\log\left(1+\frac{\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})}{8}|y|^{2}\right)+c_{0}^{k}(y)+O\left(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\right)(1+|y|)^{\delta}

for
|y|\leq\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}
,
\delta>0
small, and

|c_{0}^{k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\frac{|y|^{2}(\log(2+|y|))^{2}}{(1+|y|)^{2}}.

By standard elliptic estimates we have,

(3.43)

\left|\nabla\left(\tilde{v}_{k}+2\log\left(1+\frac{\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})}{8}|y|^{2}\right)-c_{0}^{k}(y)\right)\right|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}},

for
|y|\sim 1
. Note here that the image of
0
after scaling becomes
p_{k}=-\frac{q_{k}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}
, which satisfies (
[
44
,
83
]
)
|p_{k}|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}
. This estimate about
p_{k}
implies that
|\nabla c_{0}^{k}(p_{k})|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}})
.
Evaluating (
3.43
) at
p_{k}
and using the fact that
\nabla u_{k}(0)=0
, it is not difficult to see that,

p_{k}=\left(\frac{2}{\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})}\partial_{1}\phi_{R}^{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k},\frac{2}{\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})}\partial_{2}\phi_{R}^{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\right)+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}),

showing indeed that (
3.39
) holds true.

Now we have

\tilde{u}_{k}=\mbox{constant}
on
\partial B_{\tau}

and (
[
44
,
83
]
)

(3.44)

|\nabla\log\tilde{h}(0)|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\log(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})),

where

(3.45)

\log\tilde{h}_{k}(x)=\log\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k}+x)+\phi_{R}^{k}(q_{k}+x),\quad q_{k}+x\in B_{\tau}.

At this point let us define,

(3.46)

\bar{v}_{k}(y)=\tilde{u}_{k}(q_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)+2\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{k},

then we have,

(3.47)
