\Delta\bar{v}_{k}(y)+\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{k}}=0

in

\Omega_{k}:=\{y;\quad q_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y\in B(0,\tau)\},

and
\bar{v}_{k}=\mbox{constant}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
. Therefore we choose
the first term in the approximation of
\bar{v}_{k}
to be essentially the one suggested in
[
83
]
and then in

[
44
]
, which takes the form,

\bar{U}_{k}(y)=-2\log\left(1+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8}|y|^{2}\right).

Remark 3.3
.

The
\bar{U}_{k}
suggested in
[
83
]
and then in
[
44
]
is slightly different in the fact that
\tilde{h}_{k}
is not evaluated at
q_{k}
but at the maximum point before the subtraction of the harmonic function encoding
the oscillations. However, by using (
3.39
), (
3.44
),
it is easy to see that Theorem 1.1 in
[
44
]
hold with our definition as well.

Since we need to consider round domains, we focus on
B(0,\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})
, that is, we choose as a base domain

B(q_{k},\tau_{1}^{k})\subset B(0,\tau)
in such a way that they are tangent, i.e.

(3.48)

\tau_{1}^{k}=\tau-|q_{k}|.

To add more terms in the approximation of
\bar{v}_{k}
we first write the equation of
\bar{v}_{k}
as follows,

(3.49)

\begin{aligned} \Delta\bar{v}_{k}(y)+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\bar{v}_{k}}=&(\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y))e^{\bar{v}_{k}}\\
=&(\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y))e^{\bar{U}_{k}}\\
&+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta/2}\end{aligned}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{1,k}

where
\delta>0
is an arbitrarily small number,

\Omega_{1,k}=B(0,\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})
, and the size of the error term is estimated via the vanishing rate of the first derivative (
3.44
) and Remark 1.5 in
[
83
]
which gives

(3.50)

\bar{v}_{k}-\bar{U}_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}(\log(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}))^{2}).

Next we shall derive a first estimate of
\bar{v}_{k}
. For this purpose we write

\displaystyle(\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y))e^{\bar{U}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(h_{l,k}(r)\cos(l\theta)+\bar{h}_{l,k}(r)\sin(l\theta)),

where for
l=0,1,...
,

h_{l,k}(r)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}re^{i\theta}))e^{\bar{U}_{k}}\cos(l\theta)d\theta,

\bar{h}_{l,k}(r)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}re^{i\theta}))e^{\bar{U}_{k}}\sin(l\theta)d\theta.

Since
l=1
is of particular importance, we identify the leading terms:

\displaystyle h_{1,k}(r)=-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{1}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)re^{\bar{U}_{k}}-\frac{1}{8}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\partial_{1}\Delta\tilde{h}_{k}(0)r^{3}e^{\bar{U}_{k}}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{5}H_{1,k}(r),

\displaystyle\bar{h}_{1,k}(r)=-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{2}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)re^{\bar{U}_{k}}-\frac{1}{8}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\partial_{2}\Delta\tilde{h}_{k}(0)r^{3}e^{\bar{U}_{k}}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{5}\bar{H}_{1,k}(r),

and

(3.51)

|H_{1,k}(r)|+|\bar{H}_{1,k}(r)|\leq Cr^{5}(1+r)^{-4}.

We set

c_{l,k}=f_{l,k}(r)\cos(l\theta)+\bar{f}_{l,k}(r)\sin(l\theta)
where

f_{l,k}
is the solution of

\left(\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\bar{U}_{k}}-\frac{l^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)f_{l,k}(r)=h_{l,k}(r),\quad 0<r<\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1},

which satisfies
f_{l,k}(0)=\frac{d}{dr}f_{l,k}(0)=0
. The definition of
\bar{f}_{l,k}
is similar.
Before dealing with
l=1
we provide the estimates for all other terms. The
c_{0,k}
solves

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}c_{0,k}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\bar{U}_{k}}c_{0,k}

\displaystyle=(-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\tilde{h}_{k}(0)r^{2}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4}r^{4}))e^{\bar{U}_{k}(r)},\quad 0<r<\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1},

with
c_{0,k}(0)=c^{\prime}_{0,k}(0)=0
, see the expansion right after Remark
3.1
for more details.
A rough estimate of
c_{0,k}
takes the form,

|c_{0,k}(r)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}(\log(1+r))^{2}.

For
l>1
We define
c_{l,k}
similar to the case with singular source.
For
l>1
, two fundamental solutions of the homogeneous equation are (for convenience we assume
\tilde{h}_{k}(0)=8
)

F_{1,l}=\frac{(l+1)r^{l}+(l-1)r^{l+2}}{1+r^{2}},\quad F_{2,l}=\frac{(l+1)r^{2-l}+(l-1)r^{-l}}{1+r^{2}},

and we define
f_{l,k}
as follows,

(3.52)

f_{l,k}(r)=F_{1,l}(r)\int_{r}^{\tau_{1}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}\frac{W_{1}(s)}{W(s)}ds+F_{2,l}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{W_{2}(s)}{W(s)}ds

where

W(s)=\frac{-2l(l^{2}-1)}{s},\quad W_{1}(s)=h_{l,k}(s)F_{2,l}(s),\quad W_{2}(s)=F_{1,l}(s)h_{l,k}(s).

For
l\geq 4
we just use

|h_{l,k}(r)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4}r^{4}(1+r)^{-4}

for some
C>0
independent of
k
and
l
. Then an elementary estimate gives,

(3.53)

|f_{l,k}(r)|\leq\frac{C}{l^{2}}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4}r^{4}(1+r)^{-2},\quad l\geq 4.

For
l=1
two fundamental solutions are

f_{1}(r)=\frac{r}{1+r^{2}},\quad f_{2}(r)=-\frac{1}{2r(1+r^{2})}+\frac{2r\log r}{1+r^{2}}+\frac{r^{3}}{2(1+r^{2})}

and we set

f_{1,k}(r)=f_{1}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{W_{1}(s)}{W(s)}ds+f_{2}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{W_{2}(s)}{W(s)}ds

where

W(s)=\frac{1}{s},\quad W_{1}(s)=-h_{1,k}(s)f_{2}(s),\quad W_{2}(s)=h_{1,k}(s)f_{1}(s).

Then
c_{1,k}
satisfies

c_{1,k}(0)=|\nabla c_{1,k}(0)|=0,\quad|c_{1,k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{r^{2}}{1+r}.

Let

c_{k}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}c_{l,k},

then the estimate (
3.53
) guarantees that
c_{k}
is well defined. Next we set

\phi_{k}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\phi_{l,k}+\phi_{E}^{k},

where the sum eliminates the oscillation of
-c_{k}
while

\phi_{E}^{k}
eliminates the oscillation of

\bar{v}_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{1,k}
respectively.
As far as
l\geq 4
, by (
3.53
) we have,

|\phi_{l,k}(y)|\leq\frac{C}{l^{2}}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4}r^{2},

showing that
\phi_{k}
is well defined. The overall estimate for
\phi_{k}
is

|\phi_{k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}|y|.

Let
w_{k}=\bar{v}_{k}-(\bar{U}_{k}+{c_{k}}+\phi_{k})
, then

\Delta w_{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\xi_{k}}w_{k}=E_{k},\quad|y|\leq\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1},

where
\xi_{k}
comes from the mean value theorem, which satisfies

\xi_{k}(y)\to-2\log(1+|y|^{2})\quad\mbox{in }\,C^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\begin{cases}E_{k}=-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{U}_{k}}(c_{1,k}+\phi_{k})+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta_{0}})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta_{0}/2},\\
\\
w_{k}(0)=0,\quad|\nabla w_{k}(0)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}},\quad w_{k}=\mbox{ constant on }\,\,\Omega_{1,k}.\end{cases}

Now we claim that, for any
\delta>0
small enough,

(3.54)

|w_{k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad y\in\Omega_{1,k}

implying by standard elliptic estimates that,

(3.55)

|\nabla w_{k}(y)|\sim\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}|y|^{\delta-1},\quad 1<|y|<\frac{\tau}{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

The proof of (
3.54
) is similar to the argument used above via the Green representation formula and we sketch it here for reader’s convenience. Argue by contradiction and, setting

\Lambda_{k}=\max_{y\in\bar{\Omega}_{1,k}}\frac{|w_{k}(y)|}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}(1+|y|)^{\delta}},

assume that
\Lambda_{k}\to\infty
.
Next, let
y_{k}
be any maximum point where
\Lambda_{k}
is attained and set

\hat{w}_{k}(y)=\frac{w_{k}(y)}{\Lambda_{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}(1+|y_{k}|)^{\delta}}.

Thus we have that,

|\hat{w}_{k}(y)|\leq\frac{(1+|y|)^{\delta}}{(1+|y_{k}|)^{\delta}},

\Delta\hat{w}_{k}(y)+h_{k}(q_{k})e^{\xi_{k}}\hat{w}_{k}(y)=o(1)\frac{(1+|y|)^{-3}}{(1+|y_{k}|)^{\delta}},\quad y\in\Omega_{1,k},

and
\hat{w}_{k}(0)=0
,
\nabla\hat{w}_{k}(0)=o(1)
, and
\hat{w}_{k}=
constant on
\partial\Omega_{1,k}
. Then it is easy to see that
|y_{k}|\to\infty
and there is no way for

|\hat{w}_{k}(y_{k})|=1
to hold. This contradiction proves (
3.54
).

With the aid of (
3.54
) we refine the expansion of
\bar{v}_{k}
as follows, let

w_{1,k}=\bar{v}_{k}-\bar{U}_{k}.

Then by (
3.54
) we have

\Delta w_{1,k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\bar{U}_{k}}w_{1,k}=(\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\tilde{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y))e^{\bar{U}_{k}}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta/2},

where we used the fact that,

O(|w_{1,k}|^{2})e^{\bar{U}_{k}}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta}.

Now we abuse the notation
c_{k}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}c_{l,k}
by defining it as the projection on
e^{il\theta}
: For
l\geq 1
,

c_{l,k,1}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{v}_{k}(re^{i\theta})\cos l\theta d\theta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}w_{1,k}(re^{il\theta})\cos l\theta d\theta.

c_{l,k,2}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{v}_{k}(re^{i\theta})\sin l\theta d\theta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}w_{1,k}(re^{i\theta})\sin l\theta d\theta.

Since the projection of
e^{\bar{v}_{k}}
on
e^{i\theta}
is particular important, we emphasize that

(3.56)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\bar{U}_{k}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)c_{1,k,1}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{1}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)re^{\bar{U}_{k}}-\frac{1}{8}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\partial_{1}\Delta\tilde{h}_{k}(0)r^{3}e^{\bar{U}_{k}}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{5}H_{1,k}

\displaystyle+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})(1+r)^{-3+\delta},

where
H_{1,k}
satisfies (
3.51
).
Of course we have a corresponding equation for
c_{1,k,2}
. Here we make two important observations. First of all we have
\bar{v}_{k}(0)=|\nabla\bar{v}_{k}(0)|=0
, secondly, we recall that
\bar{v}_{k}
is constant on
\partial\Omega_{k}
. The first observation implies that,

c_{1,k,1}(0)=\frac{d}{dr}c_{1,k,1}(0)=0,\quad c_{1,k,2}(0)=\frac{d}{dr}c_{1,k,2}(0)=0,

Now we employ Lemma
6.1
in the appendix to deduce that,

(3.57)

c_{1,k,1}(\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})=-2\frac{q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}),\quad c_{1,k,2}(\tau_{1}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})=-2\frac{q_{2}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}).

Let
E_{1,k}
be the right hand side of (
3.56
), then we can compare
c_{1,k,1}
with

T_{k}(r):=-f_{1}(r)\int_{0}^{r}E_{1,k}(s)f_{2}(s)sds+f_{2}(r)\int_{0}^{r}f_{1}(s)E_{1,k}(s)sds,

where we recall that two fundamental solutions are

f_{1}(r)=\frac{r}{1+r^{2}},\quad f_{2}(r)=-\frac{1}{2r(1+r^{2})}+\frac{2r\log r}{1+r^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{r^{3}}{1+r^{2}}.

Putting
w(r)=c_{1,k,1}(r)-T_{k}(r)
, we see that
w(r)
satisfies,

\begin{cases}w^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}w^{\prime}(r)+(8e^{\bar{U}_{k}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}})w(r)=0,\quad 0<r<\tau\bar{\epsilon}_{k}^{-1},\\
w(0)=w^{\prime}(0)=0.\end{cases}

Since
w=c_{1}f_{1}+c_{2}f_{2}
, we see that
w\equiv 0
. Therefore

c_{1,k,1}(r)=-f_{1}(r)\int_{0}^{r}E_{1,k}(s)f_{2}(s)sds+f_{2}(r)\int_{0}^{r}f_{1}(s)E_{1,k}(s)sds.

By an elementary evaluation we have that,

(3.58)

\displaystyle c_{1,k,1}(r)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{1}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)r+O(r^{-1}\log r)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{1}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}\partial_{1}\Delta\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}(\frac{r}{2}\log r-\frac{5}{8}r)+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{5}C_{H}(r),

where

(3.59)

C_{H}(r)=-f_{1}(r)\int_{0}^{r}f_{2}(s)H_{1,k}(s)sds+f_{2}(r)\int_{0}^{r}sf_{1}(s)H_{1,k}(s)ds

and
C_{H}(r)
satisfies

|C_{H}(r)|\leq Cr^{3},\quad\mbox{for some }C\mbox{ independent by }k.

Thus we deduce from (
3.57
) that,

(3.60)

-\frac{\tau}{8}\partial_{1}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\frac{\tau}{16}\partial_{1}\Delta\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}-\frac{5}{4})+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{5}C_{H}(\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}).

Obviously for
c_{1,k,2}
defined by

(3.61)

c_{1,k,2}(r)=-f_{1}(r)\int_{0}^{r}f_{2}(s)\bar{H}_{1,k}(s)sds+f_{2}(r)\int_{0}^{r}sf_{1}(s)\bar{H}_{1,k}(s)ds

we have

(3.62)

-\frac{\tau}{8}\partial_{2}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)-\frac{\tau}{16}\partial_{2}\Delta\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}-\frac{5}{4})+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{5}\bar{C}_{H}(\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}).

The analysis for any
c_{l,k}
can be carried out similarly. By the uniqueness the expression of
c_{l,k}
can be also be written by (
3.52
), which gives

\nabla c_{l,k}(0)=0,\quad|c_{l,k}|\leq\frac{C}{l^{2}}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{l}r^{4}(1+r)^{-2},\quad l\geq 4.

The harmonic function we use to eliminate the oscillation of
\bar{v}_{k}
is
\phi_{E}^{k}
, by Lemma
6.1
we know that the leading term of
\phi_{E}^{k}
is

(3.63)

\phi_{2}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y):=-2\frac{q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}r\cos\theta-2\frac{q_{2}^{k}}{\tau}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}r\sin\theta+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})|y|,

In other words,
\phi_{E}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\cdot)
is orthogonal to
e^{i\theta}
.

Here we describe the location of the maximum point of

\hat{v}_{k}(y):=\bar{v}_{k}(y)-\phi_{E}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y).

In order to do this we first remark that now we have

(3.64)

\bar{v}_{k}(y)=\bar{U}_{k}+c_{k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})(1+|y|)^{\delta},

because, after incorporating the boundary information of
\bar{v}_{k}
on
\Omega_{1,k}
and using the uniqueness of the ordinary differential equations that
c_{l,k}
satisfies, we see that there is no oscillation of
\bar{v}_{k}-\bar{U}_{k}-c_{k}
. Indeed, going back to (
3.49
) and using
\tilde{w}_{k}
to denote
\bar{v}_{k}-\bar{U}_{k}-c_{k}
, we see that
\tilde{w}_{k}
satisfies

\Delta\tilde{w}_{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{\xi_{k}}\tilde{w}_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})(1+|y|)^{-2-\delta/2},

in
\Omega_{1,k}
,
\tilde{w}_{k}(0)=|\nabla\tilde{w}_{k}(0)|=0
and
\tilde{w}_{k}=
constant on
\partial\Omega_{1,k}
. Thus it is easy to see that

\tilde{w}_{k}(y)=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta/2})(1+|y|)^{\delta/2}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{1,k},

which is equivalent to (
3.64
).

Using the expression of
\bar{U}_{k}
and the estimates of
c_{k}
we see that
D^{2}(\bar{U}_{k}+c_{k})(0)\leq-CI
for some
C>0
, where
I
is the identity matrix. Thus the maximum point
\hat{p}_{k}
of
\hat{v}_{k}
satisfies,

|\hat{p}_{k}|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}.

If we use
\hat{q}_{k}
to denote the pre-image of
\hat{p}_{k}
before scaling, we have
|\hat{q}_{k}|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}})
.

After obtaining this estimate about
|\hat{p}_{k}|
, we will only use the form of
c_{1,k}
. Instead we remove the oscillation of
\bar{v}_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{1,k}
and define

\hat{v}_{k}(y)=v_{k}(\hat{p}_{k}+y)-\phi_{E}^{k}(\hat{p}_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y),\quad\hat{p}_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y\in\Omega_{1,k}

(3.65)

\log\hat{h}_{k}(y)=\log\tilde{h}_{k}(y)+\phi_{2}^{k}(y)+(\phi_{E}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})(y).

The reason we write
\phi_{E}^{k}=\phi_{2}^{k}+(\phi_{E}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})
is because when we calculate the first derivative of
\log\hat{h}_{k}(0)
, we have
\nabla(\phi_{E}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})(0)=0
and
\phi_{2}^{k}
is the most important term of
\phi_{E}^{k}
.
Now we define
\hat{v}_{k}
on
\Omega_{2,k}:=B(0,\tau_{2}^{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})
,
where
B(\hat{q}_{k},\tau_{2}^{k})\subset B(q_{k},\tau_{1}^{k})
is such that they are tangent.

Using Lemma
6.1
we see that the oscillation on
\partial\Omega_{2}^{k}
is now
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3})
. Now we write the equation of
\hat{v}_{k}
as

\Delta\hat{v}_{k}+\hat{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\hat{v}_{k}}=0,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{2,k}.

Corresponding to this setting we define
\hat{U}_{k}
,
\hat{c}_{k}
by analogy as above and we see that there is no oscillation of
\hat{v}_{k}-\hat{c}_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{2,k}
. Thus, by arguing as in (
3.54
),(
3.55
), we have,

(3.66)

|\nabla^{j}(\hat{v}_{k}-\hat{U}_{k}-\hat{c}_{k})|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta}(1+|y|)^{\delta-j}

for
j=0,1
and
|y|\leq\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}/2
.

We are now in a position to obtain the vanishing rates of
|\nabla\hat{h}_{k}(0)|
and
|\nabla\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)|
. Let
\Omega_{\sigma,k}=B(0,\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-\sigma})
. Then we consider the following Pohozaev identity on
\Omega_{\sigma,k}
,
for any fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
:

(3.67)

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\int_{\Omega_{\sigma,k}}\partial_{\xi}\hat{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\hat{v}_{k}}=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega_{\sigma,k}}(\hat{h}_{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\hat{v}_{k}}(\xi,\nu)+\partial_{\nu}\hat{v}_{k}\partial_{\xi}\hat{v}_{k})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial\Omega_{\sigma,k}}|\nabla\hat{v}_{k}|^{2}(\xi\cdot\nu).

To evaluate the left hand side, after using the expansion (
3.66
) and the current (
[
44
,
83
]
) vanishing rate of
|\nabla\hat{h}_{k}(0)|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\log{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-1})
, and taking
\sigma
close to
\frac{1}{2}
, we have

LHS=8\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{\xi}\log\hat{h}_{k}(0)+\frac{16\pi}{\hat{h}_{k}^{2}(0)}\partial_{\xi}\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}(\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-2\sigma}+O(1))+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta}).

Note that if use
H_{\xi}^{k}
to denote all the high order terms in the expansion of
\partial_{\xi}\hat{h}_{k}
:

\partial_{\xi}\hat{h}_{k}(x)=\partial_{\xi}\hat{h}_{k}(0)+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{|\alpha|=j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial^{\alpha}\hat{h}_{k}(0)x^{\alpha}+H_{\xi}^{k}(x),

we can choose
\sigma
close to
\frac{1}{2}
so the integration with
H_{\xi}^{k}
is minor compared to the error
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})
.
Using the expansion of
v_{k}
to evaluate the right hand side with
\sigma
close to
\frac{1}{2}
, we have

RHS=C_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\partial_{\xi}\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)+C_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{\xi}\hat{h}_{k}(0)+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta}).

Here we note that in the evaluation of the right hand side, the first term
\int_{\partial\Omega_{\sigma,k}}\hat{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}(\xi\cdot\nu)
gives

\int_{\partial\Omega_{\sigma,k}}\hat{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}(\xi\cdot\nu)=C_{3}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\partial_{\xi}\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta}),

while in the last two boundary terms, because of cancellations, the only term that needs to be evaluated is

\int_{\partial\Omega_{\sigma,k}}\bar{U}_{k}^{\prime}(r)\partial_{\xi}c_{1,k}.

Thus the combination of both sides gives

(3.68)

8\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\partial_{\xi}\log\hat{h}_{k}(0)+\frac{16\pi}{\hat{h}_{k}^{2}(0)}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3}\partial_{\xi}\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)(\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-2\sigma}+O(1))=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta}).

Therefore by choosing
\sigma=\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
with both of them close to
\frac{1}{2}
(without affecting the error term
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\delta})
, we have

|(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})\partial_{\xi}\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1-\delta})

for
\delta>0
arbitrarily small.
Since
\xi
is any vector on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we have

(3.69)

\nabla\Delta\hat{h}_{k}(0)=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1-\delta}),

which immediately implies that

(3.70)

\nabla\hat{h}_{k}(0)=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3-\delta}),

by the Pohozaev identity (
3.68
).
Tracking the definition of
\hat{h}_{k}
back to (
3.65
) and (
3.45
), we see that (
3.70
) is equivalent to

\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})+\nabla\phi_{R}^{k}(q_{k})(1+\frac{4}{\tau\bar{h}_{k}(0)}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3-\delta}).

Obviously Theorem
3.3
follows from direct evaluation. Remark that even if one integrates on a disk slightly different from
B_{\tau}
, but because
|q_{k}|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
, the difference on these two disks has an area of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
and on this difference
e^{u_{k}}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
. So the integral on the difference of two circles is
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4})
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

As an additional piece of information, after using the new rates in (
3.58
), (
3.59
) and (
3.57
), we can obtain more vanishing estimates on higher derivatives from (
3.62
) if

h_{k}
is assumed to have more regularity.

Remark 3.5
.

With the improved vanishing rates of
\nabla\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})
and
\nabla\Delta\bar{h}_{k}(q_{k})
at hand, it is useful to state here the following estimates of independent interest about the blow up at regular points:

|\bar{v}_{k}(y)-(\bar{U}_{k}+c_{0,k}+c_{2,k})(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{2\epsilon_{0}}\quad\mbox{{\rm in}}\quad\Omega_{k},

and

|\nabla(\bar{v}_{k}-\bar{U}_{k}-c_{0,k}-c_{2,k})(y)|\leq C\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3},\quad y\in\Omega_{k},\quad|y|\sim\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
.
By contradiction let
\nu_{1}^{k}
and
\nu_{2}^{k}
be two distinct blowup sequences of (
1.1
)
corresponding to the same
\rho_{k}
and the same set of blowup points. The case we consider in this context is
\alpha_{M}>0
.
To simplify the exposition, we assume that there exist just three blowup points:
q
,
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, where
q
is a regular blowup point,
p_{1}
is a positive singular source with
\alpha\equiv\alpha_{M}>0
,
p_{2}
is a negative singular source with
\beta\in(-1,0)
.

Let

{\rm w}_{i}^{k}=\nu_{i}^{k}+4\pi\alpha G(x,p_{1})+4\pi\beta G(x,p_{2}),

then we have

(4.1)

\Delta_{g}{\rm w}_{i}^{k}+\rho_{k}\left(\frac{He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}}{\int_{M}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}}-1\right)=0,

where

H(x)=h(x)e^{-4\pi\alpha G(x,p_{1})-4\pi\beta G(x,p_{2})}.

Since by adding any constant to
{\rm w}_{i}^{k}
in (
4.1
) we still come up with a solution, we fix this free constant to make

{\rm w}_{i}^{k}
satisfy

\int_{M}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}dV_{g}=1,\quad i=1,2.

The basic assumption is that

\sigma_{k}:=\|{\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}>0.

From previous works such as
[
10
]
,
[
81
]
we have that
\rho_{1}^{k}=\rho_{2}^{k}=\rho_{k}

implies that
\sigma_{k}=O(e^{-\gamma\lambda_{1}^{k}})
for some
\gamma>0
where we set
\lambda_{1}^{k}={\rm w}_{1}^{k}(p_{1})
and
\lambda_{2}^{k}={\rm w}_{2}^{k}(p_{1})
.
Denoting by
\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{k}={\rm w}_{i}^{k}(p_{2})
and
\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{i}=\max\limits_{B(q,\tau)}{\rm w}_{i}^{k}
, it is well known that

(4.2)

\hat{\lambda}^{k}_{i}-\lambda^{k}_{i}=O(1),\quad\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{i}-\lambda^{k}_{i}=O(1),\quad i=1,2.

We work in local isothermal coordinates around
p_{1},p_{2},q
, the metric locally taking the form,

ds^{2}=e^{\phi}((dx_{1})^{2}+(dx_{2})^{2}),

where
\phi
satisfies,

(4.3)

\Delta\phi+2Ke^{\phi}=0\quad{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau},\quad\phi(0)=|\nabla\phi(0)|=0.

In particular we choose local coordinates so that
p_{i}
,
q
are locally the origin of a ball
x\in B_{\tau}=B_{\tau}(0)
.
Remark that in our setting we have,

(4.4)

\begin{cases}\rho^{*}=24\pi+8\pi\alpha+8\pi\beta,\quad N^{*}=4\pi(\alpha+\beta),\\
\\
G^{*}_{1}(x)=8\pi(1+\alpha)R(x,p_{1})+8\pi(1+\beta)G(x,p_{2})+8\pi G(x,q).\end{cases}

Remark 4.1
.

A word of warning about notations.
B_{\tau}=B_{\tau}(0)
or either
B_{\tau}(\bar{q})
will denote the ball centered at the origin
of some local isothermal coordinates
x\in B_{\tau}
, while
B(p,\tau)\subset M

denotes a geodesic ball. Also, if
B_{\tau}
is the a ball in local isothermal coordinates centered at some point
0=x(p),p\in M
,
we will denote by
\Omega(p,\tau)\subset M

the pre-image of
B_{\tau}
. Of course we can always choose
\tau
small enough to guarantee that

\Omega(p_{i},\tau)
,
i=1,2
and
\Omega(q,\tau)
are simply connected and at
positive distance one from each other. After scaling,

x=\varepsilon y
for some
\varepsilon>0
, we will denote

B_{\tau/\varepsilon}=B_{\tau/\varepsilon}(0)
.

To avoid technicalities, we will some time use the same symbols,
say
w_{k}
,
\xi_{k}
,
\tilde{h}_{k}
, to denote functions when expressed in different local coordinates systems. Of course, it will be
clear time to time by the context what the given symbol means.

Thus, working in these local coordinates centered at
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
q
respectively, we have the local variables
x\in B_{\tau}
and we define
f_{k}
to be any solution of,

(4.5)

\Delta f_{k}=e^{\phi}\rho_{k}\quad{{\rm in}}\quad B_{\tau},\quad f_{k}(0)=0,

so that the local equation around

p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
q
, for

(4.6)

u_{i}^{k}={\rm w}_{i}^{k}-f_{k}\quad{{\rm in}}\quad B_{\tau}

takes the form,

(4.7)

\begin{cases}\Delta u_{i}^{k}+|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{u_{i}^{k}}=0\quad{{\rm in}}\quad B_{\tau},\quad i=1,2,\qquad 0=x(p_{1}),\\
\\
\tilde{h}_{k}=\rho_{k}h(x)e^{-4\pi\alpha R(x,p_{1})-4\pi\beta G(x,p_{2})+\phi+f_{k}},\end{cases}

(4.8)

\begin{cases}\Delta u_{i}^{k}+|x|^{2\beta}\hat{h}_{k}e^{u_{i}^{k}}=0\quad{{\rm in}}\quad B_{\tau},\quad i=1,2,\qquad 0=x(p_{2})\\
\\
\hat{h}_{k}=\rho_{k}h(x)e^{-4\pi\alpha G(x,p_{1})-4\pi\beta R(x,p_{2})+\phi+f_{k}},\end{cases}

(4.9)

\begin{cases}\Delta u_{i}^{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}e^{u_{i}^{k}}=0\quad{{\rm in}}\quad B_{\tau},\quad i=1,2,\qquad 0=x(q),\\
\\
\bar{h}_{k}=\rho_{k}h(x)e^{-4\pi\alpha G(x,p_{1})-4\pi\beta G(x,p_{2})+\phi+f_{k}}.\end{cases}

Obviously
\tilde{h}_{k},\hat{h}_{k},\bar{h}_{k}
satisfy (
3.1
). Also from this setting we see that (
2.4
) holds. Indeed from

\rho_{k}=\rho_{k}\int_{M}He^{w_{i}^{k}}
we evaluate the integrals around blowup points and the integration outside the bubbling disks is of the order
O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})
.

Remark 4.2
.

Concerning (
4.9
), for technical reasons, it will be useful to define
\bar{q}_{i}^{k}\to 0
to be the maximum points of
u_{i}^{k}
in
B_{\tau}
and work
in the local coordinate system centered at
\bar{q}_{i}^{k}
. In particular
q_{i}^{k}\in M
will denote the pre-images of these points via the local isothermal map and we will work sometime, possibly taking a smaller
\tau
, with (
4.9
) where
0=x(q^{k}_{i})
.

By using also (
4.3
), (
4.5
), we see that the non-degeneracy assumption
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
takes the form

\Delta_{g}\log h(p_{1})+\rho_{*}-N^{*}-2K(p_{1})\neq 0,

which, for any
k
large enough, is equivalent to (see (
4.4
))

\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\neq 0.

Clearly around
p_{1}
we have,

u_{i}^{k}=\log\frac{e^{\lambda_{1}^{k}}}{(1+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}e^{\lambda_{1}^{k}}|x|^{2\alpha+2})^{2}}+\phi_{i}^{k}+\mbox{h.o.t.}

where h.o.t stands for higher order terms and
\phi_{i}^{k}
is the harmonic function that encodes the oscillation of
u_{i}^{k}
on
\partial B_{\tau}
.
At this point let us define,

\xi_{k}:=({\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k})/\sigma_{k}.

Remark that, because of (
4.6
), locally we have

(4.10)

u_{1}^{k}-u_{2}^{k}\equiv{\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}

and in particular in local coordinates
\xi_{k}\equiv(u_{1}^{k}-u_{2}^{k})/\sigma_{k}
. Then it is well known (see
[
10
,
11
,
81
]
) that, after a suitable scaling in local coordinates as in section
4.2
below, the limit of
\xi_{k}
takes the form,

(4.11)

b_{0}\frac{1-A|y|^{2\alpha+2}}{1+A|y|^{2\alpha+2}}~\mbox{\rm near }p_{1},\quad A=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}};

(4.12)

b_{0}\frac{1-B|y|^{2\beta+2}}{1+B|y|^{2\beta+2}}~\mbox{\rm near }p_{2},\quad B=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\hat{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\beta)^{2}};

(4.13)

b_{0}\frac{1-C|y|^{2}}{1+C|y|^{2}}+b_{1}\frac{y_{1}}{1+C|y|^{2}}+b_{2}\frac{y_{2}}{1+C|y|^{2}}~\mbox{\rm near }q,\quad C=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\bar{h}_{k}(0)}{8}.

Remark 4.3
.

The reason why we find exactly the same
b_{0}
in
the three formulas is that
\xi_{k}
converges to a constant far away from blow up points,
and it is by now well known (see
[
10
,
11
,
81
]
)
that this constant is exactly
-b_{0}
.

4.1.
First estimates about
\sigma_{k}
.

As mentioned above we set,

\lambda_{i}^{k}={\rm w}_{i}^{k}(p_{1})\equiv u_{i}^{k}(p_{1})+f_{k},\;i=1,2\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\varepsilon^{2}_{k}=e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{1+\alpha}},

and recall from (
2.3
) that
\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{\gamma})
for some
\gamma>0
.
We derive first a rough estimate for
{\rm w}_{1}^{k}
and
{\rm w}_{2}^{k}
far away from blowup points:

Lemma 4.1
.

For
x\in M\setminus(\Omega(p_{1},\tau)\cup\Omega(p_{2},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau))
,

(4.14)

\begin{cases}{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)=\overline{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}+8\pi(1+\alpha)G(x,p_{1})+8\pi(1+\beta)G(x,p_{2})\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad+8\pi G(x,q_{1}^{k})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),\\
\\
{\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x)=\overline{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}}+8\pi(1+\alpha)G(x,p_{1})+8\pi(1+\beta)G(x,p_{2})\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad+8\pi G(x,q_{2}^{k})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),\end{cases}

where
q_{i}^{k}
is defined in Remark
4.2
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

From the Green representation formula (recall that
\int_{M}G(x,\eta)=0
) we have,

\displaystyle{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)-\overline{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}=\int_{M}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\bigg(G(x,p_{i})\int_{\Omega({p_{i}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}+\int_{\Omega({p_{i}},\tau/2)}(G(x,\eta)-G(x,p_{i}))\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+G(x,q_{1}^{k})\int_{\Omega(q_{1}^{k},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}+\int_{\Omega(q_{1}^{k},\tau/2)}(G(x,\eta)-G(x,q_{1}^{k}))\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau/2)\cup\Omega(q_{1}^{k},\tau/2)\}}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}.

It immediately follows from the estimates in section
2
that the last
term is
O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})=o(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
, as far as
\alpha_{M}>0
.
By using Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
, we have

\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}=\int_{B_{\tau/2}}|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{u_{1}^{k}}=8\pi(1+\alpha)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

\int_{\Omega({p_{2}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}=\int_{B_{\tau/2}}|x|^{2\beta}\hat{h}_{k}e^{u_{1}^{k}}=8\pi(1+\beta)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

and

\int_{\Omega(q_{1}^{k},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}=\int_{B_{\tau/2}}\bar{h}_{k}e^{u_{1}^{k}}=8\pi+O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})=8\pi+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

where in the last integral we also used (
4.2
) and (
2.2
), that is, in local coordinates
where
\bar{q}_{1}^{k}=x(q_{1}^{k})
,
\bar{q}:=0=x(q)
, we have

\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}=\bar{q}_{1}^{k}=O(e^{-\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{1}}\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{1})
. We sketch the argument behind the
estimates of the other terms.
When evaluating

\int_{\Omega({p_{i}},\tau/2)}(G(x,\eta)-G(x,p_{i}))\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}
, working in local coordinates
y(\eta)
,
we use the Taylor expansion of
G(x,\eta_{y})
with respect to
y
around
0=y(p_{i})
and the
expansion of
u^{k}_{1}
in Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
. Then it is easy to see that the

O(\varepsilon_{k})
term cancels out due to the integration on the symmetric set
B_{\tau/2}

and the remaining terms are of order at least

O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
. In the integration of
\int_{\Omega(q_{1}^{k},\tau/2)}(G(x,\eta)-G(x,q_{1}^{k}))\rho_{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}
,
we adopt the same argument with minor changes, this time based on Theorem 1.2 in
[
83
]
,
which yields as well to an error of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
.
This fact concludes the
{\rm w}_{1}
part of the proof of (
4.14
), the part for
{\rm w}_{2}
is worked out in the same way.

In particular, by using once more Theorem 1.2 in
[
83
]
, we deduce that

{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)-\overline{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-({\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x)-\overline{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),~x\in M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau)\},

and, say for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\partial\Omega(q,\tau)
,

{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x_{1})-{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x_{2})={\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x_{1})-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x_{2})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}).

Lemma
4.1
is established. ∎

Remark 4.4
.

Lemma
4.1
shows in particular that, around each blow up point,
the difference of the harmonic functions encoding the oscillation on the boundary
is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
.

Here and in the rest of the proof we denote by,

c_{k}(x)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)}-e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x)}}{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x)},\quad&\mbox{if}\quad{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)\neq{\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x),\\
\\
e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)},\quad&\mbox{if }\quad{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)={\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x),\end{cases}

and use the local coordinates sequences as defined in (
4.6
),

\tilde{c}_{k}(x)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{e^{u_{1}^{k}(x)}-e^{u_{2}^{k}(x)}}{u_{1}^{k}(x)-u_{2}^{k}(x)},\quad&\mbox{if}\quad u_{1}^{k}(x)\neq u_{2}^{k}(x),\\
\\
e^{u_{1}^{k}(x)},\quad&\mbox{if }\quad u_{1}^{k}(x)=u_{2}^{k}(x),\end{cases}

where (see (
4.5
)),

(4.15)

c_{k}(x)=e^{f_{k}(x)}\tilde{c}_{k}(x).

We first prove that
b_{0}=0
. By contradiction assume that
b_{0}\neq 0
, then we claim that,

Lemma 4.2
.

\sigma_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

We use in a crucial way the fact that, as far as
b_{0}\neq 0
, then

(4.16)

\frac{b_{0}}{2}({\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}})\leq\sigma_{k}\leq({\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}})2b_{0},

for any
k
large enough, see (
4.19
) below.
This being said, the proof relies on the assumption that

\rho_{1}^{k}=\rho_{2}^{k}=\rho_{k}
, which we write as follows,

\displaystyle\rho_{i}^{k}=\int_{M}\rho_{i}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)}\rho_{i}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}+\int_{\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)}\rho_{i}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}+\int_{\Omega({q},\tau)}\rho_{i}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}

\displaystyle+\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\}}\rho_{i}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{i}^{k}}

for
i=1,2
. First of all we have,

\displaystyle\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\}}(\rho_{1}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-\rho_{2}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})/\sigma_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\}}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(x)\xi_{k}.

Since, far away from blowup points,
\xi_{k}\to-b_{0}
and
c_{k}(x)\sim e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}
, we have that,

\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\}}(\rho_{1}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-\rho_{2}^{k}He^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})=O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})\sigma_{k}.

Now we focus on the comparison of the integrals on
\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)
,
\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)
and

\Omega({q},\tau)
.
We first use Theorem
3.1
to evaluate

\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)}\rho_{k}H(e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}}).

Here
\tilde{h}_{k}
is the weight function defined in (
4.7
)
relative to
u_{1}^{k}={\rm w}_{1}^{k}-f_{k}
and
u_{2}^{k}={\rm w}_{2}^{k}-f_{k}
in
\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)
.
After applying Theorem
3.1
to
{\rm w}_{1}^{k}
and
{\rm w}_{2}^{k}
, respectively and looking at their difference, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)}\rho_{k}H(e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})=d_{1,k}\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/(1+\alpha)}(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}),

where
d_{1,k}\to d_{1}<0
. As remarked above
L(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
is equivalent to

\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\neq 0
, implying that in fact

d_{1,k}\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}
is the leading term.
By using Theorem
3.2
for
\int_{\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)}\rho_{k}H(e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})
we have,

\int_{\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)}\rho_{k}H(e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})=o(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}).

At last, for the integration on
\Omega({q},\tau)
, we use Theorem
3.3
to deduce that,

\int_{\Omega({q},\tau)}\rho_{k}H(e^{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}}-e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}})=o(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\sigma}),

for some small
\sigma>0
.
In this case we note that, since
\alpha>0
, then by possibly choosing a
smaller
\epsilon_{0}>0
, we can guarantee that

O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4-\sigma})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}})
. Therefore we finally deduce that,

0=(d_{1,k}\Delta\log h_{k}(0)e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/(1+\alpha)}+o(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}))(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{4+\epsilon_{0}}),

which immediately implies
\sigma_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
. Lemma
4.2
is proved.
∎

The following subsection is devoted to the completion of the proof of
b_{0}=0
, which is rather long and non trivial.

4.2.
Proof of
b_{0}=0
: improved estimates about
\xi_{k}
.

The smallness of
\sigma_{k}
is crucial for us to obtain a better expansion of
\xi_{k}
.
Recall that

\xi_{k}(x)=\dfrac{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}(x)-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}(x)}{\parallel{\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}\parallel_{L^{\infty}(M)}},

and observe that, in view of Lemma
4.2
,
c_{k}(x)
can be written as follows,

(4.17)

\displaystyle c_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}e^{t{\rm w}_{1}^{k}+(1-t){\rm w}_{2}^{k}}dt=e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}}(1+\frac{1}{2}({\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k})+O(\sigma_{k}^{2})),

\displaystyle=e^{{\rm w}_{2}^{k}}(1+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})),

which in view of (
4.15
) obviously holds for
\tilde{c}_{k}
as well.
Therefore, in local coordinates around
p_{1}
, so that
0=x(p_{1})
,
x\in B_{\tau}=B_{\tau}(0)
,
\xi_{k}
satisfies,

\Delta\xi_{k}(x)+|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(x)\tilde{c}_{k}(x)\xi_{k}(x)=0\quad{{\rm in}}\quad B_{\tau},

where
\tilde{h}_{k}
is defined in (
4.7
). In particular recall that
the assumption
L(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
is equivalent to
\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\neq 0
for
k
large
enough and that we set
\lambda_{i}^{k}={\rm w}_{i}^{k}(p_{1})\quad i=1,2
. Next,
let
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
be the harmonic
function which encodes the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
on

\partial B_{\tau}
,

\displaystyle\Delta\psi_{\xi}^{k}=0~\mbox{in}~B_{\tau},\quad\psi_{\xi}^{k}(x)=\xi_{k}(x)-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int_{\partial B_{\tau}}\xi_{k}(s)ds~\mathrm{for}~x\in\partial B_{\tau},

and
\phi_{i}^{k}
,
i=1,2
the harmonic functions which encode the oscillations of
u_{i}^{k}
,
i=1,2
on

\partial B_{\tau}
. At this stage, since
\xi_{k}
converges to
-b_{0}
far away from blow up points,
we can just claim that the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
on
\partial B_{\tau}
is of order
o(1)
.
We shall derive first a refined estimate about
\xi_{k}
around
p_{1}
.

First of all observe that, since locally
around
p_{1}
the rescaled function
\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
(recall
0=x(p_{1})
) converges on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}

to a solution of the following linearized equation (
[
10
,
11
,
81
]
),

\Delta\xi+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}(0)e^{U}\xi=0,\quad\tilde{h}(0)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\tilde{h}_{k}(0),

where (see (
3.6
))
U
is the limit of the standard bubble
U_{2}^{k}
, then (see Lemma
2.1
) we have that,

(4.18)

\xi(y)=b_{0}\frac{1-c|y|^{2\alpha+2}}{1+c|y|^{2\alpha+2}},\quad c=\frac{\tilde{h}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}.

In particular the local convergence of
\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
to
\xi(y)
readily implies that,

(4.19)

\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}}{\|{\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}\|_{\infty}}\to b_{0}.

At this point we can use (
4.19
) and the expansions of
u_{1}^{k}
and
u_{2}^{k}
to identify the
leading order of the radial part of
\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
. Let us recall that by definition

v_{i}^{k}
is the scaling of
u_{i}^{k}
:

v_{i}^{k}(y)=u_{i}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\phi_{i}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\lambda_{i}^{k},\quad i=1,2,

where
\varepsilon_{k}=e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{2(1+\alpha)}}
.
Thus, by using
U_{1}^{k}
and
U_{2}^{k}
to denote the leading terms of
v_{1}^{k}
and
v_{2}^{k}
, respectively, we have,

U_{1}^{k}-U_{2}^{k}=\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}+2\log\frac{1+a_{k}e^{\lambda_{2}^{k}-\lambda_{1}^{k}}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}},\quad a_{k}=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)/8(1+\alpha)^{2}.

Therefore, in view of (
2.3
), we have,

\displaystyle U_{1}^{k}(y)-U_{2}^{k}(y)=~

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}+2\log(1+\frac{a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}(e^{\lambda_{2}^{k}-\lambda_{1}^{k}}-1)}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}+2\log(1+\frac{a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}(\lambda_{2}^{k}-\lambda_{1}^{k}+O(|\lambda_{2}^{k}-\lambda_{1}^{k}|^{2})}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}+2\frac{a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}(\lambda_{2}^{k}-\lambda_{1}^{k})}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}+\frac{O(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})^{2}}{(1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})\frac{1-a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}+\frac{O(\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k})^{2}}{(1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2})^{2}}.

This fact suggests that the leading order of the radial part
of
(v_{1}^{k}-v_{2}^{k})/\|u_{1}^{k}-u_{2}^{k}\|_{\infty}
converges to
\xi
,
which is a radial function as well. This is why, as a first approximation, we choose the following radial
function,

(4.20)

w_{0,\xi}^{k}=b_{0}^{k}\frac{1-a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2\alpha+2}},

which is a solution of

(4.21)

\Delta w_{0,\xi}^{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U^{k}_{2}}w_{0,\xi}^{k}=0,

where
b_{0}^{k}
is chosen to ensure that
w_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=\xi_{k}(0)
.
Remark that the equation for
\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k}
takes the form,

(4.22)

\Delta(\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k})+|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}(\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k})=-|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}\psi_{\xi}^{k}.

Our next goal is to obtain an expansion of
(\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k})(\varepsilon_{k}y)
whose leading
term is in fact
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
. This is why we write (
4.21
) as follows,

(4.23)

\displaystyle\Delta w_{0,\xi}^{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)w_{0,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\bigg(\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bigg)w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y).

At this point we consider the equation for

(4.24)

\tilde{w}_{k}(y):=\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y).

From (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) we have

(4.25)

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{w}_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\tilde{w}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}\bigg(e^{U_{2}^{k}}-\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\bigg)w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)

\displaystyle-|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y),

where in particular we also know that

\tilde{w}_{k}(0)=0,\quad\tilde{w}_{k}=\mbox{constant}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}.

Remark that the last term of (
4.25
) is of order
o(\varepsilon_{k})(1+r)^{-3-2\alpha}
and
consequently, by Remark
3.1
, we obtain a first estimate about
\tilde{w}_{k}
:

(4.26)

|\tilde{w}_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\varepsilon_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta}\quad\mbox{in}\quad B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}.

The analysis around
p_{2}
is similar, we define (recall that now we have local coordinates where
0=x(p_{2})
),

(4.27)

\hat{w}_{k}(y)=\xi_{k}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\hat{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\hat{w}^{k}_{0,\xi}(y),

where
\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\frac{\hat{\lambda}_{k}}{2(1+\beta)}}
,
\hat{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}
is the harmonic
function that encodes the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
on
\partial B_{\tau}
,
\hat{w}^{k}_{0,\xi}
is the same as

w^{k}_{0,\xi}
just with
\beta
replacing
\alpha
and the local limit of

\xi_{k}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
is now (
4.12
) which replaces (
4.18
).
By using the same argument as that adopted above for
\tilde{w}_{k}
, we have that,

(4.28)

|\hat{w}_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\beta}(1+|y|)^{\delta}=C(\delta)e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad\mbox{in}\quad B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}.

Next we describe the expansion of
\xi_{k}
near
q
. In this case (
4.18
) is replaced by (
4.13
). Recall that we denote
\bar{q}_{i}^{k}=x(q_{i}^{k})
,
i=1,2
the expressions in local isothermal
coordinates
0=x(q)
of the maximizers near
q
of
u_{i}^{k}
,
i=1,2
.
From (
2.2
) we have

|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|=O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}\lambda_{1}^{k})
. In
\Omega(q,\tau)
we denote by

\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}/2}
the scaling factor
where
\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}
and
\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}
are the maximums near
q
. With an abuse of notations
we denote by
\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}

the harmonic function that, in local isothermal
coordinates
0=x(q)
,
encodes the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
on
B_{\tau}
.
Of course, since we assume
b_{0}\neq 0
, by analogy with (
4.19
)
we still have that
|\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}|\sim\sigma_{k}
.

Now we work in more detail about the asymptotic expansion of
\xi_{k}

around
q
and use local coordinates
such that
0=x(q)
. As usual the kernel functions are the first terms in the approximation of

\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
,

\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)=\bar{b}_{0}^{k}\frac{1-\frac{\bar{h}_{2}^{k}(0)}{8}|y|^{2}}{1+\frac{\bar{h}_{2}^{k}(0)}{8}|y|^{2}}+\bar{b}_{1}^{k}\frac{y_{1}}{1+\frac{\bar{h}_{2}^{k}(0)}{8}|y|^{2}}+\bar{b}_{2}^{k}\frac{y_{2}}{1+\frac{\bar{h}_{2}^{k}(0)}{8}|y|^{2}}.

Then we have

\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nabla(\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y))|_{y=0}=0.

Next we set
\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}
to be the harmonic function which encodes the oscillation of
\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
on
\partial B_{\tau/\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}
. Let us define

(4.29)

\bar{w}_{k}(y)=\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)-\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y),

and we have

(4.30)

\begin{cases}\Delta\bar{w}_{k}(y)+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}(\tilde{h}_{k}\tilde{c}_{k})(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)\bar{w}_{k}(y)=\frac{O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})}{(1+|y|)^{3}}~\mbox{in}~B_{\tau/\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}},\\
\bar{w}_{k}(0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bar{w}_{k}={\rm constant}~\mbox{on}~\partial B_{\tau/\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}.\end{cases}

It is not difficult to check that
\bar{w}_{k}(0)=0
and
\nabla\bar{w}_{k}(0)=-\nabla\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(0)=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})
by the fact that
\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}
is a harmonic function. Then using the Green representation formula and standard potential estimates (see for example the proof of Proposition
3.1
) we have the following preliminary estimate:

(4.31)

|\bar{w}_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta}\quad{in}\quad B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}.

Now we can improve the estimate on the oscillation of
\xi_{k}

Lemma 4.3
.

(4.32)

\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\alpha}),

\forall\,x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\}
.

Remark 4.5
.

We will need the full strength of (
4.32
) later on, for the time being we will use its weaker form

\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\alpha})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}})
, for some

\epsilon_{0}>0
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

We shall divide our proof into two steps, in the first step we derive the following estimate

(4.33)

\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}),

for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\left\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\right\}.
Then in the second step we shall improve the estimation and prove the desired estimate (
4.32
).

Step 1.
By using the Green representation formula for

\xi_{k}
, we have, for
x\in M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\},

\displaystyle\xi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\bar{\xi}_{k}+\int_{M}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)\xi_{k}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\bar{\xi}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Omega({p_{i}},\tau/2)}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)\xi_{k}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega({q},\tau/2)}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)\xi_{k}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau/2)\cup\Omega(q,\tau/2)\}}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)\xi_{k}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\bar{\xi}_{k}+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4},

with obvious meaning. Clearly
I_{4}
is of order
O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})
and we write,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=G(x,p_{1})\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}+\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)}(G(x,\eta)-G(x,p_{1}))\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle=I_{11}+I_{12}.

Concerning
I_{12}
, by the Mean Value Theorem, in local coordinates after scaling we have

G(x,\eta)-G(x,p_{1})=\varepsilon_{k}(a_{0}\cdot y)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|y|^{2}),

then in view of Lemma
4.2
, (
4.17
) and (
4.26
), we see that,

\displaystyle I_{12}=~

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}\int_{B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)(a_{0}\cdot y)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)+w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)

\displaystyle+O(\varepsilon_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta}))+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}\int_{B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)(a_{0}\cdot y)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

where we used
\psi^{k}_{\xi}(\varepsilon_{k}y)=O(\varepsilon_{k}|y|)
and the fact that
the first term on the right vanishes,

\varepsilon_{k}\int_{B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)(a_{0}\cdot y)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U^{k}_{2}}w^{k}_{0,\xi}(y)=0,

because
w^{k}_{0,\xi}
is a
radial function, whence the integrand is a separable function. The estimate about
I_{11}
is similar,

\displaystyle I_{11}=~

\displaystyle G(x,p_{1})\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)(\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k})+G(x,p_{1})\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)\psi_{\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle I_{1a}+I_{1b},

where, writing
\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)=\varepsilon_{k}a_{k}\cdot y+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|y|^{2})
, we have

I_{1b}=O(1)\int_{B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\rho_{k}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U^{k}_{2}}\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

again because the term of order
O({\varepsilon_{k}})
is the integral of a
separable function. On the other side,
I_{1a}=O(\varepsilon_{k})
since we can write,

(4.34)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)(\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k})=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U^{k}_{2}}w_{0,\xi}^{k}dy+O(\varepsilon_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\partial_{\nu}w_{0,\xi}^{k}+O(\varepsilon_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})+O(\varepsilon_{k})=O(\varepsilon_{k}),

where in the first equality we used (
4.26
) and in the second equality
we used a second order Taylor expansion of
\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
,
the fact that the integral on
B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}
of an integrable
separable function vanishes and at last
the equation for
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
and the fact that
\partial_{\nu}w_{0,\xi}^{k}=O(r^{-3-2\alpha})

on the boundary. It is crucial to point out

I_{1a}
is the only term of order
\varepsilon_{k}
, and all the other terms are either of order
\varepsilon_{k}^{2}
or
e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2}=\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\alpha}.
While for the integral
I_{2}
, by (
4.28
) and a similar argument we deduce that
I_{2}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})
. Concerning
I_{3}
we have,

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})+\int_{\Omega(q^{k}_{2},\tau/2)}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(x)\xi_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega(q^{k}_{2},\tau/2)}(G(x,\eta)-G(x,q^{k}_{2}))\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}+G(x,q^{k}_{2})\int_{\Omega(q^{k}_{2},\tau/2)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle\quad+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})

\displaystyle=I_{31}+I_{32}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}).

where we also used (
4.2
) and
\bar{q}_{2}^{k}-\bar{q}=\bar{q}_{2}^{k}=O(e^{-\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{1}}\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{1})
, see (
2.2
). By arguing as for
I_{12}
above we find that
I_{32}=O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})
. Also, by using (
4.31
) instead of (
4.26
),
the same argument adopted for
I_{11}
shows that
I_{31}=O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})
. We skip these details to avoid repetitions.
Therefore, we have shown so far that,

|\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})|=O(\varepsilon_{k})+O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}_{k}),

for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\},
that is exactly the estimation (
4.33
). As a consequence, we see immediately that
\psi^{k}_{\xi}
satisfies,

(4.35)

|\psi^{k}_{\xi}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|y|)\quad{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}.

We will use these facts in a sort of bootstrap argument to improve the estimates about the integration of
I_{1a}
and the
oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away from blow up points in the following.

Step 2.
We invoke the intermediate estimate in Lemma
3.2
about the expansion of
v_{2}^{k}
near
p_{1}
:

(4.36)

v_{2}^{k}=U_{2}^{k}+c_{0,k}+c_{1,k}+c_{2,k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\delta})(1+|y|)^{-\delta}.

Although we do not need its full strength right now, by using this approximation and
\sigma_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
(see (
4.17
)),
for later purposes we state the following expansion in local coordinates around
0=x(p_{1})
,

(4.37)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\exp\left\{v_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\right\}(1+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}))

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\left(1+c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{1,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}+\frac{\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle+E_{k},

where
c_{0,k}
is a radial function corresponding to the
\varepsilon_{k}^{2}
order term in the expansion of
v_{2}^{k}
(see (
3.18
)),
\tilde{c}_{1,k}
(defined as in (
3.29
)) and
\tilde{c}_{2,k}
are given below:

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{1,k}=\varepsilon_{k}\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\cdot\theta\left(r-\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\frac{r}{1+a_{k}r^{2\alpha+2}}\right),\quad\tilde{c}_{2,k}=c_{2,k}+\Theta_{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}r^{2},

where
a_{k}=\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}.
The error term
E_{k}
in (
4.37
) satisfies,

|E_{k}|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{-2+\epsilon_{0}-2\alpha}.

At this point, around
p_{1}
we define
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
,
the next term in the approximation of
\xi_{k}
, to be a suitable solution of,

(4.38)

\Delta w_{1,\xi}^{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}w_{1,\xi}^{k}=-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{0,\xi}^{k}.

It turns out that, see (
4.46
) below for further details,
(
4.38
) can be explicitly solved in terms of a function which also satisfies

w_{1,\xi}^{k}(0)=0=|\nabla w_{1,\xi}^{k}(0)|
and

|w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}(1+|y|)^{-1}\quad{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}.

In fact we will see that
\left(\mbox{recall}~\theta=\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)/|x|\right)
,

w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)=\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{4\alpha(1+\alpha)}(\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\cdot\theta)\frac{\varepsilon_{k}r^{2\alpha+3}}{(1+r^{2+2\alpha})^{2}},

is a solution of (
4.38
) which satisfies all those properties.

Next, let us we write the equation for
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
in the following form:

(4.39)

\displaystyle\Delta w_{1,\xi}^{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)w_{1,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}\left[\left(\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-e^{U_{2}^{k}}\right)w_{1,\xi}^{k}-e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{0,\xi}^{k}\right],

and then define,

z_{k}(y)=\tilde{w}_{k}(y)-w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)=\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)-w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y).

Therefore, in view of (
4.25
), (
4.35
) and (
4.39
), the equation for
z_{k}
reads:

\displaystyle\Delta z_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)z_{k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}(e^{U_{2}^{k}}-\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y))(w_{0,\xi}^{k}+w_{1,\xi}^{k})

\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{0,\xi}^{k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})(1+|y|)^{-2-2\alpha+\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\right)(1+|y|)^{-2-2\alpha}.

By using the usual argument based on Green’s representation formula (see Remark
3.1
), we have

(4.40)

|z_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|y|)^{\delta},

implying in particular that the oscillation of
z_{k}
on

\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}
is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2-\delta})
.

In view of (
4.40
), going back through the estimates above for
\xi_{k}
,
concerning in particular
I_{1a}
we find that,

\displaystyle I_{1a}

\displaystyle=G(x,p_{1})\int_{B(p_{1},\frac{\tau}{2})}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(\eta)(\xi_{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k})

\displaystyle=G(x,p_{1})\int_{B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\rho_{k}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{U^{k}_{2}}(w_{0,\xi}^{k}+w_{1,\xi}^{k})dy+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}).

At this point recall that, after a second order Taylor expansion of
\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
, the
zero-th order term relative to
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
has already been shown above
to be of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
(see (
4.34
)), the one relative to
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
vanishes since the
integrand is separable, the term proportional to
\varepsilon_{k}w_{0,\xi}^{k}
vanishes
again because the integrand is separable, while the one proportional to

\varepsilon_{k}w_{1,\xi}^{k}
is already of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
.
On the other hand, we have already mentioned in Step 1 that all the terms except for
I_{1a}
are of order either
\varepsilon_{k}^{2}
or
\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\alpha}
. Therefore, we derive that

|\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})|=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})+O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2}),

\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\}
,
which is (
4.32
). Hence, we finish the proof.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Even with the improved estimate (
4.32
),
the leading order in the estimate of
I_{32}
is still
O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})
.
This is due to the fact that near
q^{k}_{i}
, see (
4.13
),

\xi_{k}
has non-radial parts after scaling which contribute in the expansion
of
I_{32}
at order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})=O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})
.

Before moving to the next stage we remark that (
4.32
) further
improves the harmonic functions that encode the oscillations of
\xi_{k}
near blowup points.
For example, for
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
we now have

(4.41)

|\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|).

We will see that this estimate implies that all the terms in the expansions involving

\psi_{\xi}^{k}
are in fact negligible.

Next we identify the radial and non-radial parts of
\tilde{w}_{k}
, see (
4.24
).
In the second step of the estimates about
\tilde{w}_{k}
we use
E_{w}

to denote the right hand side of (
4.25
).

By using (
4.37
), the equation of
z_{k}
can be written as follows,

\displaystyle\Delta z_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)z_{k}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bigg(w_{0,\xi}^{k}\big(c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+w_{1,\xi}^{k}\tilde{c}_{1,k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})(1+|y|)^{-2-2\alpha+\epsilon_{0}}\bigg).

By using separable functions to remove separable terms, we can write the equation of the radial part of
z_{k}
, which we denote
\tilde{z}_{k}
,

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{z}_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{z}_{k}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\left[w_{0,\xi}^{k}\left(c_{0,k}+\frac{\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}+\frac{(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2}_{r}}{2}\right)+(w_{1,\xi}^{k}\tilde{c}_{1,k})_{r}\right],

where we let
(A)_{\theta},(A)_{r}
denote the angular part and radial part of
A
.
Corresponding to these terms we construct
z_{0}^{k}
to solve

(4.42)

\begin{cases}\dfrac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}z_{0}^{k}+\frac{1}{r}\dfrac{d}{dr}z_{0}^{k}+r^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}z_{0}^{k}=F_{0}^{k},\quad 0<r<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1},\\
\\
z_{0}^{k}(0)=\frac{d}{dr}z_{0}^{k}(0)=0,\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle F_{0}^{k}(r)=

\displaystyle-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bigg(w_{0,\xi}^{k}\big(c_{0,k}+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|^{2}(g_{k}+r)^{2}\big)+(w_{1,\xi}^{k}\tilde{c}_{1,k})_{r}\bigg)

and we used the fact that the radial part of
\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2}
is just

\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|^{2}(g_{k}+r)^{2}.

Again by standard potential estimates it is not difficult to see that

|z_{0}^{k}|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+r)^{-2\alpha}\log(1+r).

After defining
w_{2,\xi}^{k}
to remove the separable terms of the order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})

in
\tilde{w}_{k}
,

(4.43)

\displaystyle\Delta w_{2,\xi}^{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}w_{2,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=-|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bigg(w_{0,\xi}^{k}\big(\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2})_{\theta}\big)+(\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{1,\xi}^{k})_{\theta}\bigg),

and using (
3.26
) we see that

(4.44)

|w_{2,\xi}^{k}|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|y|)^{-2\alpha}\quad{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}.

Also by standard potential estimates as usual we have

\left|\tilde{w}_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}w_{i,\xi}^{k}-z_{0}^{k}(y)\right|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{-2\alpha+\epsilon_{0}}.

Next, by using (
4.17
) for
\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
and Lemma
4.2
for
\sigma_{k}
and
recalling the argument about
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
in the equation (
4.34
),
we evaluate the integral around
p_{1}
as follows,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)dy

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}|y|^{2\alpha}\exp\{U_{2}^{k}+c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{1,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0))\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|y|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2}\}(w_{0,\xi}^{k}+w_{1,\xi}^{k}+z_{0}^{k})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bigg(\big(1+c_{0,k}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\big)w_{0,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle\quad+(\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{1,\xi}^{k})_{r}+z_{0}^{k}\bigg)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}).

Remark that, by using the equation for
z_{0}^{k}
in (
4.42
), we have,

(4.45)

\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)dy=-\int_{\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\frac{\partial z_{0}^{k}}{\partial\nu}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}).

Let
c_{1,k}
be defined as in (
3.8
) and let us set,

\hat{c}_{1}^{k}(x)=c_{1,k}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right),

that is,

\hat{c}_{1}^{k}(x)=-\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}(\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\cdot\theta)\frac{e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}|x|}{\left(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}|x|^{2\alpha+2}\right)^{2}}.

Putting
\lambda=\lambda_{1}^{k}
and differentiating with respect to
\lambda
we have that,

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\hat{c}_{1}^{k}(x)=\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{4\alpha(1+\alpha)}(\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\cdot\theta)\frac{e^{-\lambda}|x|^{2\alpha+3}}{\left(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}|x|^{2\alpha+2}\right)^{2}}.

Setting

w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)=b_{0}^{k}\left(\frac{d}{d\lambda}\hat{c}_{1}^{k}\right)(\varepsilon_{k}y)\quad\mbox{with}\quad\varepsilon_{k}=e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{2(1+\alpha)}},

we have

(4.46)

w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)=b_{0}^{k}\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{4\alpha(1+\alpha)}(\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\cdot\theta)\frac{\varepsilon_{k}r^{2\alpha+3}}{\left(1+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}r^{2+2\alpha}\right)^{2}},

and it is readily seen (see also (
3.9
)) that
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
satisfies (
4.38
).

Similarly, let
A_{0}^{k}(x)=b_{0}^{k}c_{0,k}(|x|/\varepsilon_{k})
,
then we have,

\displaystyle\Delta A_{0}^{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\frac{|x|^{2\alpha}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}}{\left(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}|x|^{2\alpha+2}\right)^{2}}A_{0}^{k}

\displaystyle=-b_{0}^{k}\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{4}\frac{e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}|x|^{2\alpha+2}}{\left(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}|x|^{2\alpha+2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\left(1-\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\frac{e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}}{e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}+\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}}|x|^{2\alpha+2}}\right)^{2}|\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|^{2}+\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\right).

At this point we define
A_{\lambda}^{k}(x)=\frac{d}{d\lambda}A_{0}^{k}(x)
and then a lengthy evaluation
shows that,

(4.47)

z_{0}^{k}(y)=A_{\lambda}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y).

By using (
4.47
) we deduce that,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\Delta z_{0}^{k}=~

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\Delta A_{\lambda}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)dy

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau}}\Delta A^{k}_{\lambda}(x)dx=\frac{d}{d\lambda}\int_{B_{\tau}}\Delta A_{0}^{k}(x)dx.

Since

\Delta A_{0}^{k}(x)=b_{0}^{k}\Delta c_{0,k}\left(\frac{|x|}{\varepsilon_{k}}\right)\varepsilon_{k}^{-2},

we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{b_{0}^{k}}\int_{B_{\tau}}\Delta A_{0}^{k}(x)dx=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\Delta c_{0,k}(y)dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\frac{\partial c_{0,k}}{\partial\nu}=d_{1,k}\Delta\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}),

and consequently, by a straightforward inspection of (
3.24
),

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\frac{\partial z_{0}^{k}}{\partial\nu}=\int_{B_{\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}}}\Delta z_{0}^{k}=-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}b_{0}^{k}d_{1,k}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}),

which, in view of (
4.45
) eventually implies that,

(4.48)

\int_{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(x)\xi_{k}=-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}b_{0}^{k}d_{1,k}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}).

On the other side, the contribution of the integral around
p_{2}
is very small,

(4.49)

\int_{\Omega(p_{2},\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(x)\xi_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}),

as can be seen by an argument similar to that adopted around
p_{1}
. Indeed,
in the expansion of
\xi_{k}
around
p_{2}
, the equation for
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
also comes from
the differentiation of
\lambda
with respect to the
c_{1,k}
in the expansion of
u_{1}^{k}
.
The equation for
z_{0}^{k}
, which is the second order radial term in the expansion of
\xi_{k}

around
p_{2}
, also comes from that of
c_{0,k}
in the expansion of
u_{1}^{k}
around
p_{2}
.
However the scaling is now with respect to
\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}=\varepsilon_{k}^{\frac{1+\alpha}{1+\beta}}
, whence

O(\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
, implying immediately that (
4.49
) holds.
The proof would be a cut and paste of the same argument adopted above for
p_{1}
with minor changes,
where one needs just to replace
\alpha
by
\beta
, we skip these details to avoid repetitions.

Concerning the integral on
\Omega(q,\tau)
, we first improve the estimate of

\bar{w}_{k}
(see (
4.31
)). At first, we can show that on
\partial B_{\tau/\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y_{1})-\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y_{2})=~

\displaystyle\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y_{1})-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y_{1})-\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y_{2})+\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y_{2})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}+\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

where we used Lemma
4.3
and the explicit formula of
\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)
. As a consequence, by a standard argument we could derive an estimate like
(
4.41
) holds for
\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\varepsilon_{k}y)
as well, i.e.,

(4.50)

|\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|\leq C(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2})(1+|y|),\quad y\in B_{\tau/\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}.

While at
\bar{q}_{2}^{k}
, by standard elliptic estimate for harmonic function we have
|\nabla\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(0)|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
. Therefore, in local
coordinates such that
0=x(q)
and after scaling
x=\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y
,
where
\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}/2}
,
we can write
the equation for
\bar{w}_{k}
as follows,

\Delta\bar{w}_{k}+\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\bar{c}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)\bar{w}_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})(1+|y|)^{-3}\quad{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}.

Here we remark that, compared with (
4.30
), the improvement in the estimates of the
right hand side is obtained because of (
4.17
), the vanishing rate of the gradient of the coefficient function
for regular blowup points (see (
2.1
)),
the improved estimate (
4.50
) for
\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}
and the fact that

O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
. On the other hand, we have

\bar{w}_{k}(0)=0,\quad\nabla\bar{w}_{k}(0)=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})\quad{\rm and}\quad\bar{w}_{k}\mbox{ is a constant on }\partial B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}.

As a consequence, by the usual potential estimates, we conclude that,

|\bar{w}_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad|y|\leq\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

Next, by using the expansion of
\tilde{c}_{k}
in (
4.17
) we have,

\int_{\Omega(q,\tau)}\rho_{k}H(x)c_{k}(x)\xi_{k}=\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}).

Remark that
e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}})
, then by the expansion of
\xi_{k}
in (
4.31
) we see that
all the terms including
\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
are of order

O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
.
Also, neglecting terms which vanish due to the separability of the integrand we have that,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{w}^{k}_{0,\xi}+\bar{w}_{k})+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}(\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\cdot y)\frac{e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{b}_{k}\cdot y)}{1+\frac{\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}|y|^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}(\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\cdot y)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bar{w}_{k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bar{w}_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}(\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\cdot y)\frac{e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{b}_{k}\cdot y)}{1+\frac{\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}^{k}_{2})}{8}|y|^{2}}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}),

where
\bar{b}_{k}=(\bar{b}^{k}_{1},\bar{b}^{k}_{2})
and we used the same argument as in (
4.34
) to show that

\int_{B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}}\bar{h}_{k}(\bar{q}^{k}_{2})e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bar{w}^{k}_{0,\xi}
is of order

O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
.
Therefore we eventually deduce that,

(4.51)

\int_{\Omega(q,\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(x)\xi_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}).

In view of (
4.48
), (
4.49
), (
4.51
), we come up with
a contradiction to

\int_{M}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}(x){\xi_{k}}=0

as follows,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}+\int_{\Omega(p_{2},\tau)}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}+\int_{\Omega(q,\tau)}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{M\setminus\{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)\cup\Omega(p_{2},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau)\}}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle=Cb_{0}\Delta\log h_{k}(p_{1})\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+o(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}),

for some constant
C\neq 0
, since in particular the integrals on
\Omega(p_{2},\tau)
,
\Omega(q,\tau)

and
M\setminus\{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)\cup\Omega(p_{2},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau)\}
are all of order

o(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
.
Since for
k
large
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
is the same as
\Delta\log h_{k}(p_{1})\neq 0
,
we obtain a contradiction as far as
b_{0}\neq 0
.

Remark 4.7
.

To summarize the idea of the proof of
b_{0}=0
. The assumption
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
plays an important role. First we need it to prove that
\sigma_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})
. This estimate makes it possible to have a good approximation of
\xi_{k}
around each blowup point. Then we use
L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
again together with
\int_{M}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}=0
to get a contradiction.

4.3.
Proof of
b_{1}=b_{2}=0

First we remark that in all the previous works on uniqueness of bubbling solutions, proving
b_{1}=b_{2}=0
after the establishment of
b_{0}=0
usually requires little new ideas as well as effort. But in this case we need to establish major new estimates and the proof is divided in multiple non-trivial steps. At the beginning of this part we
recall that

u^{k}_{i}

i=1,2
satisfy (
4.6
), (
4.9
),
in local coordinates around
q
,
0=x(q)
,

\Delta u_{i}^{k}+\bar{h}_{k}(x)e^{u_{i}^{k}}=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad B_{\tau},\quad i=1,2,

and that
\bar{q}_{i}^{k}
denote the local maximum points of
u_{i}^{k}

i=1,2
. Of course we have

|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|=O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})
.
As remarked right after (
4.10
) we also have that,

\xi_{k}=\frac{u_{1}^{k}-u_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}=\frac{{\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}},

where
\xi_{k}
satisfies,

\Delta\xi_{k}+\bar{h}_{k}\tilde{c}_{k}\xi_{k}=0\quad{in}\quad B_{\tau}.

The expression of
\tilde{c}_{k}
is

\tilde{c}_{k}(x)=e^{u_{2}^{k}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{t(u_{1}^{k}-u_{2}^{k})}dt=e^{u_{2}^{k}}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{k}\xi_{k}+\frac{1}{6}\sigma_{k}^{2}\xi_{k}^{2}+O(\sigma_{k}^{3})\right).

One major consequence of
b_{0}=0
is that, since after scaling around a blow up point
the local limit of
\xi_{k}
takes the form (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.13
) respectively,
then it is not difficult to see that,

\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}\to 0,\quad\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\tilde{\lambda}_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}\to 0,\quad\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}\to 0.

Also, see Remark
4.3
, at this point we know that, far
away from blow up points,
\xi_{k}
is of order
o(1)
. This fact obviously implies that,
in local coordinates around any blow up point, defining
\phi_{i}^{k}

i=1,2
to be the harmonic functions
encoding the oscillations of
u_{i}^{k}
, then we have,

(\phi_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})/\sigma_{k}=o(1).

On the other side, we cannot anymore rely on (
4.16
) which was deduced
by
b_{0}\neq 0
, whence neither we can rely on Lemma
4.2
or on the
improved estimates (
4.32
). This is why we have to derive
first a new estimate about
\sigma_{k}
, this time based on
b_{0}=0
. On the other side,
since our current goal is to prove
b_{1}=b_{2}=0
, we can assume without loss of generality that
at least one of them is not zero.

Step one: An initial estimate of
\sigma_{k}

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume that
(b_{1},b_{2})\neq(0,0)
, then

\sigma_{k}=O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{2}}).

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Let

\bar{u}^{k}_{i}=u_{i}^{k}-\phi_{i}^{k},

where, in local coordinates around
q
,
\phi_{i}^{k}

i=1,2
are the harmonic functions
encoding the oscillations, of
u_{i}^{k}
on
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{i}^{k})
, whence satisfying,
\phi_{i}^{k}(\bar{q}_{i}^{k})=0
.
Thus, working in local coordinates around
q

such that
0=x(q)
, since
u_{i}^{k}
,
i=1,2
, satisfy (
4.9
) we can write

(4.52)

\Delta\bar{u}_{2}^{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\bar{u}_{2}^{k}}=0\quad{in}\quad B_{\tau}

with

(4.53)

\bar{h}_{k,0}(x)=\bar{h}_{k}(x)e^{\phi_{2}^{k}(x)}.

Therefore the equation for
\bar{u}_{1}^{k}
reads,

(4.54)

\Delta\bar{u}_{1}^{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\phi_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k}}e^{\bar{u}_{1}^{k}}=0\quad{in}\quad B_{\tau}

The reason that we are not introducing a new coefficient function for
\bar{u}_{1}^{k}
is because we use
\bar{u}_{2}^{k}
as the base to carry out the proof and we will focus on the coefficient function
\bar{h}_{k,0}
of
\bar{u}_{2}^{k}
.
With these definitions we have

(4.55)

\xi_{k}=\frac{\bar{u}_{1}^{k}-\bar{u}_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}+\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k},\quad\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}=\frac{\phi_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}

and the expression of
c_{k}
near
q
becomes,

\rho_{k}H\tilde{c}_{k}=\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\bar{u}_{2}^{k}}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{k}\xi_{k}+O(\sigma_{k}^{2})\right).

Let
\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}
be the harmonic function on
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
that encodes the oscillation of
u_{1}^{k}
on
\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
:

\begin{cases}\Delta\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}=0,\quad&{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}),\\
\\
\phi_{1}^{k}(x)=u_{1}^{k}(x)-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(q_{2}^{k})}u_{1}^{k},&{\rm on}\quad\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}),\end{cases}

then we observe that
(\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})/\sigma_{k}=o(1)
in
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
because of the following boundary estimate,

\displaystyle\frac{(\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})(x)}{\sigma_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{1}^{k}(x)-u_{2}^{k}(x)}{\sigma_{k}}-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\frac{u_{1}^{k}-u_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{k}(x)-\frac{1}{2\pi\tau}\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\xi_{k}=o(1),\quad x\in\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}).

Next we claim that

(4.56)

\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}(x)-\phi_{1}^{k}(x)=O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{k}}),\quad x\in B_{\frac{\tau}{2}}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}).

Indeed, by the definition of
\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}
we have,

(4.57)

\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}(x)=-\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\partial_{\nu}G_{2}(x,\eta)\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}(\eta)dS_{\eta},\quad x\in B_{\tau/2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}),

where
G_{2}
is the Green function on
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
. Similarly for
\phi_{1}^{k}
we have

\phi_{1}^{k}(x)=-\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})}\partial_{\nu}G_{1}(x,\eta)\phi_{1}^{k}(\eta)dS_{\eta},\quad x\in B_{\tau/2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})

where
G_{1}
is the Green’s function on
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})
.
After a translation that moves
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})
to
B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
we see that,

(4.58)

\phi_{1}^{k}(x)=-\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\partial_{\nu}G_{2}(x,\eta)\phi_{1}^{k}(\eta+\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k})dS_{\eta},\quad x\in B_{\tau/2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}).

From (
4.57
) and (
4.58
) we see that

|\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}(x)-\phi_{1}^{k}(x)|\leq C\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}|\phi_{1}^{k}(\eta+\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k})-\bar{\phi}_{1}^{k}(\eta))|dS_{\eta}\leq C\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}

for
x\in B_{\tau/2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
because
|\nabla u_{1}^{k}|=O(1)
outside bubbling area and

|\bar{q}_{2}^{k}-\bar{q}_{1}^{k}|=O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}).

Hence, (
4.56
) is established. As a consequence, as far as
\sigma_{k}/(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})\to\infty
, we have
