\frac{\phi_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}=o(1)\quad{\rm in}\quad B_{\tau/2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}).

Here we also note that the maximum of
\bar{u}_{i}^{k}
is
\bar{q}_{i}^{k}+O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})
(see
[
83
]
).
It is readily seen that the corresponding error is negligible for our purposes, which is why,
with an abuse of notations, we will just use
\bar{q}_{i}^{k}
to denote the local maximum of
\bar{u}_{i}^{k}
as well.
Recall that the weight function in (
4.52
) is
\bar{h}_{k,0}
whence the weight in (
4.54
) is

\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\phi_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k}}
. Since

\phi_{i}^{k}(\bar{q}_{i}^{k})=0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|=O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}),

we have

\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})e^{(\phi_{1}^{k}-\phi_{2}^{k})(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})}=\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}).

Thus, by the main theorem in
[
44
]
we can write
\bar{u}_{i}^{k}
as follows,

\bar{u}_{i}^{k}=\bar{\lambda}_{i}^{k}-2\log(1+\frac{e^{\bar{\lambda}_{i}^{k}}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{i}^{k})}{8}|x-\bar{q}_{i}^{k}|^{2})+O((\lambda_{1}^{k})^{2}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}),\quad i=1,2.

To simplify the exposition let us set
h_{i}=\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{i}^{k})
, then we have,

\bar{u}_{1}^{k}-\bar{u}_{2}^{k}=\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}+2\log\left(1+\frac{e^{\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}}\frac{h_{2}}{8}|x-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|^{2}-e^{\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}}\frac{h_{1}}{8}|x-\bar{q}_{1}^{k}|^{2}}{1+e^{\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}}\frac{h_{1}}{8}|x-\bar{q}_{1}^{k}|^{2}}\right)+O((\lambda_{1}^{k})^{2}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}).

Denoting as usual
\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}/2}
we have

\displaystyle\bar{u}_{1}^{k}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\bar{u}_{2}^{k}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)

\displaystyle=\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}+2\log\left(1+\frac{\frac{e^{\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}}h_{2}}{8}|\frac{\bar{q}_{2}^{k}-\bar{q}_{1}^{k}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}+y|^{2}-\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}{1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}\right)+O((\lambda_{1}^{k})^{2}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}).

In view of
\log(1+A)=A+O(A^{2})
, we define,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}}\frac{h_{2}}{8}|\frac{\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}+y|^{2}-\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}{1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}+O(|\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}|^{2}))\frac{h_{2}}{8}(|y|^{2}+2a\cdot y+|a|^{2})-\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}{1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}

where
a=\frac{\bar{q}_{2}^{k}-\bar{q}_{1}^{k}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}
. At this point, as far as

\sigma_{k}/(\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}/2})\to+\infty
, combining the leading terms we have,

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{u}_{1}^{k}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\bar{u}_{2}^{k}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}+\bar{\epsilon}_{k}y)}{\sigma_{k}}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}}{\sigma_{k}}\frac{1-\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}{1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}+\frac{h_{1}}{2}\frac{\frac{a}{\sigma_{k}}\cdot y}{1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2}}+o(1).

Obviously the radial leading term vanishes in the limit due to

(\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k})/\sigma_{k}\to 0~(\mbox{that is }b_{0}=0),

while

b_{1},b_{2}
should arise from the term

\frac{h_{1}}{2}\frac{\frac{\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\sigma_{k}}\cdot y}{(1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2})}.

However, by using again
\sigma_{k}/(\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}/2})\to\infty
, we deduce that

b_{1}=b_{2}=0
because of,

\frac{|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}=O(\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}/2}).

A contradiction to
(b_{1},b_{2})\neq(0,0)
. Thus the Lemma
4.4
is proved.
∎

Step two: Intermediate estimates for
\bar{v}_{2}^{k}

We work in local coordinates centered at
q
,
0=x(q)
, with
\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}/2}
and define

(4.59)

\bar{v}_{2}^{k}(y)=\bar{u}_{2}^{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)+2\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{k},\quad y\in\Omega_{k},

and

(4.60)

\bar{\xi}_{k}(y)=\xi_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y),\quad y\in\Omega_{k}:=B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}.

Note that
\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
has no oscillation on
\partial\Omega_{k}
. If we invoke Lemma
3.2
for
\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
we have

(4.61)

\bar{v}_{2}^{k}(y)=U_{2}^{k}+c_{0,k}+c_{2,k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\delta})(1+|y|)^{\delta}.

In view of Lemma
4.4
we can write

\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\rho_{k}H\tilde{c}_{k}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)\xi_{k}=\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bar{\xi}_{k}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{k}\bar{\xi}_{k}+O(\sigma_{k}^{2})\right).

Here we point out that the function which we called
u_{k}
in Lemma
3.2
is
what we call
\bar{u}^{k}_{i}
,
i=1,2
here, which satisfy (
4.52
), (
4.53
). Also remark that

\bar{\lambda}_{i}^{k}=\bar{u}^{k}_{i}(\bar{q}^{k}_{i})
,
i=1,2
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}/2}
.
Now we set

w_{2}^{k}(y)=\bar{v}_{2}^{k}(y)-c_{0,k}(y)-c_{2,k}(y),

where
c_{i,k}

i=0,2
, are defined as in the proof of Theorem
3.3
. Here we explain why there is no
c_{1,k}
term. The reason is
\bar{h}_{k,0}
satisfies
\nabla\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})=O(\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}})
. The estimate of
c_{1,k}
would be

|c_{1,k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}}\log(1+|y|)(1+|y|)^{-1},\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega_{k},

and

|\nabla c_{1,k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3+\epsilon_{0}},\quad y\in\Omega_{k},\quad|y|\sim\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

These two estimates show that at this stage the term
c_{1,k}
is negligible.Indeed

w_{2}^{k}
satisfies,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta w_{2}^{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}w_{2}^{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3})(1+|y|)^{-1},\\
\\
w_{2}^{k}(0)=|\nabla w_{2}^{k}(0)|=0,\end{cases}

and the oscillation of
w_{2}^{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
is of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
. Now we use Lemma
3.2
to obtain

|w_{2}^{k}(y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{2\epsilon_{0}},

which is equivalent to

(4.62)

|\bar{v}_{2}^{k}-U_{2}^{k}-c_{0,k}-c_{2,k}|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{2\epsilon_{0}},\quad y\in\Omega_{k}.

The gradient estimate on the boundary is

(4.63)

\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}=\nabla U_{2}^{k}+\nabla c_{0,k}+\nabla c_{2,k}+O(\tau)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3},\quad y\in\Omega_{k},\quad|y|\sim\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

We shall also need the following estimate based on (
4.61
),

(4.64)

\displaystyle\bar{v}_{2}^{k}(y)+\log\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)}{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}=

\displaystyle U_{2}^{k}+c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{\Delta(\log\bar{h}_{k,0})(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}r^{2}}{4}

\displaystyle+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3})r^{3}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\delta})(1+r)^{\delta},

We will use (
4.64
) to derive a better estimate for
|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|
.

Step three: Initial estimates of the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away from blow up points

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\left\{\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau)\right\}
, there exists
C>0
independent of
k
and
\tau
that

|\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})|\leq C\varepsilon_{k}

Proof.

Since
b_{0}=0
then
\xi_{k}\to 0
in
C^{2}_{\rm loc}(M\setminus\{p_{1},p_{2},q\})
. Based on this information we consider again
the expansion of
\xi_{k}
around
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
and
q
. In local coordinates around
p_{1}
,
0=x(p_{1})
, we define

\tilde{\xi}_{k}(y)=\xi_{k}(p_{1}+\varepsilon_{k}y)

which
satisfies

(4.65)

\Delta\tilde{\xi}_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)e^{v_{2}^{k}}\tilde{\xi}_{k}=\frac{O(\sigma_{k})}{(1+|y|)^{4+2\alpha}}\quad{\rm in}\quad\Omega_{k}=B_{\tau/\varepsilon_{k}},

with
\tilde{h}_{k}
defined in (
4.7
).
Our aim is to derive a first approximation of
\tilde{\xi}_{k}
. Let
\tilde{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)

be the harmonic function that satisfies

\tilde{\xi}_{k}-\tilde{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\varepsilon_{k}\cdot)=
constant on
\partial\Omega_{k}
and
\tilde{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(0)=0
.
Then it is easy to see that

\tilde{\xi}_{1,k}(y):=\tilde{\xi}_{k}(y)-\tilde{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\varepsilon_{k}y)

satisfies

(\Delta+|y|^{\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}})\tilde{\xi}_{1,k}=(o(\varepsilon_{k})+O(\sigma_{k}))(1+|y|)^{-2\alpha-3}.

To obtain a first order approximation of
\tilde{\xi}_{1,k}
we use

\tilde{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}=\tilde{b}_{0}^{k}\frac{1-a_{k}|y|^{2+2\alpha}}{1+a_{k}|y|^{2+2\alpha}},\quad a_{k}=\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\alpha)^{2}},

where we choose
\tilde{b}_{0}^{k}
such that
\tilde{\xi}_{1,k}(0)-w_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=0
. Note that we have
\tilde{b}_{0}^{k}\to 0
but we don’t have yet any information about the rate of vanishing of
\tilde{b}_{0}^{k}
.
By the usual standard elliptic estimates we have,

(4.66)

|\tilde{\xi}_{1,k}(y)-\tilde{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)(o(\varepsilon_{k})+\sigma_{k})(1+|y|)^{\delta}\quad{in}\quad\Omega_{k}.

Similar estimates can also be obtained around
p_{2}
and
q_{2}^{k}
. Around
p_{2}
the scaling factor is

\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}=e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{2(1+\beta)}}
and, in local coordinates around
p_{2}
,
0=x(p_{2})
,
we define

\hat{\xi}_{1,k}=\xi_{k}(p_{2}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}y)-\hat{\psi}_{k}(\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}y),

with the corresponding obvious meaning
of
\hat{\psi}_{k}
, and

\hat{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)=\hat{b}_{0}^{k}\frac{1-\hat{a}_{k}|y|^{2+2\beta}}{1+\hat{a}_{k}|y|^{2+2\beta}},\quad\hat{a}_{k}=\frac{\hat{h}_{k}(0)}{8(1+\beta)^{2}}

to be the first term in approximation of
\hat{\xi}_{1,k}
, where
\hat{b}_{0}^{k}
is chosen so that
\hat{\xi}_{1,k}(0)-\hat{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=0
and
\hat{h}_{k}
defined in (
4.8
). Thus,
by arguing as above we have that,

(4.67)

|\hat{\xi}_{1,k}-\hat{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)\sigma_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad|y|\leq\tau\hat{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

At last, around
\bar{q}_{2}^{k}
, the equation for
\bar{\xi}_{k}(y)
( see (
4.60
) ) reads,

\Delta\bar{\xi}_{k}(y)+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\exp\left(\bar{v}_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)}{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\right)\bar{\xi}_{k}(y)=\frac{O(\sigma_{k})}{(1+|y|)^{4}},

for
|y|\leq\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}
. Let us recall again (see (
3.44
))
that,

\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})=O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}).

We denote by
\bar{\xi}_{1,k}
the function
\xi_{1,k}
modified to have vanishing oscillation and let

\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)=\bar{b}_{0}^{k}\frac{1-\bar{a}_{k}|y|^{2}}{1+\bar{a}_{k}|y|^{2}}+b_{1}^{k}\frac{y_{1}}{1+\bar{a}_{k}|y|^{2}}+b_{2}^{k}\frac{y_{2}}{1+\bar{a}_{k}|y|^{2}}

be the first term in the corresponding approximation, where
\bar{a}_{k}=\frac{\bar{h}_{k}^{*}(0)}{8}
and
\bar{b}_{0}^{k}\to 0
,
b_{1}^{k}\to b_{1}
,
b_{2}^{k}\to b_{2}

are such that

\bar{\xi}_{1,k}(0)=\bar{w}_{0,k}(0),\quad|\nabla(\bar{\xi}_{1,k}-\bar{w}_{0,k})(0)|=0.

Here we note that the non-radial part of
\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}
has an oscillation of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
.
However this oscillation can be removed by another harmonic function whose modulus is not larger than
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})|y|
,
which is a minor contribution at this point. Thus we have

(4.68)

|\bar{\xi}_{1,k}-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}|\leq C(\delta)\sigma_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad|y|\leq\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

By using (
4.66
),(
4.67
) and (
4.68
) we derive the desired estimate of the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away
from blow up points. For any
x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus(\Omega({p_{1}},\tau)\cup\Omega({p_{2}},\tau)\cup\Omega({q},\tau))
we use
the Green representation formula for
\xi_{k}
,

(4.69)

\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})=\int_{M}(G(x_{1},\eta)-G(x_{2},\eta))\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}(\eta)d\eta.

It is enough to evaluate the r.h.s of (
4.69
) on the three local disks surrounding the blow up points, since
the integral over the remaining region is of order
O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})
. Concerning the integral over
\Omega(p_{1},\tau)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)}(G(x_{1},\eta)-G(x_{2},\eta))\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}d\eta

\displaystyle=G_{12}(x_{12},p_{1})\int_{B_{\tau}}|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{u_{2}^{k}}\xi_{k}d\eta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{B_{\tau}}(G_{12}(x_{12},\eta)-G_{12}(x_{12},p_{1}))|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{u_{2}^{k}}\xi_{k}d\eta+O(\sigma_{k}),

where
G_{12}(x_{12},\eta)=G(x_{1},\eta)-G(x_{2},\eta)
. Recall that (see (
4.34
))
by using the equation satisfied by
\tilde{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}
and the fact that
\partial_{\nu}\tilde{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}=O(r^{-3-2\alpha})
,
then one can see that,

\int_{B_{\tau}}|x|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{u_{2}^{k}}\tilde{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}),

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
Since the error estimates in (
4.66
) is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k})
, then it is readily
seen as well that the integral over
\Omega(p_{1},\tau)
is
O(\varepsilon_{k})
.
The integrals around
p_{2}
and
q
are of the order
O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2})
.
Lemma
4.5
is established. ∎

Step four: a crucial new estimate of
|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|

Lemma 4.6
.

|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}

for
C>0
independent of
k
.

Proof.

We consider the following Pohozaev identity for the equation of
\bar{u}_{2}^{k}
: for any
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

\int_{B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\partial_{\xi}\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\bar{u}_{2}^{k}}=\int_{\partial B_{\tau}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}(\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\bar{u}_{2}^{k}}(\xi\cdot\nu)+\partial_{\nu}\bar{u}_{2}^{k}\partial_{\xi}\bar{u}_{2}^{k}-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\bar{u}_{2}^{k}|^{2}(\xi\cdot\nu))dS

and setting
\Omega_{k}=B_{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}
, then the Pohozaev identity takes the form (recall that
\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
is defined in (
4.59
))

(4.70)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\xi}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\int\limits_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\bigg(\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}(\xi\cdot\nu)+\partial_{\nu}\bar{v}_{2}^{k}\partial_{\xi}\bar{v}_{2}^{k}-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}|^{2}(\xi\cdot\nu)\bigg)dS.

We use (
4.64
) for the expansion of
\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
and (
4.63
) for
\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
. In addition we use the following identities,

\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\int_{\Omega_{k}}e^{U_{2}^{k}}dx=8\pi\left(1-\frac{1}{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\tau^{-2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4})\right),

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\frac{\partial_{\xi}(\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k,0})(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\int_{\Omega_{k}}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})|y|^{2}e^{U_{2}^{k}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\frac{\partial_{\xi}(\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k,0})(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}2\pi\int_{0}^{\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})r^{3}}{(1+\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=16\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\frac{\partial_{\xi}(\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k,0})(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{(\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}))^{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}\tau^{2}\right)+2\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\right)+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4}).

Based on (
4.61
), (
4.63
), (
4.64
) the left hand side of (
4.70
) takes the form

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\left(8\pi-\frac{8}{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\tau^{-2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+16\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\frac{\partial_{\xi}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{\bar{h}_{k,0}^{2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\bigg(\log\left(\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}\tau^{2}\right)+2\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\bigg)+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}),

where
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
comes from the
O(r^{3})
term in the expansion of
\partial_{\xi}\bar{h}_{k,0}
.

Concerning the right hand side of (
4.70
), we estimate the first term
by using the expansion in (
4.62
) and the cancellations of those integrals involving separable terms, to obtain that it is of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
. Here we also use the fact that
e^{v_{2}^{k}}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{4})
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
. For the two other terms, which involve
\nabla v_{2}^{k}
, we use (
4.63
) to deduce that their contribution is of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
. Therefore, from the evaluation of (
4.70
) around
\bar{q}_{2}^{k}
, we have that,

(4.71)

\displaystyle 16\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\frac{\partial_{\xi}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{\bar{h}_{k,0}^{2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\bigg(\log\left(\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}\tau^{2}\right)+2\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\bigg)

\displaystyle+8\pi\partial_{\xi}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}),

where we have used the fact that
|\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}})
. The same argument
yields a similar estimate for

\bar{v}_{1}^{k}(y)=\bar{u}_{1}^{k}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}y)+2\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k},\quad|y|<\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}^{-1},\quad\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}=e^{-\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{k}_{1}}{2}}.

Indeed, denoting by
\bar{h}_{k,1}=\bar{h}_{k,0}e^{\phi^{k}_{1}-\phi_{2}^{k}}
, which is the weight function relative to
\bar{u}_{1}^{k}
, we have

(4.72)

\displaystyle 16\pi\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}^{2}\frac{\partial_{\xi}\bar{h}_{k,1}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})}{\bar{h}_{k,1}^{2}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})}\Delta\log\bar{h}_{k,1}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})\bigg(\log\left(\frac{\bar{h}_{k,1}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})}{8}\tau^{2}\right)+2\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}}\bigg)

\displaystyle+8\pi\partial_{\xi}\log\bar{h}_{k,1}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}^{2}).

In view of
\rho_{1}^{k}=\rho_{2}^{k}=\rho_{k}
we already know (see (
2.3
)) that
\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}-\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{k}=O(e^{-\epsilon_{0}\lambda_{1}^{k}})
(
i=1,2
) for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
, whence we also have

\bar{\varepsilon}_{1,k}-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}}).

The difference between
\bar{h}_{k,1}
and
\bar{h}_{k,0}
is (see (
4.55
))

\bar{h}_{k,1}(x)-\bar{h}_{k,0}(x)=\bar{h}_{k,0}(x)(e^{\sigma_{k}\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}}-1).

By Lemma
4.5
we have,

|\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}y,

whence, for a small positive constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
,

|\sigma_{k}\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}|y|,\quad|y|<\frac{\tau}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}.

Thus, recalling that
\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}
is harmonic, we see that all the terms containing

\bar{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}
are negligible and we can expand the first term in (
4.72
) as follows,

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\log\bar{h}_{k,1}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k})=~

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{\xi j}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k})_{j}

\displaystyle+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})+O(|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|^{2}).

The same argument applies of course to the other terms on the left of (
4.72
). Therefore, for
\xi=1,2
, the difference between (
4.71
) and (
4.72
) takes the form,

\left(\begin{matrix}\partial_{11}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+E_{h,11}&\partial_{12}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+E_{h,12}\\
\partial_{21}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+E_{h,21}&\partial_{22}\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+E_{h,22}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k})_{1}\\
(\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k})_{2}\end{matrix}\right)=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}).

where
E_{h,ij}=O\left(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}\log\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\right)
. At this point the non-degeneracy assumption about
D^{2}\log(\bar{h}_{k,0})
readily implies that
|q_{1}^{k}-q_{2}^{k}|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
as well.
Lemma
4.6
is established. ∎

Remark 4.8
.

The non-degeneracy assumption of
{\rm det}(D^{2}f^{*})\neq 0
is equivalent to
{\rm det}(D^{2}\log(\bar{h}_{k,0}))(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\neq 0
.

As a consequence of this improved estimate about
|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|
we have,

Corollary 4.1
.

There exists
C>0
independent of
k
such that

\sigma_{k}\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}.

Proof.

We take advantage of the new estimate of
|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|
and go through the argument used in Lemma
4.4
to estimate
\sigma_{k}
, by using the expansion (
4.64
) and Lemma
4.6
. Then we see that
b_{1},b_{2}
are determined by

\frac{h_{1}}{2}\frac{\frac{\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\sigma_{k}}\cdot y}{(1+\frac{h_{1}}{8}|y|^{2})},

which implies, in view of the new estimate about
|\bar{q}_{1}^{k}-\bar{q}_{2}^{k}|
, that we have

\sigma_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}).

We omit the details of this proof just to avoid repetitions.
∎

Step five: Improved estimate on the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
away from blowup points and vanishing rates of
b_{0}^{k}
,
\hat{b}_{0}^{k}
and
\bar{b}_{0}^{k}
.

An major consequence of Corollary
4.1
is that the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away from blow up points is of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})
.

Proposition 4.1
.

For any
x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)\cup\Omega(p_{2},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau)\}
,

|\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}

for some
C>0
independent by
k
.

Proof.

Recall that Lemma
4.5
speaks that the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away from blow up points is of order
O(\varepsilon_{k})
.
Because of this fact, the estimates about the harmonic functions used to eliminate the oscillations
of
\xi_{k}
is improved as well. For example concerning the function
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
around
p_{1}
we have,

\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)=\sum_{j}a_{j}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}y_{j}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{3})|y|^{2},\quad|y|<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1},

for suitable
a_{j},j=1,2
.
We study the expansion of
\xi_{k}
around
p_{1}
again to obtain an estimate about the decay of
b_{0}^{k}
. In fact at this point we know that
b_{0}^{k}\to 0
.
In local coordinates around
p_{1}
,
0=x(p_{1})
, the first term of the approximation of
\xi_{k}
around
p_{1}
is still
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
defined as in (
4.20
) that satisfies (
4.21
).
We need to write the equation for
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
in the same form as that satisfied by

\tilde{\xi}_{k}(y)
(see (
4.65
)). Thus by using the expansion of
v_{2}^{k}
around
p_{1}
(
4.36
), we write

\displaystyle|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\mbox{exp}\bigg(v_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\mbox{exp}\{U_{2}^{k}+c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{1,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})(1+|y|)^{2+\epsilon_{0}})\}

\displaystyle=|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}(1+c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{1,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2})+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})(1+|y|)^{-2-\epsilon_{0}},

for some small
\epsilon_{0}>0
depending by
\alpha>0
. Then we can write the equation of
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
as follows,

(4.73)

\displaystyle\Delta w_{0,\xi}^{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}\exp\left(v_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\right)w_{0,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}w_{0,\xi}^{k}\left(c_{0,k}+\tilde{c}_{1,k}+\tilde{c}_{2,k}+\frac{\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}}{4}+\frac{\tilde{c}_{1,k}^{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}})b_{0}^{k}(1+|y|)^{-2-\epsilon_{0}}.

The next term in the expansion is
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
defined as in (
4.38
). Now we need to include
b_{0}^{k}
in the estimate of
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
,

|w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)|\leq Cb_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}(1+|y|)^{-1},

where we used
w_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=b_{0}^{k}
.
At this point we write the equation for
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
in the following form:

\displaystyle\Delta w_{1,\xi}^{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}\exp\bigg(v_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\bigg)w_{1,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{0,\xi}^{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{1,\xi}^{k}+\frac{O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{3})}{(1+|y|)^{3+2\alpha}}.

We first consider just a rough estimate of the last two terms,

(4.74)

\displaystyle\Delta w_{1,\xi}^{k}+\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}\exp\left({v_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}}\right)w_{1,\xi}^{k}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\tilde{c}_{1,k}w_{0,\xi}^{k}+\frac{O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2})}{(1+|y|)^{3+2\alpha}}.

Let

\underline{w}_{1}^{k}=\tilde{\xi}_{k}(y)-w_{0,\xi}^{k}-w_{1,\xi}^{k}-\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y),

then from (
4.65
), (
4.73
) and (
4.74
) we see that the equation for
\underline{w}_{1}^{k}
takes the form,

(4.75)

\displaystyle\left(\Delta+|y|^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)\exp\left(v_{2}^{k}+\log\frac{\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)}{\tilde{h}_{k}(0)}\right)\right)\underline{w}_{1}^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})}{(1+|y|)^{4+2\alpha}}+\frac{O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2})}{(1+|y|)^{3+2\alpha}}+\frac{O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})}{(1+|y|)^{3+2\alpha}}.

Concerning the r.h.s of (
4.75
), the first term comes from the expansion of
c_{k}
, the second term comes from
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
,
the third term comes from
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
. Here we also note that
w_{0,\xi}^{k}
is constant on
\partial\Omega_{k}
,
while
w_{1,\xi}^{k}
has an oscillation of order
O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
. To eliminate this oscillation we use another harmonic function of order

O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{3}|y|)
. For simplicity and with an abuse of notations we include this function in
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
.
As a consequence standard potential estimates show that,

|\underline{w}_{1}^{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad y\in\Omega_{k}.

Therefore the improved estimate of
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
yields an improved estimate about
\underline{w}_{1}^{k}
which then yields to an improvement of the estimate about
\tilde{\xi}_{k}
. In other words we run a refined bootstrap argument.

Remark 4.9
.

We only need to consider
\alpha>1
, because if
0<\alpha<1
then all these estimates are not needed.
We will be back to this point later on.

With this new estimate about
\underline{w}_{1}^{k}
we can just go through the evaluation of the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away from the blow up
points as in Lemma
4.5
to deduce that,

(4.76)

|\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})|\leq C(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}).

The new estimate (
4.76
) implies that the harmonic function
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
that encodes the oscillation of

\xi_{k}
on
\partial\Omega(p_{1},\tau)
satisfies

(4.77)

|\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|\leq C(\varepsilon_{k}^{3}+\varepsilon_{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})|y|,\quad|y|<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1}.

As a consequence of (
4.77
) we can improve the estimate about
\underline{w}_{1}^{k}
as follows,

(4.78)

|\underline{w}_{1}^{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}^{3}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})(1+|y|)^{\delta}.

In order to further improve the estimate about
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
we point out that, in view of (
4.76
), then around
p_{2}
and
q
we have the following analogous improved estimates.
First of all, around
p_{2}
, we recall that
\hat{w}_{k}
is defined in (
4.27
) and satisfies (
4.28
). Then we have,

\int_{B(p_{2},\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}).

Moreover, around
q_{2}^{k}
, we recall that
\bar{w}_{k}
is defined in (
4.29
) and satisfies (
4.31
). By using (
4.31
) and the expansion of
\bar{w}_{k}
we have

\int_{B(q_{2}^{k},\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}).

At this point we move back to the evaluation of the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
away from blow up points. From the Green’s representation formula of
\xi_{k}
, as in the proof of (
4.32
), by using (
4.77
) for
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
, (
4.78
) for
\underline{w}_{1}^{k}
, (
4.28
) for
\hat{w}_{k}
and (
4.31
) for
\bar{w}_{k}
we can further improve the estimate of
\xi_{k}
as follows,

(4.79)

|\xi_{k}(x)-\xi_{k}(y)|\leq C(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}^{4}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}),

for
x,y\in M\setminus\{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)\cup\Omega(p_{2},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau)\}
.
Then (
4.79
) further improves the estimate of
\underline{w}_{1}^{k}
, so that, by a bootstrap argument which does not involve the leading term proportional to
b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}
(which in fact comes just from the integration over
\Omega(p_{1},\tau)
), after a finite number of iterations, we deduce that,

|\underline{w}_{1}^{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})(1+|y|)^{\delta},

and the estimate about the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
takes the form,

(4.80)

|\xi_{k}(x_{1})-\xi_{k}(x_{2})|\leq C(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}),

for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in M\setminus\{\Omega(p_{1},\tau),\cup\Omega(p_{2},\tau)\cup\Omega(q,\tau)\}
.

As an immediately consequence of (
4.80
) for
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
we have

(4.81)

|\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|\leq C(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{3}+\varepsilon_{k}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})|y|.

At this point we prove a more precise estimate about
b_{0}^{k}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

There exists
C>0
independent of
k
such that

(4.82)

|b_{0}^{k}|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{2}+\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{1+\alpha}}\quad\mbox{if}\quad\alpha>1.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

Recall that
w_{2,\xi}^{k}
was defined (see (
4.43
)) to encode the
\Theta_{2}
and other separable terms of the order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
.
For the estimate of this term we have (
4.44
), but now we include the dependence of
b_{0}^{k}
. So (
4.44
) takes the form,

|w_{2,\xi}^{k}(y)|\leq Cb_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+|y|)^{-2\alpha}.

We need at this point another term in the expansion suitable to handle the radial part of order
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})b_{0}^{k}
, which is why
we set
z_{0,k}
to be the radial function satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}z_{0,k}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}z_{0,k}+r^{2\alpha}\tilde{h}_{k}(0)e^{U_{2}^{k}}z_{0,k}=F_{0,k},\quad 0<r<\tau\varepsilon_{k}^{-1},\\
\\
z_{0,k}(0)=\frac{d}{dr}z_{0,k}(0)=0\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle F_{0,k}(r)=~

\displaystyle-\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bigg(w_{0,\xi}^{k}\big(c_{0,k}+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)|y|^{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|^{2}(g_{k}+r)^{2}\big)+(w_{\xi,1}^{k}\tilde{c}_{1,k})_{r}\bigg)

where we use the fact that the projection of
\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{c}_{1,k})^{2}
along the constant direction is

\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|\nabla\log\tilde{h}_{k}(0)|^{2}(g_{k}+r)^{2}.

Observe carefully that we already considered this function,
see (
4.42
) in subsection
4.2
above, but in that case the underlying assumption was
b_{0}^{k}\to b_{0}\neq 0
. Concerning
z_{0,k}
we have,

|z_{0,k}(r)|\leq C(\delta)b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(1+r)^{\delta},

and then, by defining
\underline{z}_{k}
as follows,

\underline{z}_{k}(y)=\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-w_{0,\xi}^{k}(y)-w_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)-w_{2,\xi}^{k}(y)-z_{0,k}(y),

we have

\Delta\underline{z}_{k}+|y|^{2\alpha}h_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\varepsilon_{k}^{2+2\alpha}\tilde{c}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\underline{z}_{k}=E_{k}

with
z_{k}(0)=0
and

|E_{k}|\leq Cb_{0}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})(1+|y|)^{-2-\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}},

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
Recall that at this moment
\psi_{\xi}^{k}
satisfies (
4.81
). By standard potential estimates we have,

|z_{k}(y)|\leq C(\delta)(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})(1+|y|)^{\delta}.

At this point, by using the estimate about
\underline{z}_{k}
, we deduce that,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{k}}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{v_{2}^{k}}(\xi_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)-\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y))dy

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega_{k}}\tilde{h}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)|y|^{2\alpha}e^{U_{2}^{k}}\psi_{\xi}^{k}(\varepsilon_{k}y)

\displaystyle=d_{1,k}b_{0}^{k}(\Delta\log h_{k}(0)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{\epsilon_{0}}))\varepsilon_{k}^{2}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}),

where the derivation of the above is similar to the derivation of (
4.48
) except that we keep track of
b_{0}^{k}
in this estimate.
Therefore the integral around
p_{1}
reads,

\int_{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}=cb_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}(\Delta(\log\tilde{h}_{k})(0)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{\epsilon_{0}}))+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}),\quad c\neq 0.

From
\int_{M}\rho_{k}Hc_{k}\xi_{k}=0
we readily obtain (
4.82
) by splitting the domain of integration in the three regions near the blow up points
plus the corresponding complement in
M
. Lemma
4.7
is established. ∎

In view of (
4.80
), by (
4.82
) we immediately deduce that Proposition
4.1
holds. ∎

With Proposition
4.1
we give an estimate of the first term in the approximation of
\xi_{k}
around each blowup point. We recall that the first term in the approximation of
\xi_{k}
around
p_{1}
is proportional to
b_{0}^{k}
, around
p_{2}
is proportional to
\hat{b}_{0}^{k}
, around
q_{2}^{k}
is proportional to
\bar{b}_{0}^{k}
. For these quantities we have

Lemma 4.8
.

There exists
C>0
independent by
k
, such that

|\bar{b}_{0}^{k}|+|\hat{b}_{0}^{k}|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{-2}=Ce^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{2}+\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{1+\alpha}}.

Proof of Lemma
4.8
.

In the proof of Proposition
4.1
we already established
(
4.82
) for
b_{0}^{k}
. Also the oscillation of
\xi_{k}
far away from blow up points is of order
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})
(Proposition
4.1
). In the expansion of
\xi_{k}
around
q_{2}^{k}
we have

\xi_{k}(x)=-\bar{b}_{0}^{k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}),\quad x\in\partial B(q_{2}^{k},\tau).

On
\partial B(p_{1}^{k},\tau)
we have

\xi_{k}=-b_{0}^{k}+O(b_{0}^{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2})=-b_{0}^{k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}),

and similarly on
\partial B(p_{2},\tau)
,

\xi_{k}(x)=\hat{b}_{0}^{k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}).

Thus
|b_{0}^{k}-\hat{b}_{0}^{k}|+|b_{0}^{k}-\bar{b}_{0}^{k}|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}.

Lemma
4.8
is established.
∎

Step six: an improved expansion for
\bar{\xi}_{k}
.

As a consequence of Proposition
4.1
we now write the equation of
\bar{\xi}_{k}
(see (
4.60
)) as follows,

\Delta\bar{\xi}_{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}(1+\sigma_{k}\bar{\xi}_{k})\bar{\xi}_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3/2})(1+|y|)^{-5/2}.

By using
\nabla\log\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})=O(\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{1}^{k}})
and the expansion of
\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
in (
4.61
)
we have

\Delta\bar{\xi}_{k}(y)+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(1+\sigma_{k}\bar{\xi}_{k})\bar{\xi}_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3/2})(1+|y|)^{-5/2}.

At this point it is enough to use
\sigma_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})
so that by standard potential estimates, we have,

(4.83)

|\bar{\xi}_{k}(y)-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}|\leq C(\delta)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}(1+|y|)^{\delta}.

There is no need to subtract
\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
here because of Proposition
4.1

|\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}|y|.

In view of (
4.83
) we write the equation of
\bar{\xi}_{k}

as follows,

\Delta\bar{\xi}_{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bar{\xi}_{k}=-\sigma_{k}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})^{2}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3/2})(1+|y|)^{-5/2},

which immediately leads to

(\Delta+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}})(\bar{\xi}_{k}-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})=-\sigma_{k}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})^{2}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3/2})(1+|y|)^{-5/2},

in
\Omega_{k}
.
We now construct the next term in the expansion as follows,

\Delta\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}=-\sigma_{k}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})^{2}_{\theta,2},

where
(\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})^{2}_{\theta,2}
means the projection of
(\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})^{2}
onto
e^{2i\theta}
. Note that, because of the vanishing rate of
\bar{b}_{0}^{k}
, we can safely disregard the projection of
(\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k})^{2}
along
e^{i\theta}
, just because it is proportional to
\bar{b}_{0}^{k}
.
We use a separable function to handle this term. The radial part of
\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}
is represented by
\bar{f}_{1}^{k}
, which satisfies
for
0<r<\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}

\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}\bar{f}_{1}^{k}(r)+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}\bar{f}_{1}^{k}(r)+(\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}-\frac{4}{r^{2}})\bar{f}_{1}^{k}(r)=\bar{E}_{k}

where

\bar{E}_{k}=\sigma_{k}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\frac{r^{2}}{(1+\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}r^{2})^{4}}.

Let
\bar{f}_{1a},\bar{f}_{1b}
be a pair of fundamental solutions which behaves as follows:

|\bar{f}_{1a}(r)|\sim r^{2}
near
0
and
\infty
,

|\bar{f}_{1b}(r)|\sim r^{-2}

near
0
and
\infty
,
we construct
\bar{f}_{1}^{k}
as follows,

\bar{f}_{1}^{k}=\bar{f}_{1a}(r)\int_{r}^{L_{k}}\frac{W_{1}}{W}ds+\bar{f}_{1b}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{W_{2}}{W}ds,\quad L_{k}=\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1},

where one can easily verify that

W(r)\sim-\frac{1}{r},\quad|W_{1}(r)|\leq C\sigma_{k}(1+r)^{-8},\quad|W_{2}(r)|\leq C\sigma_{k}r^{4}(1+r)^{-8}.

Then it is readily seen that a first estimate for
\bar{f}_{1}^{k}(r)
reads,

|\bar{f}_{1}^{k}(r)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}r(1+r)^{-5},\quad 0<r<\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

Here we remark that even though
\frac{d}{dr}\bar{f}_{1}^{k}(0)\neq 0
, it does not interfere with
\nabla\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}(0)
because
\bar{f}_{1}^{k}
is multiplied by
e^{2i\theta}
in
\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}
. Thus

(4.84)

|\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}(y)|=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})(1+|y|)^{-4},

where
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})
comes from the estimate of
\sigma_{k}
. Next we use a radial term to eliminate the radial part of

\sigma_{k}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{w}_{0,\xi})^{2}_{r}
for
r\in(0,\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1})
:

\begin{cases}\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}c_{0,\xi}^{k}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,\xi}^{k}+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}c_{0,\xi}^{k}=\sigma_{k}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}}(\bar{w}_{0,\xi})^{2}_{r},\\
\\
c_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,\xi}^{k}(0)=0.\end{cases}

Then it is not difficult to check that
c_{0,\xi}^{k}
satisfies

(4.85)

|c_{0,\xi}^{k}(r)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\log(1+r),\quad|\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,\xi}^{k}(r)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}(1+r)^{-1}.

Let

z_{0,\xi}^{k}=\bar{\xi}_{k}-\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}-\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}-c_{0,\xi}^{k},

we have

(4.86)

(\Delta+\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})e^{U_{2}^{k}})(z_{0,\xi}^{k}-\psi^{k}_{\xi}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y))=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}})(1+|y|)^{-5/2}

in
\Omega_{k}
for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Note that the oscillation of
z_{0,\xi}^{k}
is
O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})
. If we use an harmonic function to eliminate it, well this harmonic function is so small that it can be safely neglected just by considering it as a part of
\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
. As a consequence in particular
we may assume without loss of generality that
z_{0,\xi}^{k}-\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)
has no oscillation on
\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Thus we deduce from (
4.86
) that,

(4.87)

|z_{0,\xi}^{k}-\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)|\leq C(\delta)\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}}(1+|y|)^{\delta},\quad|y|<\tau\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1},

for some
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Then we apply standard elliptic estimates based on (
4.86
) and (
4.87
) to obtain,

(4.88)

|\nabla(z_{0,\xi}^{k}-\bar{\psi}_{\xi}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\cdot))|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\epsilon_{0}}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial B_{\frac{\tau}{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}}

for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. From (
4.88
) we see that for
r\sim\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}
, the leading term in
\nabla\xi_{k}
is
\bar{w}_{0,\xi}^{k}+c_{0,\xi}^{k}
:

(4.89)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{1}\bar{\xi}_{k}=\frac{b_{1}^{k}(1+a_{k}r^{2})-2a_{k}y_{1}(b_{1}^{k}y_{1}+b_{2}^{k}y_{2})}{(1+a_{k}r^{2})^{2}}+\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,\xi}^{k}(r)\frac{y_{1}}{r}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\delta_{0}}),\\
\\
\partial_{2}\bar{\xi}_{k}=\frac{b_{2}^{k}(1+a_{k}r^{2})-2a_{k}y_{2}(b_{2}^{k}y_{2}+b_{1}^{k}y_{1})}{(1+a_{k}r^{2})^{2}}+\frac{d}{dr}c_{0,\xi}^{k}(r)\frac{y_{2}}{r}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2+\delta_{0}})\end{cases}

where
a_{k}=\frac{\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})}{8}
and
\delta_{0}
is a small positive constant.

Step seven: Evaluation of a simplified Pohozaev identity

In a local coordinates system around
q
,
0=x(q)
, we recall the set up in local coordinates around
q_{2}^{k}
:
u_{i}^{k}={\rm w}_{i}^{k}-f_{k}
where
f_{k}
is defined in (
4.5
) and
u_{k}
is a solution of (
4.9
). The term
e^{\phi}
in (
4.5
) is just
the local conformal factor of the metric (see (
4.3
)). Let us recall that
\rm w_{i}^{k}
satisfies

\Delta_{g}{\rm w}_{i}^{k}+\rho_{k}(He^{\rm w_{i}^{k}}-1)=0,~\ \int_{M}He^{\rm w_{i}^{k}}=1,\quad i=1,2,

where

H=he^{-4\pi\alpha G(x,p_{1})-4\pi\beta G(x,p_{2})}.

Since
{\rm w}_{i}^{k}=u_{i}^{k}+f_{k}
, we have
\xi_{k}=\sigma_{k}^{-1}({\rm w}_{1}^{k}-{\rm w}_{2}^{k})
.
Recall that, for the time being, we are working just with three blowup points, which is why we can choose
f_{k}
as follows,

(4.90)

\displaystyle f_{k}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{k}}{3+\alpha+\beta}\left(R(x,\bar{q}_{2}^{k})-R(\bar{q}_{2}^{k},\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\rho_{k}}{3+\alpha+\beta}\left((1+\alpha)(G(x,p_{1})-G(\bar{q}_{2}^{k},p_{1}))\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\rho_{k}}{3+\alpha+\beta}\left((1+\beta)(G(x,p_{2})-G(\bar{q}_{2}^{k},p_{2}))\right).

Remark 4.10
.

In the general case the corresponding definitions around a regular blow up point
q_{j}
would be,

\displaystyle f_{k,j}(x)=~

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{k}}{m+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\tau}\alpha_{j}}\sum\limits_{\ell\neq j,\ell=\tau+1}^{m}(G(x,\bar{q}_{2,\ell}^{k})-G(\bar{q}_{2,j}^{k},\bar{q}_{2,\ell}^{k}))

\displaystyle+\frac{\rho_{k}}{m+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\tau}\alpha_{j}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\tau}(1+\alpha_{i})(G(x,p_{i})-G(\bar{q}_{2,j}^{k},p_{i}))

\displaystyle+\frac{\rho_{k}}{m+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\tau}\alpha_{j}}\left(R(x,\bar{q}_{2,j}^{k})-R(\bar{q}_{2,j}^{k},\bar{q}_{2,j}^{k})\right)

Because of the refined estimates we have obtained, we can use an easier version of the Pohozaev identity (as opposed to the argument in
[
54
,
10
]
etc). We claim that, with the choice of
f_{k}
as in (
4.90
), the oscillation of

u_{2}^{k}
on
\partial B(q_{2}^{k},\tau/2)
is negligible.
Indeed, from the Green representation of
\rm w_{i}^{k}
we have

\displaystyle{\rm w_{i}^{k}}(x)=~

\displaystyle\overline{\rm w_{i}^{k}}+\int_{\Omega(p_{1},\tau)}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}He^{\rm w_{i}^{k}}+\int_{\Omega(p_{2},\tau)}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}He^{\rm w_{i}^{k}}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega(q,\tau)}G(x,\eta)\rho_{k}He^{\rm w_{i}^{k}}+O(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}).

By using our refined estimates to evaluate each integral we have

{\rm w_{i}^{k}}(x)=\overline{\rm w_{i}^{k}}+8\pi\left((1+\alpha)G(x,p_{1})+(1+\beta)G(x,p_{2})+G(x,\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\right)+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}).

Thus, as far as
x
is far away from blow up points, we have,

\displaystyle u_{i}^{k}(x)=~

\displaystyle\overline{\rm w_{i}^{k}}+8\pi(1+\alpha)G(x,p_{1})+8\pi(1+\beta)G(x,p_{2})

\displaystyle+8\pi G(x,\bar{q}_{2}^{k})-f_{k}+O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2}).

By the expression of
f_{k}
in (
4.90
), we see that if we use
\phi_{2}^{k}
to denote the local harmonic function defined by the oscillation of
u_{2}^{k}
on
\partial B_{\tau/2}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})
, we have

|\phi_{2}^{k}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)|\leq C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|y|.

Here we used
\rho_{*}-\rho_{k}=O(\varepsilon_{k}^{2})
, which follows from (
2.4
).

Then for
j=1,2
, we use the standard Pohozaev identity:

(4.91)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}(-\partial_{\nu}\bar{v}_{1}^{k}\partial_{j}\bar{v}_{1}^{k}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\bar{v}_{1}^{k}|^{2}\gamma_{j})

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}e^{v_{1}^{k}}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)\gamma_{j}dS-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\int_{\Omega_{k}}\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{1}^{k}}.

(4.92)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}(-\partial_{\nu}\bar{v}_{2}^{k}\partial_{j}\bar{v}_{2}^{k}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}|^{2}\gamma_{j})

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)\gamma_{j}dS-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\int_{\Omega_{k}}\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}.

Since by definition,

\bar{v}_{1}^{k}(y)=\bar{v}_{2}^{k}(y)+\sigma_{k}\bar{\xi}_{k}(y),\quad y\in\Omega_{k},

then from (
4.91
) and (
4.92
) we have

(4.93)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}(-\partial_{\nu}\bar{v}_{2}^{k}\partial_{j}\bar{\xi}_{k}-\partial_{\nu}\bar{\xi}_{k}\partial_{j}\bar{v}_{2}^{k}+(\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}\cdot\nabla\bar{\xi}_{k})\gamma_{j})dS

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega_{k}}\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}\bar{\xi}_{k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\delta_{0}}),\quad j=1,2.

Concerning
\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}
we use

\nabla\bar{v}_{2}^{k}(y)=-(4+O(\lambda_{1}^{k}e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}))\left(\frac{y_{1}}{r^{2}},\frac{y_{2}}{r^{2}}\right),\quad|y|\sim\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-1}.

Then (
4.93
) is reduced to

(4.94)

\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\frac{4}{r}\partial_{j}\bar{\xi}_{k}=-\int_{\Omega_{k}}\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}\bar{\xi}_{k}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}}).

To evaluate the left hand side of (
4.94
) we use
(
4.89
) to deduce that,

\frac{1}{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\frac{4}{r}\partial_{j}\bar{\xi}_{k}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}}).

To evaluate the right hand side of (
4.94
), we use,

\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y)=\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})+\sum_{s=1}^{2}\partial_{js}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}y_{s}+O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2})|y|^{2},

e^{\bar{v}_{2}^{k}}=\frac{1}{(1+a_{k}|y|^{2})^{2}}(1+O((\log\bar{\varepsilon}_{k})^{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{2}))

and (
4.87
) for
\bar{\xi}_{k}
,
(
4.85
) for
c_{0,\xi}^{k}
,
(
4.84
) for
\bar{w}_{1,\xi}^{k}
. Then by a straightforward evaluation we have,

\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}\left(\begin{matrix}\partial_{11}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})&\partial_{12}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\\
\partial_{12}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})&\partial_{22}\bar{h}_{k,0}(\bar{q}_{2}^{k})\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}b_{1}^{k}\\
b_{2}^{k}\end{matrix}\right)=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{1+\epsilon_{0}}).

Since the underlying assumption is
(b_{1}^{k},b_{2}^{k})\to(b_{1},b_{2})\neq(0,0)

and since the non-degeneracy condition is
{\rm det}(D^{2}\bar{h}_{k}(0))\neq 0
, then we readily obtain a contradiction. Theorem
1.1
is established under the assumptions that
\alpha>1
and there are three blowup points consisting of one positive singular source, one negative singular source and one regular point.
The case where
\tau=m
is easier and we omit the details to avoid repetitions.
The more general case is worked out by minor changes, essentially just about notations, which proves Theorem
1.1
as far as
\alpha>1
.

On the other side, whenever
0<\alpha<1
, since

\varepsilon_{k}^{2}=e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{k}}{1+\alpha}}=o(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}/2}),

then the proof of
b_{1}=b_{2}=0
around each regular blowup point follows as in
[
10
]
.
Theorem
1.1
is established under the assumption that the set of blowup points
consists of one positive singular source, one negative singular source and one regular point.
The case where
\tau=m
is easier and we omit the details to avoid repetitions. At last, as above,
the more general case is worked out by minor changes, essentially just about notations, which proves Theorem
1.1
.
\hfill\Box

5.
Proof of Other Theorems

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We first consider the case
\alpha_{M}=0
, so that
the set of blowup points consists either of both negative singular sources and regular points, or just of regular points. As far as all blowup points are regular points, we refer the reader to
[
10
]
for the proof. Here we mention the two situations pursued in
[
10
]
:

(1)

L(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
,
{\rm det}(D^{2}f^{*})(p_{\tau+1},...,p_{m}))\neq 0

(2)

L(\mathbf{p})=0
,
{\rm det}(D^{2}f^{*})(p_{\tau+1},...,p_{m}))\neq 0
,
D(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
.

A minor difference with respect to the argument in
[
10
]
is about the proof of
b_{0}=0
.
To simplify the exposition we just use one negative singular pole
p
with a negative index
-1<\beta<0
and a regular blowup point
q
. Then we have,

\rho_{k}=\int_{M}\rho_{k}He^{u_{i}^{k}}=\int_{B(p,\tau)}\rho_{k}He^{u_{i}^{k}}+\int_{B(q,\tau)}\rho_{k}He^{u_{i}^{k}}+\int_{M\setminus(B(p,\tau)\cup B(q,\tau))}\rho_{k}He^{u_{i}^{k}},

and our local expansions imply, as far as either
L(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
or
L(\mathbf{q})=0
but
D(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
, that

|\lambda_{1}^{k}-\lambda_{2}^{k}|=O(e^{-(1-\epsilon_{0})\lambda_{1}^{k}})
for some

\epsilon_{0}>0
. At this point, if
b_{0}\neq 0
, the key observation is that the integral around
p
satisfies,

A_{p}^{k}:=\int_{B(p,\tau)}\rho_{k}c_{k}(x)\xi_{k}=b_{0}c(\tau)e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}}+o(e^{-\lambda_{1}^{k}})

where
c(\tau)\to 0
as
\tau\to 0
, which comes from the local integration around a negative singular source. This means in either one of the cases above (
L(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
or
L(\mathbf{q})=0,D(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
) that
A_{p}^{k}
is not the leading term. As a consequence the whole argument in
[
10
]
can be applied exactly as it stands,
because
A_{p}^{k}
does not play any role. On then other side, as far as there are no regular blowup points, then all the blowup points are negative singular sources and a careful evaluation based on our local estimates shows that the leading term is the one proportional to
D(\mathbf{q})\neq 0
, which comes, see
[
10
]
for details, as a result of a global integral. In other words, local bubbles contributions are minor compared to that proportional to
D(\mathbf{q})
, which is why the proof of
b_{0}=0
follows again by the same argument in
[
10
]
. Theorem
1.2
is established. ∎

Finally, we prove the theorems about Dirichlet problems.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
.

For the blowup solutions to (
1.9
), the corresponding estimates as in section
2
have been also obtained in
[
33
,
84
]
for
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\mathbb{N}
and in
[
30
,
83
,
44
]
for
\alpha_{j}=0
. Those preliminary estimates have almost the same form except for
\phi_{j}=0
and
K\equiv 0
, where
\phi_{j}
are the conformal factor at
p_{j}
and
K
is the Gaussian curvature of
M
.

Then, under the assumption of regularity about
\partial\Omega
and
q_{j}\in\Omega

(1\leq j\leq N)
, it has been shown in
[
59
]
via the moving planes method that the blowup points of (
1.9
) are far away from
\partial\Omega
. On the other hand, the crucial estimates obtained in section
4.2
and
4.3
are in fact just local estimates. Consequently, a careful inspection of the proof shows that the new facts pushed forward here are not at all affected by the presence of the boundary of
\Omega
throughout the argument. As a consequence Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
can be proved by the same argument.
∎

6.
Appendix

In this appendix we prove a useful lemma. The notations in this section overlap with those in other sections, so the readers should treat these notations as being independent of those in other sections.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
u_{k}
satisfy

\Delta u_{k}+h_{k}e^{u_{k}}=0,\quad\mbox{in}\quad B(0,\tau)

where
h_{k}
is
C^{5}
, bounded above and below and there is a uniform bound on
\int_{B_{\tau}}h_{k}e^{u_{k}}
. Suppose
0
is the only blowup point of
u_{k}
and
u_{k}
is constant on
\partial B_{\tau}
. Then we assume that
q_{k}=O(\epsilon_{k}^{2})
is the local maximum of
u_{k}
, and

\epsilon_{k}=e^{-\frac{u_{k}(q_{k})}{2}}
. We also require
\nabla h_{k}(q_{k})=O(\epsilon_{k}^{2}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon_{k}})
. Let
B(q_{k},\tilde{\tau}_{k})
be inside
B(0,\tau_{k})
and these two balls are tangent. Then
\tau-\tilde{\tau}_{k}=|q_{k}|
. Then we claim that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}u_{k}(q_{k}+\tilde{\tau}_{k}e^{i\theta})\cos\theta d\theta=-\frac{2q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}u_{k}(q_{k}+\tilde{\tau}_{k}e^{i\theta})\sin\theta d\theta=-\frac{2q_{2}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3}),

where
q_{k}=(q_{1}^{k},q_{2}^{k})
.

Remark 6.1
.

If we use
\phi_{E}^{k}
to remove the oscillation of
u_{k}
on
\partial B(q_{k},\tilde{\tau}_{k})
with
\phi_{E}^{k}(q_{k})=0
, we have

\phi_{E}^{k}(q_{k}+\epsilon_{k}y)=-\frac{2q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}\epsilon_{k}y_{1}-\frac{2q_{2}^{k}}{\tau}\epsilon_{k}y_{2}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3})|y|+\phi_{E,2}(\epsilon_{k}y)

where
\phi_{E,2}^{k}
is orthogonal to
e^{i\theta}
. By adding the estimates on the projections of
e^{ik\theta}
(
k\neq 1
) we have

|\phi_{E,2}^{k}(\epsilon_{k}y)|\leq C\epsilon_{k}^{4}|y|^{2}.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

First we remark that the oscillation of
u_{k}
on
\partial B(q_{k},\tilde{\tau}_{k})
is
O(\epsilon_{k}^{2})
, because for any two points
x_{1},x_{2}
on
\partial B(q_{k},\tilde{\tau}_{k})
, there exists
x_{1}^{k},x_{2}^{k}
on
\partial B(0,\tau)
such that
|x_{1}-x_{1}^{k}|+|x_{2}-x_{2}^{k}|\leq C\epsilon_{K}^{2}
. Since
|\nabla u_{k}|\sim 1
near
\partial B(0,\tau)
and
u_{k}(x_{1}^{k})=u_{k}(x_{2}^{k})
, we see that

|u_{k}(x_{1})-u_{k}(x_{2})|=|u_{k}(x_{1})-u_{k}(x_{1}^{k})-(u_{k}(x_{2})-u_{k}(x_{2}^{k})|\leq C\epsilon_{K}^{2}.

Let

v_{k}(y)=u_{k}(\epsilon_{k}y)+2\log\epsilon_{k},\quad y\in\Omega_{k}:=B(0,\tau\epsilon_{k}^{-1}),

and
\Gamma_{k}
be the image of
\partial B(q_{k},\tilde{\tau}_{k})
after scaling, we first observe that the oscillation of
v_{k}
on
\partial\Omega_{1,k}
is
O(\epsilon_{k}^{2})
. In order to identify the leading term in the projection to
e^{i\theta}
, we use
G_{k}
to denote the Green’s function on
\Omega_{k}
:

(6.1)

G_{k}(y,\eta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|y-\eta|+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|y|}{L_{k}}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|y^{*}-\eta|

where
L_{k}=\tau\epsilon_{k}^{-1}
,
y^{*}=\frac{L_{k}^{2}y}{|y|^{2}}
. Now the image of
q_{k}
becomes
q_{k}/\epsilon_{k}
.
For
y\in\Gamma_{k}
the Green representation formula gives

(6.2)

v_{k}(y)=\int_{\Omega_{k}}G_{k}(y,\eta)\tilde{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}d\eta-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\partial_{\nu}G_{k}v_{k}dS,

Our goal is to prove

(6.3)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}v_{k}(\frac{q_{k}}{\epsilon_{k}}+re^{i\theta})\cos\theta d\theta=2\frac{q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3}),\quad r=\tilde{\tau}\epsilon_{k}^{-1},

and the corresponding equation with
\sin\theta
.

If we use (
6.2
) as the expression for
v_{k}
to evaluate (
6.3
), we observe that the last term in (
6.2
) is minor, because it is a constant.
Next we deal with the term
\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|y|}{L_{k}}
, which corresponds to the following integral:

(6.4)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{|y|^{2}}{L_{k}^{2}}\cos\theta d\theta\int_{\Omega_{k}}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}

Here
y=q_{k}/\epsilon_{k}+\tilde{\tau}\epsilon_{k}^{-1}e^{i\theta}
.
Direct computation gives

\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{|y|^{2}}{L_{k}^{2}}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{|q_{k}|^{2}+\tilde{\tau}^{2}+2q_{1}^{k}\tilde{\tau}\cos\theta+2q_{2}^{k}\tilde{\tau}\sin\theta}{\tau^{2}}.

Using this expression in the evaluation of (
6.4
) we have

(6.5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{|y|^{2}}{L_{k}^{2}}\cos\theta d\theta\int_{\Omega_{k}}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{q_{1}^{k}\tilde{\tau}}{\tau^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{k}}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\frac{q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3}).

Next we evaluate

(6.6)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bigg(\int_{\Omega_{k}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|y^{*}-\eta|}{|y-\eta|}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}d\eta\bigg)\cos\theta d\theta.

Now we evaluate the integral by cutting
\Omega_{k}
in several subsets. First, for each fixed
y\in\Gamma_{k}
and
\eta\in B(y,\epsilon_{k}^{-1}/2)
, we use

\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|y^{*}-\eta|}{|y-\eta|}\leq C\epsilon_{k}/|y^{**}-\eta|

where we used
|y-y^{*}|\leq C\epsilon_{k}
and
y^{**}
is between
y
and
y^{*}
. Over this region
e^{v_{k}}\sim\epsilon_{k}^{4}
. So the integral of this part is
O(\epsilon_{k}^{4})
. Next we consider for fixed
y\in\Gamma_{k}
, the integration over
\Omega_{k}\setminus(B(y,\epsilon_{k}^{-1}/2)\cup B(0,\epsilon_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}})
. When
\eta
is in this region,
|y^{**}-\eta|\sim\epsilon_{k}^{-1}
, then one sees that the integral over this region is
O(\epsilon_{k}^{3})
. Finally we consider the integral over
B(0,\epsilon_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}})
when
y\in\Gamma_{k}
is fixed.
For this case we use

(6.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\frac{|y^{*}-\eta|^{2}}{|y-\eta|^{2}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(1+\frac{|y^{*}|^{2}-|y|^{2}+2(y^{*}-y)\cdot\eta}{|y-\eta|^{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(1+\frac{|y^{*}|^{2}-|y|^{2}}{|y|^{2}}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3})|\eta|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\big(1+(\frac{|L_{k}|^{4}}{|y|^{4}}-1)+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3})|\eta|\big)

where in the second step we used

(6.8)

\frac{1}{|y-\eta|^{2}}-\frac{1}{|y|^{2}}=O(\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{3})|\eta|

for
|\eta|<C\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Using (
6.8
) and

|y|^{2}=\epsilon_{k}^{-1}\bigg(\tilde{\tau}^{2}+2q_{1}^{k}\tau\cos\theta+2q_{2}^{k}\tau\sin\theta+O(\epsilon_{k}^{4})\bigg),

we have

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bigg(\int_{B(0,\epsilon_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}})}\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|y^{*}-\eta|}{|y-\eta|}\tilde{h}_{k}e^{v_{k}}d\eta)\cos\theta d\theta=-4\frac{q_{1}^{k}}{\tau}+O(\epsilon_{k}^{3}).

The proof about the last estimate in the claim is similar and we skip it here to avoid repetitions.

Lemma
6.1
is established.
∎
